**Summary.** This research uses numerical simulations to study how coupling a degenerate Fermi gas to an optical cavity influences the simultaneous development of density and pairing orders. The authors demonstrate that the cavity field fundamentally alters the system's ground state, leading to distinct intertwined phases depending on the nature of the atomic interactions. This work highlights the potential of cavity QED to realize and control complex, multi-ordered quantum states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it explores how cavity-mediated light-matter interactions can engineer and stabilize exotic, multi-component ordered states (like intertwined CDW/PDW) in ultracold atomic systems, bridging quantum optics and many-body physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the interplay and potential intertwining of density ordering and pairing order in a degenerate Fermi gas when coupled to an optical cavity.

**Main result.** The cavity-induced interactions can support intertwined fermionic orders, leading to distinct phase transitions depending on whether the bare interaction is repulsive or attractive.

**Method.** The study employs extensive numerical simulations using the Hartree-Fock-Bogoliubov (HFB) formalism to solve the mean-field Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The system is a two-component degenerate Fermi gas trapped in a planar optical cavity, driven by a transverse pump laser, allowing for simultaneous control over density and pairing via cavity coupling.

**Key observables.** Charge-Density-Wave (CDW) instability, Pair-Density-Wave (PDW) instability, superfluid coherence, and the cavity field amplitude $|\alpha|$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bare atom-atom interaction ($g_0$), cavity-induced single-atom interaction ($g_a$), and cavity-induced pair interaction ($g_\Delta$), as well as the pumping strength ($eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses a mean-field description and operates in the far off-resonant dispersive regime, allowing for adiabatic elimination of excited states.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup scheme, phase diagrams showing transitions between Normal, SF, CDW, and PDW phases based on interaction strength and pumping, and plots detailing the evolution of density and pairing harmonics.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical setup and Hamiltonian to analyzing the system in different interaction regimes (repulsive vs. attractive) and finally detailing the joint instability leading to intertwined CDW+PDW states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent quantum gas cavity-QED experiments demonstrated simultaneous coupling of photons to single atom transitions as well as correlated pairs of ultracold fermions trapped inside optical cavities. This enables simultaneous control over density ordering and pairing. Using extensive numerical simulations, we show that in a transversely driven, two-component degenerate Fermi gas, the interplay of cavity-induced and bare atom--atom interactions controls not only the power threshold for self-organization, but also the type of spatial ordering in the $\mathbb Z_2$-symmetry-broken superradiant state. In the repulsive interaction regime, unpaired or weakly paired fermions first self-organize through a charge-density-wave instability, and finite-momentum pairing only occurs at much stronger pump strengths. In contrast, an attractive superfluid undergoes a joint density--pairing instability, directly entering into intertwined phase with charge-density-wave and pair-density-wave orders. At strong pumping the photon-enhanced pair interaction generates localized density and pairing order even when the bare contact interaction is repulsive. In this cavity-dominated regime strong spatial localization suppresses the long range superfluid coherence. Our results identify the role of cavity-induced atomic interactions in supporting intertwined fermionic orders and pave the way for exploring exotic states with multiple orders in highly controlled hybrid light-matter systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This review introduces and compares four Matrix Product State (MPS) based algorithms for numerically solving the Lindblad equation in 1D quantum systems. It benchmarks these methods—including quantum trajectory and quantum state diffusion—against an exactly solvable free-fermion model. The paper provides valuable insights into the trade-offs between computational efficiency, accuracy, and the inherent limitations of MPS truncation when simulating open quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant for AMO physicists studying open quantum systems, as it provides a detailed, comparative guide on using tensor network methods (MPS) to simulate dissipation and environmental coupling, which is crucial for understanding realistic quantum devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop and compare various numerical algorithms for accurately integrating the Lindblad equation, which governs the time evolution of open quantum systems.

**Main result.** The comparison shows that the quantum-state diffusion algorithm can outperform the quantum trajectory method at small times, although at large times, time discretization and MPS truncation errors dominate.

**Method.** The core methodology involves representing the quantum state using Matrix Product States (MPS) and applying techniques like Time-Evolving Block Decimation (TEBD) to simulate the dynamics dictated by the Lindblad master equation.

**Model / system.** The study uses a one-dimensional quantum lattice system, specifically a free-fermion model with particle sources and sinks, whose dynamics are described by the Lindblad equation.

**Key observables.** Average density $\langle n_i(t) angle$, average current $\langle j_i angle$, and entanglement entropy ($S_E$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond dimension ($\chi$), time step ($\Delta t$), and the number of stochastic histories ($n_{	ext{conf}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dynamics are assumed to be Markovian, the interaction is weak (Born approximation), and the MPS representation assumes low entanglement.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the average density and current profiles over time, comparing numerical results against exact solutions, and showing the mean square deviation of observables.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem by defining the Lindblad equation for open systems, reviews the MPS formalism and TEBD for unitary evolution, and then compares four specific algorithms (two direct integration, two stochastic unravelings) using the free fermion model as a benchmark.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this introductory review, we present and compare four algorithms for the numerical integration of the Lindblad equation for one-dimensional quantum lattice systems. All four methods are based on Matrix Product State representations and can be viewed as extensions of the Time-Evolving Block Decimation (TEBD) algorithm to open quantum systems. Two approaches directly integrate the vectorized Lindblad equation, one of them explicitly enforcing the positivity of the density matrix. The other two rely on stochastic unravelings of the Lindblad equation, namely the quantum trajectory and quantum state diffusion approaches. We discuss the principles, numerical implementation, accuracy, and computational efficiency of the different methods, and benchmark them against an exactly solvable free fermion model.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously derives Heisenberg's Error-Disturbance Relation for quantum measurements, focusing on the error and disturbance associated with individual measurement outcomes. By using unitary transformation equations, the authors prove the EDR holds for specific readouts, providing a refined theoretical basis for understanding the fundamental trade-off inherent in quantum measurement processes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work refines the fundamental understanding of measurement back-action by providing a rigorous, outcome-conditional derivation of the EDR, which is crucial for theoretical work in quantum measurement theory and open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper provides a rigorous derivation of Heisenberg's Error-Disturbance Relation (EDR) specifically for individual quantum measurements, addressing conceptual ambiguities in prior formulations.

**Main result.** The EDR, $\epsilon_X(\hat{\sigma}_t) \eta_X(\hat{y}_0) \geq rac{1}{2} |\langle\Psi_t|[\hat{\sigma}_0, \hat{y}_0]|\Psi_tangle_X|$, is proven to hold strictly at the level of individual measurement outcomes, and this relation also holds when errors are averaged over the unconditioned state.

**Method.** The derivation utilizes the unitary transformation equations of observables ($\hat{U}$) to analyze the measurement process, calculating conditional variances ($\epsilon_X$) and disturbances ($\eta_X$) across various spectral regimes (discrete/continuous).

**Model / system.** The model describes a quantum measurement interaction where an object observable ($\hat{\sigma}_0$) interacts with a probe observable ($\hat{y}_0$) over time $t$ via a unitary operator $\hat{U}$.

**Key observables.** Measurement error $\epsilon_X(\hat{\sigma}_t)$ and disturbance $\eta_X(\hat{y}_0)$ associated with specific measurement outcomes $X$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The time evolution parameter $t$, and the structure of the observables ($\hat{\sigma}_0, \hat{y}_0$) relative to the unitary evolution $\hat{U}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation assumes that the mapping from initial state/readout to the measured observable is strictly single-valued for the EDR to hold.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon preliminary work by first establishing the EDR for individual outcomes using unitary transformations, then verifying its consistency across different spectral types (discrete/continuous), and finally confirming the result in the standard von Neumann interaction model.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Heisenberg originally envisioned the error-disturbance relation under the premise that the state after measurement must remain consistent with the Kennard-Robertson uncertainty relation. This implies that the measurement error must be formulated via a posterior observable $\hat{x}_t$, rather than a prior observable $\hat{x}_0$, because the posterior measurement error determines the post-measurement uncertainty of the electron. Building upon the preliminary conceptual foundation reported in arXiv:1504.03779, this paper presents a rigorous derivation of the error-disturbance principle formulated from the unitary transformation equations of observables. Each readout $X$ of a posterior probe observable $\hat{X}_t$ completes a single measurement event, producing a specific conditional object state. The Kennard-Robertson uncertainty relation must hold for these states. Specifically, we show that the uncertainty relation between the error $ε_{X}(\hat{x}_t)$ and the disturbance $η_{X}(\hat{p}_0)$ holds strictly at the level of individual measurements, characterized by the specific readout $X$. We also verify that conventional error-disturbance relations, where the errors are evaluated by averaging over the unconditioned state, hold true. Our results provide a refined theoretical basis for understanding the fundamental trade-off in individual measurement outcomes.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an exact theoretical method using continued fractions to solve the dynamics of few-atom lasers coupled to a cavity. By constructing a block-tridiagonal Liouvillian, the authors derive exact expressions for collective correlations and benchmark existing approximations. The results are crucial for understanding the true quantum nature of light emission in these systems, particularly highlighting where simple approximations break down.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, exact, and systematically improvable tool for analyzing open quantum systems in cavity QED, offering quantitative benchmarks against common, but often inaccurate, mean-field or cumulant approximations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop an exact theoretical framework for few-atom lasers, surpassing the limitations of standard approximations like second-order cumulant closure.

**Main result.** The authors derive exact identities for collective correlations and show that the standard second-order cumulant closure fails qualitatively for inter-emitter coherence above inversion.

**Method.** The method combines the Briegel--Englert damping basis for the cavity mode with the permutation-invariant Liouville basis for the atoms, leading to a block-tridiagonal Liouvillian solved via a matrix continued fraction.

**Model / system.** The system models $N$ identical, incoherently pumped two-level emitters coupled symmetrically to one lossy cavity mode (Tavis–Cummings model). The dynamics are governed by a master equation incorporating cavity decay and atomic pumping/decay rates.

**Key observables.** Photon number ($\langle \hat{n} angle$), pair coherence, emission spectrum $S(\omega')$, and various connected correlation functions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of emitters ($N$), cavity decay rate ($A$), and pumping/decay rates ($B, s, C$). The analysis covers the few-quanta regime and the good-cavity limit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that the combined Liouvillian structure allows for a block-Jacobi reduction, leading to a uniquely solvable linear matrix equation for the stationary state.

**Figures summary.** Figures show time evolution plots comparing exact results to approximations for photon number and $g^{(2)}(0)$, and illustrate the spectral pole width for small $N$.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the exact block-Jacobi operator structure, solves the stationary state using a matrix continued fraction leading to a solvable Riccati equation, and then applies this machinery to derive exact correlation identities and benchmark existing approximations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We combine the Briegel--Englert damping basis of a lossy cavity mode with the permutation-invariant Liouville basis of \(N\) identical, incoherently pumped two-level emitters. The resulting Liouvillian is exactly block tridiagonal in a single radial field index, with atomic blocks of dimension \(\binom{N+3}{3}\). The stationary state follows from a matrix continued fraction whose asymptotic closure is a linear matrix equation that we prove to be uniquely solvable throughout the dissipative regime \(A>0\), and the same construction in the coherence-one sector yields the emission spectrum. The method is validated against direct diagonalization, weak-pump perturbation theory, and closed-system spectra, reaches \(N=16\) in tens of seconds in the few-quanta regime, and has a quantified precision limit at large photon number. We use it to derive an exact two-atom photon-balance identity that isolates the cavity-induced pair coherence and relates it to a singlet--triplet population difference; to establish an exact pump-order hierarchy of connected correlations for a zero-temperature cavity and a parity property of the minimal collective-spin sector; and to benchmark the second-order cumulant closure used in few-emitter laser theory, which we find to be semi-quantitative for the photon number but to predict the wrong sign of the inter-emitter coherence above inversion. In a good-cavity regime the exact solution shows near-Poissonian statistics and strong line narrowing for two and three emitters.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an experimental demonstration of generating highly indistinguishable photon pairs using integrated silicon nitride microresonators pumped by a continuous-wave laser. By developing and implementing a double-window coincidence detection scheme, the authors overcome theoretical limitations that previously predicted poor performance. This establishes CW-driven high-Q resonators as a robust and scalable platform for quantum network applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial experimental blueprint for realizing quantum network primitives using integrated photonics, directly addressing the scalability and performance limitations inherent in CW-driven sources, which is highly relevant to quantum optics and quantum information.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is generating scalable, high-indistinguishability photon sources required for quantum networks, as conventional theory predicts poor Hong-Ou-Mandel (HOM) interference visibility from independent continuous-wave (CW)-driven microresonators.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated that by employing a detector-conditioned heralded state approach, they achieved high HOM visibilities (e.g., 0.992) and near-unity indistinguishability and heralding efficiency without spectral filtering.

**Method.** The work moves beyond source-only descriptions by analyzing the system using finite-time detection logic, specifically implementing a double-window coincidence detection scheme.

**Model / system.** The experiment utilizes integrated high-Q silicon nitride microresonators pumped by a CW laser, generating photon pairs via cavity-enhanced spontaneous four-wave mixing. The setup involves complex on-chip routing, interferometry, and superconducting nanowire single-photon detectors (SNSPDs).

**Key observables.** HOM visibility (V), detector-conditioned purity (P), and intrinsic heralding efficiency ($\eta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** High-Q silicon nitride platform, CW pumping, double-window coincidence logic, and specific count rates (e.g., 4.5 Hz).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a flat spectral acceptance window bandwidth B >> $\kappa$, and the key technical advancement relies on decoupling the purity and efficiency measurements via double-window logic.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the failure of the JSA model for CW sources, show how P and $\eta$ approach unity by optimizing detection windows ($T$ and $T'$), and detail the comparison between single-window and double-window coincidence logic.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the theoretical limitation of source-only descriptions. It then introduces the detector-conditioned state formalism. The experimental section details the $	ext{Si}_3	ext{N}_4$ chip fabrication and setup. The core argument is built by comparing the performance under single-window versus the superior performance achieved using double-window coincidence logic.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum networks require scalable photon sources combining narrow linewidth, high efficiency, and high indistinguishability. Microresonator photon-pair sources are promising candidates, yet a source-only description based on the joint spectral amplitude (JSA) predicts near-zero Hong-Ou-Mandel (HOM) interference visibility between photons generated by independent, CW-driven microresonators. In this work, we show that the observed HOM interference is not determined by the JSA alone. By combining finite-time detection with cavity-enhanced spontaneous four-wave mixing, we characterize a detector-conditioned heralded state governed by the idler-photon detection window. We further demonstrate that independently optimizing the idler and signal detection windows allows both heralded-photon indistinguishability and intrinsic heralding efficiency to approach unity, without spectral filtering or complex source engineering. Utilizing integrated high-$Q$ silicon nitride microresonators, we achieve HOM visibilities of 0.992(8) and 0.942(12), without background subtraction, at fourfold count rates of 4.5(3) and 12.2(6) Hz, respectively. Our work establishes CW-driven high-$Q$ microresonators as a robust and scalable platform for quantum-network primitives.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores photon blockade in quantum systems using giant atoms coupled to multiple optical cavities. It demonstrates that the extended nature of these atoms enables the simultaneous occurrence of unconventional and conventional photon blockades through destructive interference pathways. The theoretical analysis uses master equations and $g^{(2)}(0)$ measurements to map out the required physical parameters for this advanced quantum control.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses advanced topics in cavity QED and open quantum systems by extending blockade phenomena from simple point atoms to complex, spatially extended giant atoms, opening new avenues for quantum light sources.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the realization and control of simultaneous unconventional and conventional photon blockades in quantum systems mediated by giant atoms.

**Main result.** The spatially extended nature of giant atoms allows for simultaneous photon blockades (both conventional and unconventional) in multiple coupled cavities via multipath destructive interference, a feat impossible in simple point atom systems.

**Method.** The analysis uses the Lindblad master equation to model system dynamics and calculates the second-order correlation function, g^(2)(0), to diagnose photon statistics.

**Model / system.** The system consists of a giant atom coupled to two or three optical cavities. The Hamiltonian and dynamics are described using cavity and atomic coupling strengths, detunings, and driving fields.

**Key observables.** Second-order correlation function g^(2)(0) (where < 1 indicates blockade), and the detection of simultaneous CPBs and UPBs.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cavity frequencies ($\omega_j$), atom transition frequency ($\omega_0$), coupling strengths ($J, V$), inter-cavity coupling ($\lambda$), and detuning ($\Delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Dissipation rates are often assumed equal ($\kappa_1 = \kappa_2 = \kappa_3 = \gamma$), and specific resonance conditions are used for detailed blockade analysis.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic coupling structures, level diagrams showing CPB mechanisms, and plots of log10 g^(2)(0) vs. detuning/coupling strength demonstrating the optimal conditions for simultaneous blockades.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the general Hamiltonian and master equation for $N=2$ and $N=3$ cavities, to deriving the diagnostic tool $g^{(2)}(0)$, and finally demonstrating the physical mechanisms and optimal parameters for achieving simultaneous CPBs and UPBs.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Photon blockade is a phenomenon where the presence of system nonlinearity causes the output to consist of single photons, which has been extensively studied in point atom systems, but it is barely explored in giant atom ones. In this paper, we propose giant atom-mediated photon blockade scheme based on two cavities and three cavities systems with driving field applied to the first cavity. We show that simultaneous unconventional photon blockades (UPBs) can not occur in the point atom system (the atom coupling only to the leftmost cavity) due to there always existing a cavity to have a single path. In contrast, the spatially extended nature of giant atom enables coupling to multiple cavities and allows for the introduction of a phase and coupling strength. Consequently, simultaneous UPBs in multiple cavities can be obtained due to the multipath destructive interference. Moreover, by manipulating the detuning, we observe simultaneous conventional photon blockades (CPBs) in multiple cavities. Finally, we study simultaneous two-photon blockades (2PBs) in point atom multiple cavities system.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes using a giant atom coupled to two waveguides as a programmable Hong-Ou-Mandel (HOM) interferometer. By tuning the coupling phases, the system can continuously switch the two-photon output statistics between bunching and antibunching. This platform offers potential applications in quantum information processing and quantum sensing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges cavity QED concepts (giant atoms, structured baths) with fundamental quantum optics phenomena (HOM effect) and advanced applications like quantum sensing, making it highly relevant for AMO physicists working on open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To demonstrate that a giant atom coupled to two waveguides can function as a programmable Hong-Ou-Mandel (HOM) interferometer, allowing continuous control over single-photon beam splitting and two-photon interference.

**Main result.** The system exhibits a pronounced HOM dip under specific conditions, and by tuning the coupling phases, the two-photon output statistics can be continuously controlled between bunching and antibunching.

**Method.** The analysis involves simulating single-photon and two-photon scattering using the system Hamiltonian, calculating generalized coincidence probabilities, and applying quantum parameter estimation theory.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a giant atom (GA) coupled to two waveguides structured as coupled-cavity arrays (CCAs). The system is modeled using a Hamiltonian involving GA energy, nearest-neighbor hopping in the CCAs, and tunable coupling terms with phases.

**Key observables.** Bunching and antibunching probabilities, the HOM dip in the coincidence probability, single-photon transmission/reflection amplitudes, and the classical Fisher information.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Tunable coupling phases (theta_a, theta_b), GA-cavity detuning (Delta e), and the relative input delay between two-photon wave packets.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the ability to characterize two-photon interference using correlation functions derived from quadrature measurements, despite the difficulty of photon-number-resolving detection in the underlying superconducting circuit platform.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows the GA coupled to two CCAs, illustrating phase control. Figure 2 shows single-photon transmission probabilities as a function of coupling phase and detuning, confirming the 50:50 beam-splitter condition.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the concept of the programmable HOM interferometer using the GA. It then analyzes single-photon scattering to derive the conditions for balanced beam splitting. Finally, it extends the analysis to two-photon scattering to demonstrate the programmable control over interference statistics and its application to quantum sensing.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Hong--Ou--Mandel (HOM) effect is a hallmark of two-photon quantum interference, in which two indistinguishable photons impinging on a balanced beam splitter bunch into the same output port. Here, we show that a giant atom (GA), coupled to two waveguides through two coupling points each, can function as a programmable HOM interferometer, enabling continuous control over single-photon beam splitting and two-photon interference. This programmability arises from coupling-phase differences in the GA, which tune its self-interference and directionality, and thereby its scattering response. To characterize the two-photon interference, we analyze the scattering of two Gaussian single-photon wave packets injected through different waveguides and evaluate the bunching and antibunching (coincidence) probabilities of the resulting four output ports. At the operating point where the GA acts as an effective 50:50 beam splitter for single photons, we observe a pronounced HOM dip as the relative input delay between the two wave packets is varied. Away from this point, adjusting the coupling phases continuously tunes the two-photon output statistics between bunching into the same output port and antibunching across distinct output ports. In addition, we explore an application to quantum parameter estimation, showing that small deviations of coupling phases can be estimated from the two-photon output statistics, with the achievable sensitivity quantified by the classical Fisher information associated with a binary coincidence measurement. Our giant-atom beam splitter thus provides a programmable platform for two-photon interference in waveguide quantum electrodynamics, with potential applications in quantum information processing, quantum communication, and quantum sensing.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes quantum synchronization in coupled systems like lasers by examining the Liouvillian spectrum. It demonstrates that the transition between desynchronized and synchronized states is governed by exceptional points within the slow modes of the Liouvillian. The findings provide a quantum signature for these transitions observable even in the few-particle regime.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses open quantum systems dynamics, using the Liouvillian formalism to map classical nonlinear phenomena (like synchronization) onto quantum spectral singularities (Exceptional Points), which is highly relevant for quantum optics and AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the quantum analog of synchronization transitions occurring in coupled self-sustained oscillators, focusing on how these transitions are controlled by singularities in the quantum Liouvillian.

**Main result.** The synchronization transition is shown to be controlled by cascades of exceptional points in the slow modes of the Liouvillian spectrum, which are protected by a generalized PT symmetry.

**Method.** The analysis involves calculating the spectrum and eigenmatrices of the Liouvillian superoperator, comparing results to semiclassical approximations, and analyzing the spectral flow between different steady-state regimes.

**Model / system.** The system models coupled lasers or polariton condensates, whose dynamics are governed by a Lindblad form Liouvillian superoperator. The analysis utilizes concepts like limit cycles and the $U(1)$ symmetry inherent in the system.

**Key observables.** The Liouvillian spectrum (eigenvalues $\lambda$), the slow modes of the spectrum, and correlation functions like $g^{(1)}(	au)$ for single and coupled lasers.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strength, detuning ($\delta\omega$), and the coherence order ($n$) of the correlations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Liouvillian formalism and involves approximations for the inverse saturation operator in some derivations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Liouvillian spectrum, showing parabolic branches approaching zero decay rates near the limit cycle, and display the Husimi function for various eigenmatrices.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the Liouvillian framework for coupled oscillators, analyzing the spectral properties related to the classical limit cycle, and then detailing how the synchronization transition manifests as exceptional point cascades in the quantum spectrum.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The coupling between self-sustained oscillators, such as lasers or polariton condensates, leads to different steady-states in different regimes. Weak coupling allows the oscillators to behave independently, with different frequencies and uncorrelated phases, while stronger coupling can produce synchronization. In classical or semiclassical theories transitions between these steady-states correspond to changes in the topology of the phase space attractors and occur at generalized exceptional points. We investigate the corresponding changes in a quantum theory of coupled lasers or condensates. We show how the different steady-state regimes of the classical limit give rise to distinct forms for the spectra and eigenmatrices of the slow modes of the Liouvillian. By following the spectral flow between the different regimes we show that the synchronization transition is controlled by cascades of exceptional points in these slow modes, protected by a generalized PT symmetry. Our results show how singularities of the quantum Liouvillian control the different dynamical regimes, and give rise to experimental signatures of the synchronization transition which remain well-defined in the few-particle regime dominated by quantum effects.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents Qurrium, a Python package built on Qiskit, designed to estimate quantum state properties efficiently using randomized measurement protocols like the classical shadow protocol. It offers two workflows—one for simulation and one for analyzing raw hardware data—allowing researchers to characterize complex states like cluster states and measure observables on real quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** For AMO physicists studying open quantum systems, this provides a crucial, hardware-agnostic tool to characterize complex quantum states (like those generated by cavity QED or trapped ions) using measurement data, bypassing the need for full tomography.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the essential need to estimate quantum state properties (like purity, entropy, and overlaps) without performing full quantum state tomography.

**Main result.** It introduces Qurrium, a Python package built on Qiskit, that implements randomized measurement protocols, specifically the classical shadow protocol, for efficient state characterization.

**Method.** The methodology centers on using randomized measurements (classical shadow protocol) to estimate expectation values and state properties, demonstrated through both end-to-end simulation workflows and standalone analysis of hardware-collected data.

**Model / system.** The package is tested on theoretical models like the cluster state and Ising time-evolved states, and validated using measurements from a superconducting quantum processor.

**Key observables.** Purity ($	ext{Tr}(ho^2)$), second-order Rényi entropy ($S^{(2)}(ho)$), expectation values of Pauli observables ($\langle O angle$), and state overlaps ($	ext{Tr}(hoho')$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis depends on the structure of the quantum state (e.g., cluster state stabilizers) and the separation distance ($d$) between subsystems.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The standalone workflow assumes measurement outcomes and random unitaries are recorded in the specific Qurrium data format; the efficiency gains are implementation-level and do not change statistical complexity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the end-to-end and standalone workflows. Specific results show the overlap between the cluster state and reference states, and the decay of mutual information $I^{(2)}(A:B)$ with separation distance $d$.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews the classical shadow protocol, details the two distinct workflows within Qurrium (end-to-end and standalone), and then demonstrates usage with concrete examples like the cluster state and Ising dynamics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Estimating quantum state properties is essential across a wide range of applications, from studying quantum many-body physics to benchmarking quantum hardware. We present Qurrium, a Python package built on Qiskit that implements randomized measurement protocols for estimating purity, second-order Rényi entropy, expectation values of Pauli observables, and state overlap. In this paper, we focus on the classical shadow protocol and demonstrate two workflows in Qurrium. One is the end-to-end workflow that integrates quantum circuit preparation, simulation, measurement, and analysis. The other is the standalone estimation workflow that accepts pre-collected measurement data from any hardware platform in Qurrium's data format. We demonstrate both workflows through examples of a cluster state and an Ising time-evolved state. Furthermore, we report results obtained from a superconducting quantum processor developed by Academia Sinica and analyzed using the standalone estimation workflow. Qurrium is built on Qiskit, the dominant framework in quantum computing software, making it directly accessible to the large community of researchers already working with Qiskit. The source code is openly available at https://github.com/qurrium/qurrium and the example code is provided at https://github.com/qurrium/classical-shadow-examples.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the Loschmidt echo for free fermions undergoing a dynamical quantum phase transition (DQPT). By mapping the problem to instanton calculations, the authors show that the transition manifests as a logarithmic singularity in the dynamical free energy. This provides a deep connection between quantum quench dynamics, topology, and large-deviation theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between non-equilibrium quantum dynamics (Loschmidt echo) and topological phase transitions, using tools from random matrix theory and large deviations, is highly relevant to understanding quantum thermalization and non-equilibrium critical phenomena.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the Loschmidt echo in a system undergoing a dynamical quantum phase transition (DQPT).

**Main result.** The dynamical free energy develops a logarithmic singularity at the DQPT, which is understood as a topological change in the dominant instanton configuration.

**Method.** The analysis uses large-deviation theory, mapping the problem to a Coulomb gas model and employing instanton calculations in the complex time plane.

**Model / system.** The system consists of N non-interacting spinless lattice fermions on a 1D lattice, initialized in a double-domain-wall state. The dynamics are governed by a tight-binding Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Loschmidt echo, dynamical free energy, and the instanton configuration.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Large N limit, large time limit, and the rescaled time $\tau = t/N$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the large-N limit and the characterization of the echo by the dominant complex instanton configuration.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the initial double-domain-wall configuration and the topological change of the dominant instanton configuration across the critical point.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews known results, maps the problem to the Coulomb gas, analyzes the real-time DQPT, and extends the analysis to complex time to study post-selection effects.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the Loschmidt echo in a system of N non-interacting spinless lattice fermions released from a double-domain-wall initial state. In the large-N limit, the return probability is characterized by a large-deviation rate function known as the dynamical free energy. The Loschmidt echo is dominated by a complex instanton configuration, and the dynamical free energy develops a logarithmic singularity at the dynamical quantum phase transition. We obtain analytical results in the short-time and long-time regimes and support them with numerical computations. We show that the transition can be understood as a topological change of the dominant instanton configuration, analogous to the emergence of a cut in random matrix theory. We further show that post-selection can drive the system through two successive DQPTs, associated with successive topological changes of the dominant instanton configuration in complex time.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a quantum algorithm to simulate Lindbladian evolution with optimal query and gate complexity. It achieves a complexity matching the lower bound for Hamiltonian simulation by employing a sophisticated one-query transducer construction. This breakthrough provides a highly efficient tool for simulating open quantum systems in quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the computational efficiency of simulating open quantum systems, which is crucial for modeling realistic physical processes like decoherence in quantum computing hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to determine the optimal query complexity for simulating quantum evolution governed by a Lindbladian master equation.

**Main result.** The authors achieve a query complexity matching the lower bound for Hamiltonian simulation, $O(	au + rac{\log(1/\epsilon)}{\log(e + \log(1/\epsilon)/	au)})$, for time-independent and time-dependent Lindbladians.

**Method.** The simulation is implemented using a one-query transducer construction that approximates the evolution channel as a product of short-time steps, leveraging compressed Kraus-label representations and rotation factorization.

**Model / system.** The system is described by Lindbladian dynamics, $\mathcal{L}(ho) = -i[H, ho] + 	ext{Tr}_E(Bho B^\dagger) - rac{1}{2}\{B^\dagger B, ho\}$, which models open-system evolution. The simulation approximates $e^{t\mathcal{L}}(ho)$ in the diamond norm.

**Key observables.** Diamond-norm error ($\epsilon$), Query Complexity, Gate Complexity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time $t$, time normalization $	au = (\alpha_H + \alpha_B^2)t$, and the approximation parameter $q$ (related to the number of factors).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes access to specific block and projected unitary encodings for the Hamiltonian $H$ and jump operators $B$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 4 illustrates the sequential product structure for $J=4$ steps, showing the path through subspaces. Figure 5 shows the output Kraus labels for one summand of the expansion, detailing index determination.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the simulation by first establishing the required isometry $W_\delta$ using a transducer formalism. It then constructs the product isometry $W_J$ via a reuse construction, bounding the error using polynomial approximations $Q_q(\zeta)$, and finally deriving the optimal query and gate complexity bounds.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We give a quantum algorithm for Lindbladian simulation given a block encoding of the Hamiltonian $H$ and a projected unitary encoding of the stacked jump operator $B=\sum_{k=1}^m \lvert k\rangle\otimes L_k$, with normalization factors $α_H$ and $α_B$, respectively. For evolution time $t$, set $τ=(α_H+α_B^2)t$. The algorithm approximates the evolution channel to diamond-norm error $\varepsilon$ using $O\!\left(τ+\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)}{\log\!\left(e+\log(1/\varepsilon)/τ\right)}\right)$ oracle queries, matching the query lower bound for Hamiltonian simulation. The number of additional one- and two-qubit gates is linear in the query complexity up to polylogarithmic factors. The query- and gate-complexity bounds extend to Lipschitz-continuous time-dependent Lindbladians under coherent time-indexed oracle access. Our construction uses a one-query transducer that implements a product of rational approximations to short-time evolution when supplied with a catalyst. We bound the error from omitting the catalyst by exploiting orthogonality between different sequences of Kraus labels. The gate implementation combines a compressed Kraus-label representation, which stores only the positions and values of the nonzero labels, with the rotation factorization of Chen et al.

</details>

### [A Programmable Rydberg Quantum Bus for Nonlocal Connectivity](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18447v1)

**Authors:** X. Jin, F. Yang, Weibin Li, X. Q. Shao

**Summary.** This paper shows how a chain of Rydberg atoms can act as a programmable quantum bus to overcome the limitations of local connectivity in neutral-atom quantum processors. By analyzing virtual excitations, the authors derive an effective Hamiltonian that mediates controllable, nonlocal interactions. This establishes a scalable route toward reconfigurable quantum network architectures with quasi-all-to-all coupling.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, hardware-level mechanism for engineering long-range, programmable interactions in neutral-atom quantum simulators, directly addressing a major bottleneck in scaling quantum networks.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is achieving flexible, nonlocal connectivity in quantum processing nodes using neutral atoms, as native Rydberg interactions are too spatially dependent for large-scale systems.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that a Rydberg atom chain can function as a programmable quantum bus, effectively transforming a local 1D architecture into a quasi-all-to-all interacting network.

**Method.** The analysis employs the Green's-function continued-fraction method and Feshbach partitioning to derive an effective Hamiltonian describing the mediated interactions in the dispersive regime.

**Model / system.** The system is a 1D chain of Rydberg atoms coupled to external data units. The dynamics are analyzed by deriving an effective Hamiltonian that mediates interactions via virtual excitations in the dispersive regime.

**Key observables.** Population dynamics in different excitation manifolds, chiral transport fidelity, and the ability to generate W-states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The mechanism is robust against long-range interactions, atomic position fluctuations, and finite Rydberg-state lifetimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis initially focuses on the single-excitation subspace, though simulations incorporate full long-range interactions for realism.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the atomic chain coupling, time evolution of populations in excitation manifolds, and the mechanism for chiral transport using alternating Hamiltonians.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the connectivity problem to deriving the effective Hamiltonian using Green's functions, demonstrating specific functionalities like chiral transport, and concluding with the realization of scalable W-state generation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Scalable quantum networks require processing nodes with flexible internal connectivity, yet neutral-atom architectures remain constrained by the strong spatial dependence of native Rydberg interactions. Here we show that a Rydberg atom chain can act as a coherent quantum bus, converting a locally connected one-dimensional architecture into an effectively nonlocal interaction network. Virtual excitations in the dispersive regime mediate controllable interactions between spatially separated data units, which we derive analytically using a Green's-function continued-fraction method. The resulting mechanism is not restricted to single-excitation dynamics and supports several distinct functionalities, including Floquet-engineered chiral transport, remote entanglement of mechanical oscillators, and destructive interference for selectively suppressing unwanted dipole exchange. Simulations incorporating full long-range Rydberg interactions, atomic position fluctuations, and finite Rydberg-state lifetimes show that the mediated dynamics remain robust under experimentally relevant conditions. These results establish Rydberg chains as programmable coherent mediators for extending the internal connectivity of neutral-atom quantum nodes toward quasi-all-to-all coupling, providing a hardware-level route toward scalable and reconfigurable quantum-network architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates entanglement-enhanced distributed quantum sensing using continuous variable light implemented on a scalable Silicon-on-Insulator photonic chip. By generating and measuring four-mode entanglement, the authors achieved a significantly higher precision in phase estimation compared to classical methods. This work advances the field by proving the feasibility of quantum sensing in compact, integrated optical hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, scalable, and integrated platform demonstration of quantum metrology principles, directly advancing the hardware realization of quantum sensing protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to demonstrate and quantify the advantage of using continuous variable (CV) distributed quantum sensing with entangled states over classical (separable) states for measuring phase shifts.

**Main result.** The entangled sensing protocol achieved a precision of 0.199(16) dB below the shot noise limit, significantly outperforming the separable state limit of 0.041(18) dB.

**Method.** The sensing relies on measuring the DC offset in integrated homodyne detectors after generating a four-mode entangled state on a photonic chip, allowing for the estimation of the average phase shift.

**Model / system.** The experiment uses a Silicon-on-Insulator (SOI) Photonic Integrated Circuit (PIC) containing a Beam Splitter Network (BSN) and four integrated homodyne detectors. Entanglement is generated from an off-chip squeezed vacuum source.

**Key observables.** Mean subtracted photocurrent ($\langle\delta\hat{i}_jangle$), average phase shift ($\phi_{avg}$), and the measured phase variance ($\Delta^2\varphi_{avg}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of modes ($M=4$), total system efficiency ($\eta = 0.203(5)$), and the comparison between entangled and separable state precision limits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitation noted is the loss introduced by coupling the squeezed light on or off the chip, which limits the achievable performance.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the PIC schematic, the process of converting raw time-domain data into well-defined quadratures, and comparative plots showing the measured phase precision advantage for both entangled and separable states.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the physical setup (SOI PIC, BSN, detectors), the theoretical framework for precision calculation ($\sigma_e$ vs $\sigma_s$), the measurement techniques (noise locking, quadrature extraction), and finally presents the quantitative comparison of sensing performance.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Distributed quantum sensing is an emerging application of quantum networking, where entangled probe states are employed to sense combinations of delocalized parameters with enhanced precision relative to using separable states. Squeezed states of light are a prime resource for experimental demonstrations of entanglement-enhanced sensing, because they can be generated and entangled deterministically. Existing distributed quantum sensing experiments have been fundamentally limited in scalability due to their bulk-optic architectures. Meanwhile, integrated photonics provides a scalable and compact platform for quantum sensors. Here we demonstrate entanglement-enhanced sensing of linear functions of four phase shifts in an integrated photonic circuit. We find an entanglement-enhanced precision of 0.199(16) dB below the shot noise limit compared to 0.041(18) dB for separable states. A four-mode entangled state is generated on-chip with entanglement verification and phase sensing also performed on-chip with an array of four integrated homodyne detectors.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a general, first-principles derivation showing how stochastic dynamics, like Brownian motion, emerge from quantum mechanics when a particle interacts with a thermal environment. It derives the necessary quantum master equation and the corresponding Langevin equation without assuming specific forms for the environment coupling or correlation statistics. The work is significant for understanding the fundamental link between microscopic quantum field theory and macroscopic dissipation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it provides a rigorous, general framework for open quantum systems, directly addressing the transition from unitary quantum evolution to dissipative, classical-like stochastic behavior, which is central to quantum thermodynamics and open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental problem of deriving how macroscopic, stochastic dynamics, such as Brownian motion, emerges from the underlying, microscopic quantum dynamics of a probe particle interacting with a large thermal environment.

**Main result.** The authors provide a general, first-principles derivation linking microscopic quantum correlations to the emergent Langevin description, establishing the necessary equilibration conditions via KMS-type properties.

**Method.** The methodology involves deriving the quantum master equation, utilizing the Schwinger-Keldysh formalism for path integrals, and taking the long-time limit to establish the Markovian approximation.

**Model / system.** The system consists of a quantum probe particle coupled to a general environment (bath) described by a Hamiltonian. The analysis focuses on the reduced density matrix evolution and the resulting stochastic force.

**Key observables.** The reduced density matrix, the emergent stochastic force, and the rate constant $S(L, v)$ governing the decay.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inverse temperature ($eta$), separation of scales between probe and environment dynamics, and the nature of the correlation functions (Gaussian vs. non-Gaussian).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation requires spacetime translations, parity, and time reversal symmetries, and crucially assumes a separation of scales to justify the Markovian limit.

**Figures summary.** No specific figures were summarized, but the notes indicate derivations involving evolution loops $\langle W v,t angle(L)$ and rate equations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the general Hamiltonian and deriving the quantum master equation (Section III), establishing the thermal equilibrium condition via the KMS property (Section IV), and finally deriving the emergent stochastic dynamics and Langevin description (Section V).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Starting from a general Hamiltonian describing the quantum dynamics of probe particles interacting with a set of environment degrees of freedom, we derive the quantum master equation with which the reduced density matrix of a probe particle evolves, the general equilibration condition that they satisfy when the environment is prepared in a thermal state -- encoded in a KMS property for line operators extended in real time -- and the Langevin description that emerges. The novelty of our results resides in their generality: We do not assume a specific form of the environment self-coupling, of the environment operator that couples the particle to it, of the statistics of its correlation functions, whether the statistics of the momentum transfer is Gaussian or not, or whether the probe particles move relativistically. Our results only require that spacetime translations, parity and time reversal be symmetries of the Hamiltonian, and that there exists a separation of scales between those characterizing the dispersion relation of the probe particle and those of the environment. We also discuss the limiting case in which Gaussian Brownian motion emerges. Overall, our results constitute a first-principles derivation of what properties of the underlying quantum field theory govern the energy loss and momentum fluctuations of probe particles.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes using quantum spin systems to simulate complex detector observables from high-energy physics. By mapping lattice operators to continuum CFT predictions, the authors demonstrate that advanced numerical techniques successfully reproduce theoretical expectations at critical points. This provides a concrete roadmap for realizing collider physics simulations on tabletop quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** The direct application of numerical methods (DMRG, TEBD) to model high-energy physics observables like energy flow provides a concrete, testable pathway for quantum simulation platforms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental challenge of calculating detector observables in Quantum Field Theory (QFT) because these observables are intrinsically real-time and asymptotic.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that lattice constructions for energy-flow observables in the 1+1D Ising model show quantitative agreement with Conformal Field Theory (CFT) predictions at criticality.

**Method.** They construct lattice detector operators and use advanced numerical techniques like DMRG and TEBD to simulate real-time evolution and calculate correlators, comparing results against CFT predictions.

**Model / system.** The study uses quantum spin systems, specifically the 1+1-dimensional transverse-field Ising model, as a platform to simulate high-energy physics phenomena.

**Key observables.** Detector observables, energy-flow operators $E(\hat{n})$, multi-point correlators $\langle E(\hat{n}_1) \cdots E(\hat{n}_k) angle$, and one-point energy fractions ($F_R$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical point ($g=1$), the lattice spacing ($a_l$), the source-detector separation ($R_{	ext{det}}$), and time evolution parameters ($\delta t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on the controlled setting provided by the critical point for quantitative benchmarking, and some calculations assume boundary effects are negligible.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare CFT predictions (solid markers) against DMRG/simulation extractions (translucent markers) for operator decompositions and two-point functions, showing agreement at criticality.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical connection between lattice operators and continuum CFT observables, details the numerical implementation using DMRG/TEBD for both static and real-time calculations, and validates the framework by comparing results across different coupling regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Detector observables pose a fundamental challenge for nonperturbative lattice methods due to their intrinsically real-time and asymptotic nature. We propose quantum simulation platforms as an ideal setting for studying these observables. Using quantum spin systems as a demonstration, we construct lattice detector operators and establish their connection to continuum energy-flow observables. We demonstrate this framework in the $1+1$-dimensional quantum Ising model, where finite lattice calculations show quantitative agreement with conformal field theory expectations at criticality. These results support the interpretation of the lattice flow operators as regulated counterparts of continuum detectors in the scaling and asymptotic limits, providing a concrete route toward realizing high-energy collider physics on table-top quantum platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper re-evaluates the concept of 'instant thermalization' in large-$q$ SYK models by analyzing the two-time Green's function. The authors demonstrate that while some correlations appear instantaneously thermal, the overall effective temperature is governed by a finite relaxation rate set by non-thermal, off-diagonal time correlations. This suggests that 'instantaneous' thermalization is a concept restricted to specific parts of the time evolution.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed analysis of non-equilibrium dynamics and the distinction between different correlation functions' thermalization rates provides a sophisticated framework for understanding how memory effects persist in strongly interacting quantum systems, which is relevant to open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the concept of 'instant thermalization' in large-$q$ Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) models, contrasting different physical interpretations of how quickly a system reaches equilibrium.

**Main result.** The authors find that while the diagonal blocks of the Green's function instantly acquire a thermal form, the overall effective temperature inherits a finite, non-zero thermalization rate set by the inherently non-thermal off-diagonal time blocks.

**Method.** The analysis involves studying the real-time Green's function in the two-time plane using a $1/q$ expansion, calculating effective temperatures, and comparing results from different time-block correlations.

**Model / system.** The system is the Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) model, specifically considering $q/2$-body interactions, subjected to a quench protocol. The analysis relies on mapping the dynamics onto the two-time plane using Wigner coordinates.

**Key observables.** Effective temperatures ($T_{	ext{eff}}$), thermalization rates ($\Gamma$), and the Green's function $G(t_1, t_2)$ across different time blocks.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Large connectivity $q$, quench time, and the relative time $	au = t_1 - t_2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is performed to leading order in $1/q$, and the authors distinguish between the thermal nature of diagonal time blocks and the non-thermal nature of off-diagonal blocks.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the four blocks of the two-time plane, showing diagonal blocks as thermal and off-diagonal blocks as non-thermal. They also show fits for $T_{	ext{eff}}(t_{	ext{avg}})$ and the decay rate $\Gamma$ extracted from these fits.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical context of instant thermalization in SYK models. It then analyzes the Green's function in time blocks, contrasting the instantaneous thermal behavior of the diagonal blocks with the finite rate set by the off-diagonal blocks, leading to a refined understanding of the concept.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Motivated by the Planckian thermalization rate $Γ\sim T$ observed in the two-body interacting SYK model, we study thermalization in the $q/2$-body case. Previous studies of such systems have established the notion of instantaneous thermalization to leading order in $1/q$. It was conjectured that the thermalization may still be Planckian but with a divergent rate $Γ\sim T q$, explaining the ``instantaneous'' part. For an analytically and numerically tractable system, we calculate the effective temperatures after a quench at time $t = 0$ and indeed find a Planckian rate, albeit with the unexpected decaying behavior $Γ\sim T q^{-1} $. This is contrasted with the behavior of the causal Green's function $\mathcal{G}(t_1, t_2)$, which instantly acquires a thermal form in the two-time plane block $t_1, t_2 > 0$.   The resulting picture is that instant thermalization is a meaningful concept only for this block, while the off-diagonal blocks $t_1 \cdot t_2 < 0$ are inherently non-thermal at any $q$. Since the effective temperature is obtained from correlations spanning all blocks, it inherits a finite rate set by the off-diagonal. We illustrate this directly by studying the non-thermal correlations' time dependence.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an advanced mathematical technique to drastically speed up the calculation of the sixth-order time-convolutionless master-equation generator. By decomposing complex time integrals into simpler components solvable by convolutions and recurrences, the computational scaling is improved from cubic to near-linear in the number of time steps. This enables long-time simulations of complex, non-Markovian quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a significant algorithmic breakthrough for simulating non-Markovian dynamics, which is crucial for understanding open quantum systems where memory effects are dominant.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the computational bottleneck associated with directly evaluating the high-order (sixth-order) time-convolutionless (TCL6) master-equation generator for open quantum systems.

**Main result.** A novel algorithmic reduction is derived that lowers the computational complexity from $O(N_t^3)$ to $O(N_t \log^2 N_t)$ for the TCL6 time series evaluation.

**Method.** The core method involves mathematically separating the time-dependent bath correlation functions into independent, joined, and interlocked history components, allowing for evaluation via cumulative sums, convolutions, and specialized recurrences.

**Model / system.** The system is a fixed finite-dimensional quantum system coupled to a stationary centered Gaussian bath via a single Hermitian operator. The dynamics are governed by the time-local generator derived from the TCL expansion.

**Key observables.** The complete TCL6 time series, $K_I(t)$, and its matrix elements $S_{6,k}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The time step count ($N_t$), the order of the expansion (e.g., TCL6, $	ext{TCL}_{2n}$), and the history-coupling depth ($\ell_H$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The reduction relies on the system coupling through a single Hermitian operator to a Gaussian bath, and the complexity analysis assumes the availability of specific reduction identities.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the overall reduction process from commutators to evaluating scalar sums. Figure 2 details the recursive structure used for the interlocked-history contraction.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically moves from the general definition of the TCL6 generator to deriving an exact reduction by separating fixed operator coefficients from scalar bath kernels, which are then evaluated using cumulative sums, FFT convolutions, and specialized dyadic recursions for interlocked histories.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Direct quadrature of the Hadamard-reduced sixth-order time-convolutionless (TCL6) generator at all $N_t$ sampled times requires $O(N_t^3)$ operations at fixed system dimension. We derive an exact reduction for a finite-dimensional system coupled through one Hermitian operator to a stationary centered Gaussian bath. By separating fixed operator coefficients from scalar bath kernels, the complete TCL6 time series is reduced to cumulative sums and first-order recurrences, one-dimensional causal convolutions, and an exact dyadic recursion for interlocked histories. With the algorithm, evaluating the complete TCL6 time series requires $O(N_t\log^2 N_t)$ operations at fixed system dimension. In addition, we show that TCL$2n$ can be evaluated with $O(N_t\log^{n-1} N_t)$ complexity. A fixed finite Matsubara expansion of the bath correlation function permits $O(N_t\log N_t)$ evaluation at any fixed TCL order. These reductions enable fast long-time simulations of non-Markovian open quantum systems within the regime of validity of the TCL expansion.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theory to understand how projective measurements affect entanglement in quantum critical systems subjected to quenched disorder. By applying the Strong-Disorder Renormalization Group to models like the random Ising chain, the authors calculate the universal decay of measurement-induced entanglement. The findings establish that disorder provides an analytically solvable arena for studying measurement effects, showing remarkable robustness of critical properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a powerful, analytically tractable framework (IRFPs) to study non-equilibrium quantum dynamics (measurements) in disordered systems, complementing the well-established CFT results for clean critical points.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the effect of projective measurements on the entanglement structure of quantum states, specifically focusing on critical states characterized by quenched disorder.

**Main result.** The measurement-induced entanglement in random critical models decays with a universal power-law exponent, and the critical properties of the state remain remarkably robust against finite-density measurements.

**Method.** The authors develop a theory using the Strong-Disorder Renormalization Group (SDRG) approach, which is adapted to analyze measurement processes in systems lacking a Conformal Field Theory (CFT) description.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on 1D critical quantum spin chains, modeling the random transverse-field Ising model (rTFIM) and the random XXZ chain. The analysis utilizes RG flow equations to track the evolution of coupling strengths and cluster sizes.

**Key observables.** Measurement-Induced Entanglement (MIE), measurement-altered criticality, entanglement entropy ($S_A$), and the probability distribution of coupling strengths across a cut.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The universal exponent $(3-\sqrt{5})/2$, the golden ratio $\phi = (1 + \sqrt{5})/2$, and the RG time scale $\Gamma$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the initial state is pure and that the strong disorder limit allows for the successful application of the SDRG framework, making the outcome randomness trivial compared to clean systems.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the geometry of measurement regions (A, B, M) and show the ground state structure for rTFIM, highlighting non-intersecting clusters formed during the RG process.

**Paper structure.** The paper contrasts the known CFT case with the disordered case, developing the measurement theory for Infinite-Randomness Fixed Points (IRFPs). It applies the SDRG to model the ground state and then extends this framework to calculate MIE and analyze the robustness of critical exponents under measurement.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The impact of projective measurements on a many-body quantum state is tightly linked to its underlying entanglement structure. Critical states are particularly sensitive, as long-range entanglement allows local measurements to have global consequences. This has been extensively studied in the context of critical states described by a conformal field theory (CFT), where measurements can alter critical properties in post-measurement quantities ('measurement-altered criticality') and induce long-range entanglement with universal features ('measurement-induced entanglement'). By contrast, little is known about the role of measurements on critical states with quenched disorder, which admit no CFT description. Here, we develop a theory of measurements on one-dimensional critical states governed by infinite-randomness fixed points (IRFPs), focusing on two closely related examples: the random XXZ chain and the random transverse-field Ising model. We show that the measurement-induced entanglement decays as a power-law with universal exponent $(3-\sqrt{5})/2$ in both models, and that the minimum number of measurements required to establish this entanglement obeys a universal scaling form. When only a finite density of measurements is made, we show that the critical properties of the state are remarkably robust: `measurement-altered criticality' is absent, with measurements either preserving the critical exponents, or destroying the criticality entirely. These results establish IRFPs as an analytically tractable arena for measurements on critical states, complementary to the CFT case. The outcome randomness that necessitates replica methods in clean systems becomes trivial at infinite randomness, with the universal response governed by the statistics induced by the quenched disorder.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops and applies a stochastic-generator expansion method to analyze the slow relaxation dynamics in kinetically constrained models (KCMs). By expanding the system's dynamics in terms of the low-temperature excitation concentration, the authors systematically predict the nested hierarchy of metastable states. This work provides a unified theoretical framework connecting quantum and classical descriptions of glassy dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** Although focused on classical models, the successful adaptation of a quantum-derived expansion technique to describe slow relaxation in classical stochastic systems provides a powerful, generalizable theoretical tool for understanding glassy dynamics in many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper revisits the complex hierarchy of relaxation time scales observed in stochastic kinetically constrained models (KCMs). It aims to provide a systematic theoretical description of this slow relaxation process.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that an expansion method, adapted from quantum KCMs, successfully recovers the known nested hierarchy of metastable plateaus in classical KCMs like the East and Fredrickson-Andersen models.

**Method.** The core method is a low-temperature expansion of the stochastic generator in powers of the equilibrium concentration of excitations. This systematic expansion allows for the identification of successive metastable states.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on Kinetically Constrained Models (KCMs), specifically applied to the one-dimensional East and Fredrickson-Andersen (FA) models. The dynamics are governed by a master equation defined by a stochastic generator.

**Key observables.** Relaxation time scales, nested hierarchy of metastable configurations, and the decay of correlation functions following a quench.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The equilibrium concentration of excitations ($c$) serves as the small expansion parameter. The quench process (high-to-low temperature) is crucial for observing the hierarchy.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system obeys detailed balance, allowing for the symmetrization of the stochastic generator. The method relies on the existence of a small parameter ($c$) governing the hopping rates.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the hierarchical relaxation of energy in the East model and show the time evolution of the effective number of configurations for both the East and FA models after a quench.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first establishing the mathematical framework using the stochastic-generator expansion, then applying this method to the classical East and FA models, and finally demonstrating that the resulting time scales systematically reproduce known results for glassy relaxation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We revisit the hierarchy of relaxation time scales in stochastic kinetically constrained models (KCMs) obeying detailed balance, using an expansion method developed recently for their quantum counterparts. In the classical setting, we expand the stochastic generator in the low-temperature equilibrium concentration of excitations. As in quantum KCMs, successive truncations of the expansion reveal a nested hierarchy of metastable configurations that remain frozen on progressively longer time scales. Applying the method to the high-to-low temperature quench in the classical one-dimensional East and Fredrickson-Andersen models, we recover the known hierarchy of metastable plateaus and the associated perturbative time scales. We find that the hierarchical time scales are related to the smallest domain lengths. Our results show that the method developed for quantum kinetically constrained models can be used to provide a systematic description of slow relaxation in their classical counterparts as well.

</details>
