**Summary.** This paper reports the first experimental demonstration of entanglement swapping across a multi-node quantum relay while simultaneously transmitting high-speed classical data over the same optical fibers. By characterizing the noise introduced by co-propagating classical signals, the authors establish a roadmap for deploying advanced quantum technologies in real-world, noisy communication channels.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles the engineering challenge of integrating quantum signals into existing classical infrastructure, which is a major bottleneck for scaling quantum networks.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is demonstrating entanglement swapping and conventional classical communications operating simultaneously over the same optical fibers, which is crucial for real-world quantum network deployment.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated entanglement swapping over 40 km while simultaneously transmitting 10-Gbps classical data, proving the feasibility of coexisting quantum and classical networks.

**Method.** The work combines experimental measurements of entanglement fidelity and noise characterization with theoretical modeling using Gaussian overlap integrals to simulate detection probabilities.

**Model / system.** The system involves a five-node quantum relay topology connected by long-distance fibers, utilizing dense-wavelength division multiplexing (DWDM) to co-propagate time-bin entangled photons and high-power classical data signals.

**Key observables.** Entanglement swapping visibility, quantum fidelity ($F$), and the rate of spontaneous Raman scattering (SpRS) noise photons.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Maximum fiber length of 40 km, simultaneous 10-Gbps classical data transmission, and the trade-off between quantum fidelity and classical launch power.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis characterizes the trade-off with Raman noise, and simulations explore different wavelength allocations (C/C, O/C, C/O) to mitigate noise effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the conceptual setup, detailed experimental architecture, and quantitative results showing visibility and noise rates as a function of classical launch power and fiber length.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical necessity of quantum-classical coexistence, detailing the experimental setup and measurements of noise/fidelity, and concluding with theoretical modeling to predict performance limits based on noise sources.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum networks are resources for scaling quantum computers and distributed sensing technologies while offering post-quantum security benefits. Teleporting non-classical resources like entanglement, via so called entanglement swapping, is essential for networks in particular overcoming rate-loss limits via quantum repeaters. Deploying these systems on real infrastructure will likely require multiplexing photonic qubits into fibers carrying 'classical' light encoding standard Internet communications and control plane signals for multi-node quantum protocols. Here, we report the first demonstration of entanglement swapping and conventional communications operating over the same fibers. Entanglement is swapped across a five-node quantum relay topology connected by four long-distance fibers, each populated with classical data signals. Time-bin entangled photons in the C-band are multiplexed alongside C-band classical signals using dense-wavelength division multiplexing, introducing noise photons generated by high-power classical light. We experimentally and theoretically characterize the trade-off between quantum fidelity and Raman noise photons. Entanglement swapping is demonstrated over a maximum fiber length of 40 km (four 10-km fibers) while simultaneously transmitting 10-Gbps classical data through all fibers. These results represent a significant advancement in the demonstrated complexity of coexisting quantum and classical networks and provide a roadmap for achieving the widespread deployment of advanced quantum technologies.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes how the purity of a quantum system decays when it interacts with a continuous-variable pointer observable. The decay dynamics are shown to be entirely dictated by the spectral structure of the system's Hamiltonian, leading to quantifiable bounds and characteristic timescales. It provides deep insights into how spectral gaps govern the rate of quantum information loss.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, analytical framework for understanding decoherence mechanisms in quantum systems where the environment coupling is structured by the system's internal energy spectrum, which is highly relevant to quantum measurement theory and open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the time evolution of purity in a quantum system coupled to a continuous-variable pointer via a von Neumann-type interaction.

**Main result.** Purity loss is governed by the spectral separation of the system Hamiltonian, leading to state-dependent bounds and a characteristic timescale $t_\epsilon \propto \sigma/(|g|\Delta_{\min})$.

**Method.** The authors derive an exact analytical expression for the time-dependent purity by tracing out the pointer degrees of freedom, utilizing properties of Gaussian states.

**Model / system.** The model consists of a quantum system ($\hat{H}$) coupled to a continuous-variable pointer ($\hat{p}$) via the interaction $\hat{H}_{	ext{int}} = g \hat{H} \otimes \hat{p}$. The analysis is performed assuming the pointer starts in a Gaussian state.

**Key observables.** Purity $P(t) = 	ext{Tr}[ho_S(t)^2]$, short-time purity curvature $P''(0)$, and the asymptotic purity $P_\infty$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strength ($g$), pointer width ($\sigma$), minimum populated spectral gap ($\Delta_{\min}$), and the energy variance ($	ext{Var}_{\psi_S}(\hat{H})$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The pointer is assumed to be initially in a Gaussian pure state, and the interaction is strictly bilinear ($\hat{H}_{	ext{int}} = g \hat{H} \otimes \hat{p}$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of purity for equally spaced $N$-level systems, confirming $P(0)=1$ and $P(\infty)=1/N$, and show the dependence of purity decay on the spectral spacing $\Delta$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the exact purity formula, derives state-dependent two-sided spectral-gap bounds, analyzes the short-time dynamics controlled by energy variance, and finally establishes the long-time approach timescale controlled by $\Delta_{\min}$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the purity dynamics of a quantum system coupled to a continuous-variable pointer through a von Neumann-type interaction Hamiltonian of the form $\hat H_{\mathrm{int}}=g\,\hat H\otimes\hat p$. For an initially Gaussian pointer state, the interaction generates energy-dependent conditional translations whose mutual overlaps are determined explicitly by the populated spectral separations of the system Hamiltonian. After tracing out the pointer degrees of freedom, we obtain the reduced density operator and derive an exact analytical expression for the time-dependent purity.   Using this expression, we establish two-sided purity bounds governed by the minimum and maximum nonzero energy gaps on the populated spectral support. These bounds provide a state-dependent spectral characterization of the loss of purity and become exact for two-level systems. We further show that the short-time decrease of purity is controlled by the Hamiltonian variance of the initial state, with $\mathcal P''(0)=-(g^2/σ^2) \operatorname{Var}_{ψ_S}(\hat H)$. In addition, an explicit sufficient timescale is derived for the purity to approach its asymptotic value within a prescribed tolerance, revealing the scaling $t_{\varepsilon}\proptoσ/(|g|Δ_{\min})$. Finally, the general results are illustrated for an equally weighted $N$-level system with an equally spaced spectrum, for which the asymptotic purity is $1/N$. The analysis clarifies the distinct roles of spectral separation, energy variance, coupling strength, and pointer width in Hamiltonian-conditioned purity loss.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates how entanglement survives decoherence in a two-spin system coupled to an environment. By using advanced mathematical techniques like the stationary-phase approximation, the authors show that coherence is protected when the local environmental fields satisfy a specific resonance condition. This protection leads to an anomalously slow $t^{-1/2}$ decay, revealing that the pointer state is governed by a collective environmental resonance rather than individual system couplings.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a detailed, quantitative mechanism for environmental protection of entanglement, showing that the pointer basis selection can be dictated by a global, statistical resonance condition rather than local couplings.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes quantum decoherence in a two-spin system coupled to an environment to determine the protected quantum states (pointer states) that survive environmental interaction.

**Main result.** Under a specific resonance condition between the two environments, coherence between maximally entangled Bell-type states survives with an anomalously slow decay rate of $t^{-1/2}$, which is protected by a collective environmental resonance.

**Method.** The analysis employs the Stationary-Phase Approximation (saddle-point method) on the integral representation of the reduced density matrix, comparing decay rates from isolated points versus resonance manifolds.

**Model / system.** The system consists of two spin-1/2 particles coupled noncommutingly to two independent static environmental fields and mutually coupled via an interaction term ($\sigma_y^{(1)}\sigma_y^{(2)}$).

**Key observables.** Coherence decay rates ($\sim t^{-1}$ vs. $\sim t^{-1/2}$), the reduced density matrix $ho_S(t)$, and the pointer observable.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The resonance condition ($\Omega_1(B_1) = \Omega_2(B_2)$), the inter-system coupling strength ($g$), and the time scale $t$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Stationary-Phase Approximation and treats the environmental couplings as independent variables in the continuum limit.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the Hamiltonian, constructs the reduced density matrix via an integral representation, applies the SPA to identify different stationary points (isolated vs. manifold), and compares the resulting coherence decay laws.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this manuscript, we analyze the dynamics of a model in which two spin-1/2 systems are each coupled noncommutingly to a static environmental field and to a self-Hamiltonian, while also being mutually coupled through a $σ_y^{(1)}σ_y^{(2)}$ interaction. By constructing an exact solution that exploits parity symmetry, and by applying the stationary-phase approximation (the saddle-point method) in the continuum limit of the environmental field, we show that coherence associated with the ordinary isolated stationary point decays as $t^{-1}$, whereas, under the resonance condition at which the local effective fields of the two spins cancel each other, only the coherence between a pair of maximally entangled, Bell-type states- the eigenbasis of the inter-system coupling-survives, decaying via the anomalously slow power law $t^{-1/2}$. Through a comparison with the pointer-basis theory of W. H. Zurek and coworkers (the 1981 and 2005 models), we further reveal a mechanism not reducible to local coupling: in this system, the pointer observable is determined not by the individual system-environment couplings but by a statistical resonance condition between the two environments.

</details>

**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This paper improves the theoretical framework for quantum feedback cooling of a harmonic oscillator by redesigning the standard LQG cost function. By making the cost function sensitive to the feedback-induced potential shift, the authors derive a superior optimal control law. This redesigned control achieves better cooling performance, especially when measurement efficiencies are very high, confirming the physical necessity of matching the cost function to the true physical objective.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to AMO physics and quantum optics as it tackles optimal quantum control, specifically demonstrating how the precise physical objective (relative to the shifted potential) must dictate the mathematical cost function used in the control theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the optimal control problem for feedback cooling of a quantum harmonic oscillator by redesigning the Linear-Quadratic-Gaussian (LQG) cost function.

**Main result.** The redesigned LQG control achieves a lower steady-state phonon occupation number than conventional methods, providing a significant advantage as the measurement detection efficiency approaches unity.

**Method.** The authors derive the optimal feedback law by formulating and minimizing a redesigned cost function that explicitly accounts for the feedback-induced shift of the potential, solving the resulting optimal control problem.

**Model / system.** The system is a quantum harmonic oscillator, modeled by a Hamiltonian whose potential minimum is shifted by the feedback process. The dynamics are governed by a stochastic quantum master equation.

**Key observables.** The achievable minimum steady-state phonon occupation number ($n$) is the primary diagnostic.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Detection efficiency ($\eta$) is crucial, with the advantage of the new method becoming pronounced as $\eta 	o 1$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the conditional state remains Gaussian, and the authors clarify that the redesigned and conventional cost functions represent distinct physical control objectives.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares phonon occupation number vs. measurement strength for low efficiency, showing near indistinguishability; Figure 2 compares the same for high efficiency ($\eta=1$), showing a clear advantage for the redesigned LQG control.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the problem by first considering the classical limit, then formulating the quantum optimal control problem using the redesigned cost function, deriving the optimal feedback law, and finally comparing the resulting cooling performance against established methods like LPF feedback.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Linear--quadratic--Gaussian (LQG) control is optimal only with respect to a prescribed cost function, the choice of which dictates the physical objective of the control. We consider feedback cooling of a continuously monitored quantum harmonic oscillator by shifting the minimum of its trapping potential. In this setting, the physically relevant cooling objective can be defined as minimizing the oscillator's energy relative to the feedback-shifted potential. In contrast, conventional LQG control evaluates the energy from a fixed origin and thus fails to directly optimize this quantity. To address this problem, we introduce a redesigned cost function that explicitly accounts for the feedback-induced shift of the potential. We then derive the corresponding optimal feedback law and obtain an analytic expression for the minimum achievable steady-state phonon occupation number. The redesigned LQG control achieves a lower occupation number than low-pass-filter (LPF) feedback formulated for the same cooling objective. While this improvement is minor at detection efficiencies currently attainable in experiments---indicating that LPF feedback already delivers near-optimal cooling performance---the advantage becomes pronounced as the detection efficiency approaches unity. In this regime, the redesigned LQG control provides an increasing advantage for reaching the motional ground state at a finite measurement strength. We clarify that the conventional and redesigned cost functions represent distinct control objectives rather than different implementations of the same optimization problem.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper quantifies the 'observational ergotropic advantage'—the extra work extractable due to measurement coherence—by introducing a novel operational quantifier. It proves that this advantage is directly linked to measurement coherence and demonstrates that this resource is not preserved under all physically allowed operations, which has implications for quantum resource theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly connects fundamental concepts from quantum thermodynamics (work extraction, ergotropy) with the resource theory of quantum measurements, providing rigorous, quantitative tools for assessing quantum advantages in energy harvesting.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to establish a quantitative, operational measure for the advantage gained in work extraction from a quantum system when only partial state knowledge is available via measurement, and to characterize this advantage using measurement coherence.

**Main result.** The authors derive a faithful operational quantifier for the work advantage, showing that measurement coherence is necessary and sufficient for a positive advantage. Crucially, they demonstrate that this advantage is not monotonic under physically incoherent operations.

**Method.** The work involves defining and maximizing the 'observational ergotropic advantage' ($A_H^M$) by comparing the passive energy gap before and after coarse-graining the state based on the measurement outcome. The analysis is extended to test monotonicity using specific quantum channels.

**Model / system.** The study considers general quantum systems, using qubits and qutrits as explicit examples. The physics revolves around work extraction, ergotropy, and the resource quantification of measurement coherence within the framework of quantum thermodynamics.

**Key observables.** Observational Ergotropic Advantage ($A_H^M$), Robustness of measurement coherence ($RC(M)$), and $l_{\infty}$-norm based coherence measure ($Cl_{\infty}(M)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension of the Hilbert space ($d$), the Hamiltonian ($H$), and the specific structure of the measurement POVMs $\{M(\mu)\}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis builds upon the definition of ergotropy and assumes the existence of free operations (like PIOs) to test resource monotonicity.

**Figures summary.** The notes do not provide a summary of figures, but the analysis involves deriving specific mathematical expressions for the advantage under different conditions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for a quantitative measure (Chunk 1), developing the operational advantage quantifier $A_H^M$ (Chunk 2), exploring resource theory bounds (Chunk 3), and finally testing the monotonicity of this advantage under physical constraints (Chunk 4).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The use of quantum resources in the work extraction from a quantum system is an emerging research topic. Recently, [arXiv:2602.22893] established the necessity of measurement coherence for obtaining advantage in work extraction from an unknown isolated quantum system. However, a quantitative relation between the magnitude of this advantage and established measures of measurement coherence is missing. Here, we establish such a connection by introducing a faithful operational quantifier of the work advantage provided by a measurement. We show that measurement coherence is necessary and sufficient for a positive advantage, and derive upper and lower bounds in terms of the robustness of measurement coherence and an $l_{\infty}$ norm based coherence measure respectively. Finally, we examine this quantifier of advantage from the resource theoretic perspective. Our results provide an operational characterisation of measurement coherence as a resource for work extraction and reveal a nontrivial relation between its resource content and thermodynamic value.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study models a photon-pair source using a semiconductor quantum dot coupled to a plasmonic cavity. The key finding is that a permanent dipole moment, arising from structural asymmetry, is necessary to break a symmetry that otherwise forbids the required radiative transition. The paper calculates the resulting photon correlations, providing a theoretical blueprint for such a quantum light source.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum light sources by modeling strong light-matter coupling in solid-state systems, which is a core topic in cavity QED and open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to theoretically model a semiconductor photon-pair source based on a radiative cascade mechanism.

**Main result.** The presence of a permanent exciton dipole, which breaks a key symmetry, is shown to be necessary to enable the radiative transition between the upper and lower polariton states, thus establishing an operating principle for the source.

**Method.** The analysis uses an effective quantum model and photon-counting theory to calculate emission probabilities and analyze photon correlations.

**Model / system.** The system involves a GaAs quantum dot coupled to a localized electromagnetic mode (plasmon) between a metal nanoparticle and a mirror. The dynamics are governed by a Hamiltonian incorporating exciton and plasmon interactions, crucially including a permanent dipole term.

**Key observables.** Idler photon emission rate, signal photon emission rate, photon count fluctuations, and correlations between the two emission channels.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strengths ($g, d_p$), cavity/plasmon frequencies ($\omega_m$), and the permanent dipole strength ($\Delta\mu$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations rely on an effective model, assume specific emitter properties and optical loss rates, and the final feasibility remains open to experimental realization.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the hypothetical device geometry (GaAs QD between Au nanoparticle and mirror). Figure 2 compares the forbidden vs. allowed idler transition rates based on the dipole moment.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical system and the role of the permanent dipole in breaking symmetry. It then uses an effective model to calculate the probability of the two-step cascade (Upper $ightarrow$ Lower $ightarrow$ Ground), analyzing correlations and fluctuations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a theoretical study of a semiconductor photon-pair source based on a radiative cascade enabled by a permanent exciton dipole. The source consists of a GaAs quantum dot placed between a metal nanoparticle and a mirror. The dot exciton and the localized electromagnetic mode mix to form upper and lower polaritons. Without a permanent dipole, a symmetry separating states with even and odd excitation numbers forbids photon emission between the polaritons. Separation of the mean electron and hole positions gives the exciton a permanent dipole and breaks this symmetry. The upper polariton can then emit an idler photon as it decays to the lower polariton. The lower polariton can emit a signal photon as the system returns to its ground state. Using an effective model, we calculate the probability that a prepared upper polariton emits both photons through the metal antenna. We also analyse fluctuations in the emitted photon counts and correlations between the two emission channels. The calculation establishes an operating principle for assumed emitter properties and optical loss rates. Whether the device can be built and its photon correlations measured remains open.

</details>

**Summary.** This research develops a method for precisely controlling spin reversal in photonic interface states within a Kagome lattice structure. By analyzing the coupling mechanisms, the authors determined that a specific sublattice pattern ($1:1:-2$) is required to overcome inherent symmetries that cause coupling cancellation. The findings provide quantitative electromagnetic design targets for realizing such spin-dependent quantum control in metasurface-coupled systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it merges concepts from topological photonics, open quantum systems (Lindblad dynamics), and structured light-matter interaction, providing concrete, quantifiable design rules for controlling quantum states in engineered photonic environments.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of achieving sublattice-selective control for spin reversal in photonic interface states, overcoming limitations where uniform control fields fail to couple opposite-spin, same-valley modes.

**Main result.** An optimal sublattice pattern, specifically $1:1:-2$, was identified to maximize the projected coupling, achieving a high target-mode fidelity of $0.999937$ in full-zone topology calculations.

**Method.** The study combines theoretical analysis of lattice Hamiltonians with numerical simulations, including full-zone topology calculations and an exploratory 3D supercell model.

**Model / system.** The system is a two-component Kagome lattice motivated by photon-mediated exchange near a nonlinear nonlocal metasurface. The dynamics are governed by an effective Hamiltonian and analyzed using projected Rabi amplitudes.

**Key observables.** Target-mode fidelity, leakage probability, projected Rabi amplitude ($\Omega_e$), and spin populations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** RMS drive amplitude ($0.04t$), sublattice pattern coefficients ($1, 1, -2$), and the lattice hopping terms ($t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derived control principle provides quantitative electromagnetic design targets, but the full spin-dependent device realization requires further calibration and assumptions about material constants are made in the EM tests.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the electric field magnitude, Raman-product phase, coherent exchange, and decay across the lattice structure in an exploratory COMSOL supercell.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the fundamental coupling limitations of uniform control to deriving the optimal sublattice pattern ($1:1:-2$), validating this pattern through full-zone topology calculations, and finally demonstrating the principle using an exploratory 3D supercell simulation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Coherent manipulation of photonic interface states requires a control field that matches their internal mode structure. We study this requirement in a two-component Kagome lattice motivated by photon-mediated exchange near a nonlinear nonlocal metasurface. The Dirac spinors show why uniform Raman control cannot couple opposite-spin, same-valley interface modes at leading order, even when their spatial envelopes coincide. A $1:1:-2$ sublattice pattern removes this cancellation and maximizes the projected coupling at fixed root-mean-square amplitude within the diagonal-control class. We test this control scheme through full-zone topology and complete lattice propagation. For a smooth, gapped interface, a $720$-dimensional calculation gives target-mode fidelity $0.999937$ with leakage $6.27\times10^{-5}$ at RMS drive $0.04t$. Trace-preserving dynamics gives the separate survival condition needed for successful conversion. An exploratory three-dimensional lithium-niobate supercell reproduces the complex addressing pattern with $0.373\%$ relative error and provides nonlocal exchange, decay and electro-optic frequency-conversion matrices. The mode-resolved control principle thus gives quantitative electromagnetic design targets; the full spin-dependent device realization still requires further calibration.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper solves Hilbert's sixth problem by showing that statistical probability is not an added postulate but an emergent property. It derives the reduced dynamics for open quantum systems using exact methods, demonstrating that irreversible thermalization occurs naturally when the environment spectrum is continuous. This establishes a dynamical link between unitary quantum mechanics and classical statistical thermodynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work offers a rigorous, non-equilibrium quantum mechanical derivation of thermalization and statistical mechanics, which is a foundational problem in open quantum systems and quantum thermodynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses Hilbert's sixth problem: deriving irreversible statistical probability and thermodynamics from the fundamentally reversible, deterministic dynamics of a closed quantum system.

**Main result.** It provides an exact dynamical route showing that probability emerges as the objective statistical structure of a subsystem when coupled to an environment, leading to thermalization in the continuum limit.

**Method.** The methodology involves deriving the exact reduced density operator by performing a partial trace over the environment states, utilizing generalized fluctuation-dissipation theorems.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a composite of a system (s) and an environment (e), governed by a quadratic Hamiltonian ($H_{tot} = H_s + H_e + H_{se}$) for bosonic or fermionic excitations.

**Key observables.** Reduced density operator ($ho_s(t)$), entanglement entropy, and the system's approach to a thermal Gibbs state ($ho_g$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The spectral density of the environment (continuous vs. gapped), and the limit where the number of environmental degrees of freedom tends to infinity.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation is exact for quadratic bosonic or fermionic systems coupled bilinearly; it explicitly removes the assumption of an initially thermal environment.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds a dynamical chain: starting from a pure, deterministic composite state, unitary evolution generates entanglement, leading to an exact, non-unitary reduced dynamics that approaches a thermal Gibbs state in the continuum limit, thus generating statistical probability.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hilbert's sixth problem placed probability and mechanics at the center of the axiomatization of physics. It asks how irreversible statistical probability and thermodynamics can produce from the reversible, deterministic dynamics of a system. Building on an exact open quantum system theory developed in the past two decades, we construct a dynamical route from the reversible quantum dynamics to reduced statistics. For quadratic bosonic and fermionic systems bilinearly coupled to arbitrary environments, we derive the exact reduced density operator determined completely by a dissipative propagator u and a fluctuation correlation function v of the system. For continuous spectral densities with no bound-state pole, u vanishes in the long-time limit, information about the system initial states is lost completely, and the reduced density operator of systems approaches a thermal Gibbs state. Gapped spectral densities with localized bound states instead preserve long-time memory and prevent thermalization. We then remove the usual assumption of an initially thermal, mixed environment, start from a product of pure states for the system and environment, unitary evolution first generates system-environment entanglement and a periodic quasi-Gibbs reduced state for a finite environment. When the number of environmental degrees of freedom tends to infinity, the spectrum becomes continuous, the recurrence disappears, the exact reduced density operator converges to a thermal Gibbs state. The total entropy remains zero, whereas the system entropy becomes entanglement entropy and reaches its maximum. Probability is therefore not an additional random postulate imposed on unitary dynamics, it is the objective statistical structure of an open system in a composite. These results provide an exact, testable dynamical solution of the determinism to statistics.

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides a rigorous, exact theoretical framework for analyzing nonlinear active microrheology in single-file systems. By employing single-file duality, the authors solve for the force-displacement relationship and the density profile under arbitrary driving forces. This advances the understanding of non-equilibrium statistical mechanics in confined, driven matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical framework developed for non-equilibrium transport in confined geometries is highly relevant to understanding driven many-body systems, which share mathematical parallels with quantum many-body dynamics in engineered potentials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to determine the exact nonlinear response ($\xi(F)$) and the full density profile for a tracer driven by an arbitrary constant force ($F$) in diffusive single-file systems.

**Main result.** The authors developed a closed exact boundary-value problem using single-file duality, successfully determining the force-response relation and showing that the bath-density dipole is fixed by the applied force independently of the interaction potential.

**Method.** The core methodology involves transforming the complex moving-boundary problem into a fixed-boundary diffusion problem via single-file duality, allowing the use of advanced fluctuation theory and self-similar solutions.

**Model / system.** The system models diffusive single-file transport, applicable to molecular transport in zeolites or colloidal particles in narrow channels, described by a fluctuating hydrodynamic equation for the line-density $ho(x, t)$.

**Key observables.** Mean tracer displacement amplitude $\xi(F)$, the full bath density profile $\langleho(x, t)angle$, and the bath-density dipole moment.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Applied constant force $F$, time $t$ (leading to $\sqrt{t}$ scaling), and the system's equilibrium equation of state.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the hydrodynamic limit (long times) and the ability to map the local drive onto a pressure mismatch across the tracer.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the transformation from the moving-boundary problem to a fixed-boundary diffusion problem using single-file duality. Figure 2 compares predictions for mean tracer displacement and density profile against simulations for various single-file models.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the hydrodynamic framework, then introducing single-file duality to convert the problem into a fixed-boundary diffusion problem. It solves this problem to find the mean response ($\xi(F)$) and density profile, and finally extends the formalism to calculate the full probability distribution via fluctuation theory.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Active microrheology probes a crowded medium by forcing a tracer and measuring its response. In single-file transport, where particles cannot overtake, a constant force $F$ produces a subballistic displacement $\langle X_t\rangle\simeq \sqrt{t}\,ξ(F)$ together with a persistent bath deformation. Despite decades of work, the exact nonlinear response at arbitrary force has remained confined to a few special solvable models. Here we remove this restriction by combining recent advances in hydrodynamic transport coefficients, a pressure-balance formulation of the local drive, and single-file duality, which eliminates the resulting moving boundary. This yields a closed exact boundary-value problem for general diffusive single files, determining both $ξ(F)$ and the full density profile. For overdamped Brownian particles with general interactions, all microscopic interaction details enter only through the equilibrium equation of state, bringing realistic interacting systems, including finite-width quasi-one-dimensional channels, within exact reach. The solution also reveals universal global laws; in particular, the bath-density dipole is fixed by the applied force independently of the interaction potential.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes current fluctuations in diffusive systems on a ring containing a battery, using Macroscopic Fluctuation Theory. The authors find a surprising mathematical equivalence between the fluctuation statistics of non-interacting particles and those of the SSEP under certain conditions. The research provides detailed comparisons between theoretical predictions and long-time numerical simulations for these non-equilibrium transport phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study bridges classical non-equilibrium statistical mechanics (MFT) with particle exclusion processes (SSEP), providing deep insights into how boundary conditions and particle interactions affect current fluctuations in mesoscopic systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the large deviation function and fluctuation statistics of the current in diffusive systems subjected to a localized non-conservative force (a battery).

**Main result.** The large deviation function for non-interacting particles on a ring surprisingly matches the form found for the Symmetric Simple Exclusion Process (SSEP) with open boundary conditions.

**Method.** The authors employ Macroscopic Fluctuation Theory (MFT) for analytical predictions and complement this with direct exact microscopic calculations and extensive Monte Carlo simulations.

**Model / system.** The system is a diffusive particle model on a ring containing a battery at the origin. Models include non-interacting particles and the SSEP, which are analyzed under steady-state conditions.

**Key observables.** Large deviation function of the current, average occupations, density profiles, and the second cumulant of the integrated current.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The particle density ($ar{ho}$), the battery strength ($G, H$), and the system size ($L$) are key parameters explored across different regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** MFT relies on assumptions about the optimal profile being time-independent, and the analysis compares results derived from different theoretical limits (e.g., non-interacting vs. SSEP).

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the average current and the second cumulant of the current (MC vs. MFT) as a function of density, notably highlighting a specific value of 1/3 at half-filling for the SSEP.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the framework using MFT for current fluctuations, then deriving specific results for non-interacting particles, and finally comparing these findings and the second cumulant analysis for the SSEP against long Monte Carlo simulations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

For a diffusive system on a ring, with a battery at the origin, we use the macroscopic fluctuation theory (MFT) to obtain the large deviation function of the current. In the case of non-interacting particles, the predictions of the MFT agree with the result of a direct exact microscopic calculation. To our surprise, the large deviation function of non-interacting particles with a battery has the same form as for the Symmetric Simple Exclusion Process (SSEP) with open boundary conditions. For the SSEP on a ring with a battery, the predictions of the MFT seem to agree with the results of long Monte Carlo simulations, although much longer simulations would be needed to better test the validity of these predictions.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper examines chaos in an open quantum system of coupled spins, linking classical dynamics to quantum spectral properties. It demonstrates that quantum signatures of chaos, such as random-matrix statistics, can appear during the transient approach to a stable fixed point, rather than requiring the classical system to possess a fully chaotic attractor. This refines existing conjectures regarding quantum-classical correspondence in dissipative environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the quantum-classical correspondence in open quantum systems, providing a mechanism to detect quantum chaotic signatures even when the classical dynamics settle into a stable, non-chaotic steady state.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the connection between classical and quantum chaos in a dissipative system, specifically exploring if quantum chaos signatures can arise from transient classical chaos rather than requiring a fully chaotic asymptotic attractor.

**Main result.** Liouvillian quantum chaos can be associated with transient classical chaos, suggesting a refinement of the Grobe–Haake–Sommers conjecture.

**Method.** The study employs both classical stability analysis (analyzing fixed points and relaxation times) and quantum spectral analysis (diagonalizing the Liouvillian superoperator and examining spectral statistics).

**Model / system.** The system is a quantum many-body model of two coupled collective spins, whose dynamics are governed by a Lindblad master equation. The classical limit is obtained by analyzing the expectation values of the spin operators.

**Key observables.** Liouvillian spectrum (eigenvalues), random-matrix statistics ($ho_{\lambda}$), expectation values of physical observables, and the standard deviations of the relaxation time ($\sigma_{ho}, \sigma_{\epsilon}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dissipation strength ($\Gamma$) relative to a critical bifurcation point ($\Gamma_c$), and the initial distance from the fixed point ($\delta_{	ext{initial}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The classical analysis relies on the mean-field approximation, and the quantum analysis focuses on restricting spectral statistics to regions with significant observable expectation values.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of magnetization for both chaotic and stable regimes, and show the dependence of spectral statistics and the number of contributing eigenmodes on the dissipation strength $\Gamma$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the classical dynamics and identifying regimes (chaotic vs. stable fixed point). It then maps these classical behaviors onto quantum diagnostics, showing the emergence of random-matrix statistics and changes in observable expectation values in the Liouvillian spectrum, particularly in the transient regime.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the connection between classical and quantum chaos in a dissipative system of two coupled collective spins. Although the classical dynamics possesses a stable fixed-point attractor, we find pronounced signatures of chaos in the transient dynamics preceding relaxation. In the quantum model, such transient chaos is accompanied by a transition to random-matrix statistics in the relevant part of the Liouvillian spectrum and by a qualitative increase in the number of Liouvillian modes contributing to expectation values of physical observables. The transition occurs before the bifurcation at which the stable fixed point disappears and a chaotic attractor emerges. Our results show that Liouvillian quantum chaos can be associated with transient classical chaos, rather than requiring a chaotic asymptotic attractor, and provide a refinement of the Grobe-Haake-Sommers conjecture.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the complex dynamics of active fluid membranes, showing how they achieve emergent symmetry at large scales while exhibiting rich pattern formation at intermediate length scales. The dynamics are governed by a modified hydrodynamic equation, which predicts instabilities driven by active stresses. The work details how nonlinearity can stabilize or destroy these patterned states depending on its magnitude.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of non-equilibrium pattern formation and symmetry breaking in active matter systems provides theoretical frameworks applicable to complex many-body dynamics, which shares conceptual links with driven quantum many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the non-equilibrium dynamics, emergent symmetries, instabilities, and pattern formation in active fluid membranes.

**Main result.** The membrane exhibits emergent flatness and quasi-long-range order at large scales, but active stresses can induce instabilities leading to pattern formation at intermediate scales.

**Method.** The analysis employs hydrodynamic theory, Renormalization Group (RG) techniques, and linear stability analysis applied to the height field equation.

**Model / system.** The system is a nearly flat, inversion-asymmetric, permeable active fluid membrane embedded in a 3D bulk isotropic fluid, modeled by a height field $h(x, t)$.

**Key observables.** Height fluctuations $\langle(\delta h)^2angle$, scaling exponents ($\chi, 	ilde{z}$), and the selection of preferred wavevectors ($q_c$) leading to stripe patterns.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Active hydrodynamic tension ($\zeta_1, \zeta_2$), viscosities ($
u_1, 
u_2$), bending stiffness ($K$), and the nonlinearity parameter ($\lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis utilizes the Stokesian limit (neglecting inertia) and makes approximations regarding the relevance of nonlinear terms in the RG sense.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate steady-state anisotropic patterns under varying parameters, show the stability profile of the dispersion relation $\lambda_h(q)$, and demonstrate the robustness of the patterned phase against initial conditions and the suppression of patterns by increasing $|\lambda|$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the long-wavelength, statistically flat regime (emergent symmetry) to analyzing the intermediate-scale dynamics governed by KPZ-like equations. It then uses linear stability analysis to predict pattern formation at finite wavevectors, concluding with numerical verification of pattern robustness and the role of nonlinearity in suppressing these patterns.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that microscopically inversion-asymmetric, permeable active fluid membranes are statistically flat and effectively inversion-symmetric at large scales. Their fluctuations are governed by an asymptotically exact linear hydrodynamic equation giving orientational long-range order and positional quasi-long-range order. At intermediate scales, their dynamics is described by a Kardar-Parisi-Zhang equation with spatially long-range noise, producing rough membranes with short-range translational and long-range orientational order described by exactly known scaling exponents. Active stresses can destabilize the membrane at long wavelengths, while sufficiently strong active permeation flow can drive finite-wavevector instabilities and pattern formation. These results reveal a novel activity-driven route to the destruction of flat fluid membranes.

</details>

### [Hydrodynamic memory in overdamped colloidal dynamics](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17677v1)

**Authors:** Benjamin Sorkin, Günther Turk, Howard A. Stone  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.17677v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **3/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **2/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **2/5** · `Frenkel-Kontorova` **1/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5** · `scars & prethermalization` **1/5**

Color corresponds to the indicated ϵ values (Eq. (6)), which we chose to correspond to the two experiments in Ref. [23]. The directly adjacent black dashed curves are our late-time prediction, Eq. (9), found from the overdamped Basset-Langevin equation (Eq. (7)). Inset: A log-linear plot of the early-time, primar- ily exponential decay of the positional autocorrelation function. We clearly see the crossover...</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically proves that hydrodynamic memory, characterized by a power-law decay, survives even when simplifying the dynamics to the overdamped (inertialess) limit for colloidal particles. By deriving a closed-form Basset-Langevin equation, the authors provide a more accurate description of particle motion than conventional models. This is crucial for interpreting high-resolution experiments that observe long-time positional correlations.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical fluid dynamics, the rigorous derivation of memory effects in the low-Reynolds number limit provides a powerful theoretical framework for understanding non-Markovian dynamics, which has parallels in open quantum systems and quantum dissipation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fate of power-law hydrodynamic memory in the overdamped (inertialess) limit for colloidal particle dynamics.

**Main result.** The authors derive a closed-form, overdamped Basset-Langevin equation that successfully incorporates hydrodynamic memory, showing it persists even when particle inertia is neglected.

**Method.** The primary method involves performing a small-$\epsilon$ expansion on the full equation of motion, eliminating the velocity variable to derive the final memory-inclusive equation.

**Model / system.** The system is micron-sized colloid particles diffusing through a fluid, modeled using Langevin dynamics that accounts for Stokes drag, added mass, and a power-law Basset memory term.

**Key observables.** Velocity autocorrelation function (expected power-law decay $\sim t^{-3/2}$), Mean-squared displacement (MSD), and positional autocorrelation functions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionless parameter $\epsilon$ controls the crossover between early-time (exponential) and late-time (power-law) dynamics; smaller particles enhance memory effects.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system operates in the low-Reynolds-number, incompressible flow limit, and the standard overdamped model is insufficient as it ignores power-law memory.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the hierarchy of time scales, showing how the theory incorporating Basset memory predicts a crossover from conventional exponential relaxation to memory-dominated power-law decay.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from the full underdamped equation to the overdamped limit via small-$\epsilon$ expansion, deriving the final Basset-Langevin equation, and then applying this theory to predict measurable long-time correlations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Micron-sized colloid particles diffusing through a fluid experience hydrodynamic inertial and memory effects, the latter causing velocity autocorrelation to decay as a power law. At the same time, many theoretical descriptions treat colloidal diffusion in a fluid using overdamped Langevin dynamics for its convenience, eliminating velocity, omitting inertia, but also ignoring the power-law memory. In this Letter, we show that hydrodynamic memory survives in the overdamped (colloid-inertialess) limit. By identifying the dimensionless parameter controlling the crossover between early- and late-time dynamics, we derive in closed form the overdamped Langevin equation in the presence of hydrodynamic memory. This provides a theoretical framework for realistically describing colloidal dynamics in a fluid, and establishes a rigorous basis for the positional memory observed in high-resolution experiments. Our theory predicts that hydrodynamic memory becomes increasingly pronounced for smaller particles and under stronger external forcing, and offers experimental probes for the crossover from conventional exponential relaxation to memory-dominated power-law dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This work characterizes the recovery dynamics of gated SPADs by sweeping the gate frequency, revealing that the recovery time constant systematically decreases as detection efficiency increases. The authors developed a sophisticated model that successfully accounts for both the exponential recharge process and parasitic capacitive modulations in the count rate. This provides crucial, quantitative data for optimizing SPAD performance in quantum key distribution systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core physics is semiconductor device characterization, the rigorous application of time-domain modeling (exponential recovery) and advanced statistical fitting (AIC/BIC model selection) to extract fundamental physical constants ($	au_{rec}$) from complex, rate-limited measurements is relevant to open quantum systems modeling.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of characterizing the recovery dynamics of Single Photon Avalanche Detectors (SPADs) when operated in a gated mode, as this recovery process limits the achievable count rates in practical quantum communication systems.

**Main result.** The recovery time constant ($	au_{rec}$) was found to decrease linearly with increasing detection efficiency ($\eta$), a dependence that contrasts with previous assumptions for free-running detectors. The analysis also resolved a periodic modulation in the count rate attributed to capacitive gate feedthrough.

**Method.** A novel frequency-sweep method using continuous-wave illumination was employed to globally fit count rates across multiple dead times, avoiding the need for high-speed pulsed lasers or time-to-digital converters.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an InGaAs/InP SPAD operated in a gated mode. The analysis uses an exponential recovery model ($\eta(t)$) and incorporates a 'Full Model' that accounts for discrete re-arming and capacitive ripple effects beyond simple dead-time limitations.

**Key observables.** Count rate $C(f)$ as a function of gate frequency $f$, and the recovery time constant $	au_{rec}$ as a function of detection efficiency $\eta$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Recovery time constant ($	au_{rec}$), Detection Efficiency ($\eta$), Gate Frequency ($f$), and modulation parameters ($a, f_0, \phi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The detection efficiency recovers exponentially toward a steady-state value ($\eta_0$), and the analysis relies on fitting count rates across multiple dead times using a global least-squares approach.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the exponential recovery profile, the dependence of $	au_{rec}$ on $\eta$, and the count rate modulation curves compared between baseline and full models.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of gated recovery limits, develops and compares theoretical models (Baseline vs. Full Model) incorporating recharge dynamics and ripple effects, presents the frequency-sweep measurement methodology, and concludes with quantitative results on $	au_{rec}(\eta)$ and modulation analysis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Detection efficiency in gated-mode single-photon avalanche detectors recovers gradually after each gate edge, limiting attainable count rates, yet quantitative recovery models exist only for free-running operation. We extract the gated recovery time constant of a free-running InGaAs/InP detector at 1550 nm by sweeping the gate frequency from 100 kHz to 1 MHz and globally fitting count rates across four dead times, requiring neither a pulsed laser nor a time-to-digital converter. The recovery time decreases linearly with detection efficiency, from 301 to 161 ns between 10 % and 25 %, at 9.3 ns per percentage point. We further resolve a periodic count-rate modulation consistent with bias-dependent capacitive gate feedthrough.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces the Flag Game, a toy model to study how AI agents form and update collective beliefs based on private data and social evidence. The authors demonstrate that collective behavior shifts from collapse to polarization as population size increases, developing both attribution techniques and a statistical mechanical theory to mechanistically understand these emergent social dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the subject is AI/social science, the mathematical tools—phase diagrams, mean-field approximations, and analyzing emergent collective states—are directly analogous to modeling phase transitions in complex physical systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the mechanisms of collective belief formation and coordination in AI agents, which is framed as a problem of emergent collective behavior.

**Main result.** The Flag Game exhibits a shift from collective belief collapse at small populations to collective belief polarization at large populations, which can be modeled using statistical mechanical theory.

**Method.** The authors combine empirical simulation (using the Flag Game) with theoretical development, introducing social circuit attribution and deriving a statistical mechanical theory for large populations.

**Model / system.** The Flag Game is a toy model where bounded agents observe private evidence (a 'crop') of a hidden ground truth (flag) and update beliefs through communication protocols (pairwise, broadcast, manager).

**Key observables.** Collective mean accuracy, belief polarization vs. collapse, social uplift ($\Delta_{	ext{social}}$), and the social circuit attribution score ($S_i$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Population size ($N$), social-evidence uptake ($\alpha$), communication protocol, and the relative weight given to private vs. social evidence.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The Flag Game is a simplified model for mechanistic interpretability, and the statistical mechanical theory is developed to handle the limitations of causal interventions in large populations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Flag Game setup, performance sweeps under various controls (population size, prompting), and the theoretical phase diagram matching empirical results.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the Flag Game, details the empirical testing across various protocols and controls, develops the social circuit attribution technique, and culminates in deriving and verifying a statistical mechanical theory for large-scale belief dynamics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Emergent coordinated behaviors of AI agents are starting to present critical safety risks. A key phenomenon driving these behaviors is the rapid formation and spread of beliefs about the world, and mechanistic understanding is crucial for collective alignment. To this end, we introduce the Flag Game, a toy model for studying the mechanisms of collective belief formation. Concretely, a hidden country flag defines the ground truth, and each bounded agent directly observes only a private crop but can exchange beliefs and weigh social evidence from peers. Despite its simplicity, the Flag Game reproduces rich collective phenomenology: non-monotonic scaling of performance with population size, accuracy gains from social-awareness prompting and team diversity, and strong effects of organizational structure. In particular, we identify that collective belief collapse at small population sizes turns into collective belief polarization as the population grows. This polarization causes the performance decline at large population sizes, but creates diversity in collective beliefs. Finally, we dissect the mechanisms underlying collective belief collapse and polarization with two complementary approaches. We first introduce social circuit attribution, a technique to predict which agent, and what view, matters most to collective dynamics, and verify its predictions by causal interventions on agents, tracing how agent patching changes collective outcomes. However, the efficacy of causal interventions on agents decreases as the population grows. We therefore develop a statistical mechanical theory for larger populations and verify that it matches the empirical phase diagram. Together, these results take a first step toward mechanistic swarm interpretability, a science of how the properties of individual agents and their communication give rise to emergent collective behavior.

</details>

**Authors:** Jayashish Das, Filiberto Ares, Arnab Kundu

The driving periods are chosen as (T0/L, T1/L) = (1, cosh(2θ)/2). The Floquet dynamics produces persistent crossings of the two asymmetry curves.</sub>

**Summary.** This work develops a geometric theory describing entanglement asymmetry in driven 2D CFTs by mapping the dynamics onto the iteration of conformal maps. It shows that phenomena like the quantum Mpemba effect are dictated by the fixed-point structure of these maps. This framework offers a universal tool for understanding non-equilibrium quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful geometric framework (conformal mapping) to organize complex non-equilibrium phenomena like symmetry restoration and the Mpemba effect in periodically driven quantum systems, which is highly relevant to Floquet engineering.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the dynamics of entanglement asymmetry in periodically driven two-dimensional conformal field theories, aiming to find a geometric description for non-equilibrium phenomena.

**Main result.** The dynamics of entanglement asymmetry, including the quantum Mpemba effect, are shown to be governed by the invariant geometry of the underlying conformal map (Möbius map).

**Method.** The analysis uses a dynamical-system description based on iterated conformal maps and relates the entanglement asymmetry to the relative positions of the map's fixed points and the subsystem endpoints.

**Model / system.** The system is a 2D CFT with a U(1) symmetry subjected to periodic driving (Floquet evolution). The dynamics are mapped onto the iteration of Möbius maps, particularly for the SL(2, R) subclass.

**Key observables.** Entanglement Asymmetry ($\Delta S_A$), local symmetry restoration, and the quantum Mpemba effect.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relative positions of the state-preparing operator insertion, the subsystem, and the fixed points of the conformal map.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping the dynamics to conformal maps and uses perturbative expansions in the coupling constant $\kappa$ for the leading-order results.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the evolution of $\Delta S_A(N)$ for hyperbolic and parabolic map classes, showing Mpemba/inverse Mpemba effects. Figure 2 shows persistent crossings of $\Delta S_A(N)$ curves in the non-heating phase, indicating recurrent oscillations.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the link between entanglement asymmetry and conformal maps, analyzes the asymptotic behavior in different fixed-point regimes (heating/phase transition), and finally examines the non-heating phase to demonstrate persistent, non-relaxing oscillations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the dynamics of entanglement asymmetry in periodically driven two-dimensional conformal field theories with a global U$(1)$ symmetry, using a dynamical-system description based on iterated conformal maps, in particular Möbius maps. Starting from a symmetry-breaking excited state prepared by a local operator insertion, we show that a broad range of nonequilibrium phenomena, including local symmetry restoration, the quantum Mpemba effect, and its inverse, admit a simple geometric description in terms of the invariant data of the conformal map. We explicitly demonstrate this using Möbius maps, for which the invariant structure is described by the conjugacy classes. In particular, the dynamics of entanglement asymmetry is determined by the relative positions of the state-preparing operator insertion, the subsystem, and the fixed points, yielding distinct patterns of growth, decay, oscillation, and saturation. We further argue that this geometric picture extends to general holomorphic maps, establishing the conformal-map dynamics as a natural framework for organizing the phenomenology of entanglement asymmetry in periodically driven two-dimensional conformal field theories.

</details>

**Summary.** This review analyzes heat transport in 1D lattices with long-range interactions, showing that the decay rate of these interactions ($\sigma$) dictates the transport regime. The findings reveal complex transitions between superdiffusion, normal diffusion, and subdiffusion, suggesting that LR coupling offers a tunable mechanism for controlling heat flow, potentially leading to thermal diodes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it explores non-equilibrium dynamics and energy transport in complex many-body systems, touching upon concepts of non-integrability and anomalous diffusion that are central to modern condensed matter theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how long-range (LR) interactions fundamentally alter the mechanisms of heat transport in one-dimensional (1D) lattice systems, moving beyond established models for short-range interactions.

**Main result.** The transport regime is highly dependent on the LR decay exponent ($\sigma$): strong LR coupling leads to subdiffusion, while specific weak-LR regimes can approach normal diffusion or even ballistic transport.

**Method.** The study employs theoretical analysis and numerical simulations using non-equilibrium molecular dynamics (NEMD) and the Green-Kubo formalism to calculate thermal conductivity and energy current correlations.

**Model / system.** The model is a 1D Fermi-Pasta-Ulam-Tsingou (FPUT)-type lattice whose anharmonic coupling terms decay as a power law, $V(r) \propto 1/r^\sigma$. The focus is on the effects of this long-range, quartic anharmonicity.

**Key observables.** Thermal conductivity ($\kappa$), energy current autocorrelation function ($C_{JJ}(t)$), and spatiotemporal correlation functions of energy fluctuations ($ho_Q(m, t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The LR decay exponent ($\sigma$), the system size ($N$), and the temperature/nonlinearity strength.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on comparing results from NEMD, Green-Kubo, and correlation function scaling, while acknowledging the challenge of ensemble inequivalence in LR systems.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the temperature profile, the scaling of spatiotemporal correlations ($ho_Q(m, t)$) showing different growth rates (diffusive vs. ballistic), and the energy current autocorrelation function exhibiting negative correlations in certain regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews the theoretical framework, contrasting short-range vs. LR effects, systematically analyzes transport regimes based on $\sigma$ (e.g., $\sigma=2$, $1 < \sigma \le 3$, $0 \le \sigma \le 1$), and discusses the implications for thermal control.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Long-range (LR) interactions are no longer just a theoretical idea: they can be engineered in several low-dimensional platforms and offer a new way to control heat transport. At the same time, they push beyond the standard picture developed mainly for short-range, momentum-conserving lattices. In this review, we consider one-dimensional Fermi-Pasta-Ulam-Tsingou (FPUT)-type lattices where LR effects enter mainly through a quartic anharmonic coupling that decays as a power law, characterized by an exponent σ. Our goal is to explain, in a unified way, how LR anharmonicity changes microscopic energy exchange, collective dynamics, and the macroscopic scaling laws of heat conduction-while also clarifying what is well established and what is still debated (For more details of the abstract, please see the main text and paper).

</details>

**Summary.** This review surveys advanced computational techniques for calculating kinetic rates and the committor function in molecular dynamics simulations. It emphasizes modern, self-supervised machine learning approaches that solve the underlying partial differential equations governing these transitions. The work provides a unified theoretical framework connecting classical statistical mechanics tools with deep learning methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of self-supervised ML methods to solve fundamental equations of non-equilibrium statistical mechanics (like the committor PDE) is highly relevant for modeling open quantum systems and dynamics where full state space exploration is impossible.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of estimating kinetic statistics, particularly the committor, for molecular transitions that occur on timescales inaccessible to direct molecular dynamics simulations.

**Main result.** It surveys modern, self-supervised methods that connect various mathematical frameworks—including PDEs, variational principles, and neural networks—to estimate these kinetic parameters without relying on labeled simulation data.

**Method.** The core methodology involves reformulating the committor calculation as solving a defining partial differential equation (PDE) or minimizing a related functional, which is then tackled using Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) or variational approaches.

**Model / system.** The focus is on molecular transitions in general, modeled using molecular dynamics simulations. The underlying dynamics are often described by the infinitesimal generator $L$ of the process, applicable to systems undergoing Langevin dynamics.

**Key observables.** The committor, $q(x)$, which is the probability of reaching a product state before a reactant state; rate constants ($k$) derived from this statistic.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The timescale gap between simulation limits and physical rates; the dimensionality of the configuration space.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The review discusses approximations such as the Markovian assumption and the use of the steady-state solution to the backward Kolmogorov equation.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the physical problem (committor estimation) and reviewing traditional methods (shooting, regression). It then systematically builds the modern theoretical framework by connecting generator-based PDEs, variational principles, MSMs, and finally showing how machine learning (NNs) provides a unified, self-supervised approach.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Most molecular transitions occur on timescales far beyond direct molecular dynamics simulations. The committor, the probability that a configuration reaches a product state before a reactant state, is a central kinetic statistic, providing a mechanism-independent reaction coordinate and a foundation for transition path theory and the calculation of rates. This review surveys modern approaches for estimating the committor and related kinetic statistics from molecular simulations, with an emphasis on self-supervised methods that learn solutions of their defining dynamical equations rather than relying on labeled shooting data. We develop a common operator viewpoint connecting generator-based partial differential equations, variational principles, Markov state models, dynamical Galerkin approximation, and neural networks. Empirical and theoretical evidence points to the efficiency of these methods. We provide theoretical and practical guidance for realizing their full potential in applications, including strategies for treating non-Markovian effects and for sampling. We conclude by identifying opportunities for further research, including connections to reinforcement learning and generative modeling.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a theory-agnostic method to certify nonclassicality using only experimental statistics from an integrated photonic circuit. By combining theory-agnostic tomography with simplex-embeddability certification, the authors robustly demonstrate that the measured correlations cannot be explained by any classical physical model. This advances the field by providing a general, device-independent verification tool for quantum advantage in scalable photonic platforms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, general mathematical tool for verifying quantum resources directly from measurements, which is highly relevant for assessing the performance and fundamental limits of photonic quantum hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the foundational challenge of certifying nonclassicality in a manner that is independent of any specific underlying physical theory, which is vital for device-independent quantum protocols.

**Main result.** The authors successfully applied a theory-agnostic certification method to experimental data from a three-mode integrated photonic circuit, providing robust evidence of nonclassicality by ruling out simplex-embeddability.

**Method.** The core method combines theory-agnostic tomography with simplex-embeddability certification, which analyzes experimental statistics to determine the minimal mathematical structure (rank) required to explain the observed correlations.

**Model / system.** The experiment utilizes a three-mode integrated photonic circuit, operating in a prepare-and-measure scenario, where quantum correlations are generated and measured using single photons.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the conditional probability distribution $p(a|y, x)$, and the diagnostic tools are the optimal factorization rank ($k^*$) and the robustness of contextuality ($r$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis depends on the measured coincidence counts ($N$) and the independently controlled voltages ($\Delta V_x, \Delta V_y$) used to tune the photonic circuit settings.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method's strength lies in its minimal assumptions, but limitations include the fact that the reconstructed fragments are 'GPT shadows' and the complexity of polytope representation growth.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the conceptual setup (prepare-and-measure), the measured/modeled probabilities as functions of applied voltages, and comparisons of robustness ($r$) and $\chi^2$ values for classical versus theoretical models.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory-agnostic framework by defining the optimization problem ($\chi^2$ minimization over rank $k$), applies it to the photonic circuit data, and validates the procedure by comparing results against known classical and theoretical quantum models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Certifying nonclassicality without assuming any particular underlying physical theory constitutes both a foundational challenge and a requirement for device-independent protocols. Realizing such theory-independent certification in scalable architectures is essential for connecting fundamental tests with emerging quantum technologies. Integrated photonic circuits are a leading platform for scalable quantum information processing, motivating the development of rigorous methods to certify the nonclassical resources underpinning quantum advantage. In this paper, we report a theory-independent certification of generalized contextuality on a three-mode integrated photonic circuit. Our approach combines theory-agnostic tomography with simplex-embeddability certification---a framework that requires no assumptions about the underlying physical theory---and applies it to experimental data from a three-mode photonic circuit seeded by single photons. An independently constructed quantum model of the experiment provides a consistency check on the observed nonclassicality, without entering the certification as a theoretical assumption. The method rules out simplex-embeddability for our experimental data, providing robust, theory-independent evidence that our photonic circuit exhibits nonclassicality.

</details>
