**Summary.** This paper presents a general and computationally efficient method to estimate the entropy difference between any two probability distributions using stochastic interpolants. By reformulating the calculation in terms of the inner product of the probability flow velocity and the score field, it bypasses difficult divergence calculations. This technique is highly valuable for calculating non-equilibrium entropies in complex physical systems, ranging from classical spin models to active matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core focus is on classical statistical mechanics, the reliance on stochastic differential equations and flow modeling connects deeply with modern techniques used in quantum dynamics and open quantum systems, particularly those involving non-equilibrium processes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the general problem of estimating entropy differences between two arbitrary probability distributions ($ho_0$ and $ho_1$) characterizing physical systems.

**Main result.** It derives an efficient formula for the entropy difference ($\Delta S$) based on the inner product of the probability flow velocity ($\mathbf{b}$) and the score field ($\mathbf{s}$), avoiding computationally expensive divergence calculations.

**Method.** The method utilizes stochastic interpolants governed by a continuity equation to define a continuous path between the base and target distributions, allowing the entropy difference to be calculated as a time integral of the expected inner product $\mathbf{b} \cdot \mathbf{s}$.

**Model / system.** The method is general, relying only on the Gibbs-Shannon entropy definition, and has been demonstrated on complex systems including Gaussian mixture models, the classical XY model, Lennard-Jones clusters, and active Brownian polymers.

**Key observables.** Entropy difference ($\Delta S$), Free Energy difference ($\Delta F$), Probability Flow Velocity ($\mathbf{b}$), and Score Field ($\mathbf{s}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The time parameter $t \in [0, 1]$ defining the interpolation path, and system-specific parameters for the tested models (e.g., polymer constants, interaction strengths).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation assumes that the probability distribution $ho_t(x)$ varies smoothly with the interpolation parameter $t$.

**Figures summary.** The notes do not provide a summary of figures, but mention results for several systems (GMM, XY Chain, LJ, Active Polymer) showing good agreement with reference values.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the general framework using stochastic interpolants and the continuity equation to derive the $\Delta S$ formula. It then discusses the computational advantages of the inner product formulation and applies this method to several increasingly complex physical models, including polymer dynamics and lattice models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a general method for estimating entropy differences between arbitrary probability distributions using stochastic interpolants. The marginal distributions which bridge the base and target obey a continuity equation, which allows a straightforward calculation of the entropy difference in terms of an inner product of the probability flow velocity and score fields. This formulation has several advantages: (i) no computationally expensive divergence calculations of either field are required, (ii) the score field need not even be learned directly if model transferability is not required, and (iii) on-the-fly estimates are produced nearly for free during training. When tractable base distributions are chosen (e.g. Gaussian chain, ideal gas, etc.), statistical thermodynamic entropies are then immediately recovered. Notably, the analysis relies only on the definition of the Gibbs-Shannon entropy, rather than any particular statistical mechanical ensemble, so that generalized non-equilibrium entropies may be computed. The method is demonstrated on several systems of increasing complexity: (i) a 40-dimensional Gaussian mixture model, (ii) the classical XY model of N spins arranged in one dimension, (iii) the 13-atom Lennard-Jones cluster, and (iv) an active Brownian polymer.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the challenge of controlling excitation transport in complex quantum systems by reducing the number of necessary control parameters. The authors propose an edge-ranking method based on analyzing the quantum probability current's gradient component. This technique successfully identifies a small subset of couplings that preserves near-maximal transport efficiency while drastically lowering the required external control effort.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, physically motivated diagnostic tool (the probability current gradient) to guide quantum control optimization, moving beyond brute-force optimization by identifying the most critical couplings.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to enhance excitation transport in open quantum networks while drastically reducing the high dimensionality of the required control space.

**Main result.** Edge selection guided by the quantum probability current can substantially reduce the control space, allowing for high transport performance (e.g., 97.60% retention) with significantly lower control effort (e.g., 41.55% reduction in fluence).

**Method.** The authors introduce an edge-ranking strategy based on analyzing the change in the gradient component of the time-integrated quantum probability current, derived using graph Hodge decomposition.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled after excitation transport in photosynthetic protein complexes, specifically using the seven-site Fenna-Matthews-Olson (FMO) model. Dynamics are governed by a Lindblad master equation incorporating coherent evolution and dissipation.

**Key observables.** Transport Efficiency ($\eta(T)$), Pulse Fluence (control effort proxy), Quantum Probability Current (gradient and cycle components).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of sites (e.g., seven for FMO), the degree of dephasing, and the specific set of controllable edges selected.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the sink and ground states are absorbing states, and the ranking criterion relies on the gradient component of the integrated quantum probability current.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the open network model structure (sites, ground, sink). Other figures likely show dephasing dependence and scatter plots comparing efficiency against the norms of the probability current components.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem of high-dimensional control, introduces the quantum probability current and graph Hodge decomposition, develops the edge-ranking strategy based on the gradient component, and validates this strategy using the FMO model results.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Time-dependent coherent control can enhance excitation transport in open quantum networks, but independently controlling every inter-site coupling creates a control space of high dimension and leads to difficult optimization problems. We introduce an edge-ranking strategy based on the control-induced change in the gradient component of the time-integrated quantum probability current, which is obtained via a graph Hodge decomposition. When our strategy is applied to the seven-site Fenna-Matthews-Olson (FMO) model, the six-edge set retains $99.83\%$ of the enhancement achieved by full control, and the four-edge set retains $97.60\%$ while reducing the pulse fluence---used here as a proxy for control effort---by $41.55\%$ relative to full control. Dephasing scans and comparisons with random edge sets and random networks provide numerical support for the relevance and potential broader utility of the ranking. These results show that edge selection guided by the quantum probability current can substantially reduce the control space while preserving high transport performance with lower control effort.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel, mathematically rigorous framework for Wilson's Renormalization Group theory by employing inverse limits of finite systems. It successfully defines a continuous RG map on the space of physical Hamiltonians, thereby avoiding pathologies found in previous rigorous attempts. This provides a powerful new tool to study critical phenomena and fixed points in statistical mechanics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the rigorous mathematical machinery developed—especially the use of functional spaces, continuity proofs, and flow analysis—is highly relevant to understanding the dynamics of open quantum systems or quantum field theories where renormalization is key.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a mathematically rigorous and pathology-free framework for Wilson's Renormalization Group (RG) theory.

**Main result.** The authors establish a continuous RG map on a quotient space of Hamilton functions, proving the framework's mathematical soundness and providing a path to study fixed points.

**Method.** The core method involves using inverse limits of finite systems and defining the RG transformation on the space of physical interactions ($V_{	ext{phys}}$) to ensure mathematical consistency.

**Model / system.** The theory is applied to the Ising model on the hypercubic lattice ($\mathbb{Z}^d$), analyzing the flow of interactions (Hamilton functions) in the infinite volume limit.

**Key observables.** RG flow, fixed points (e.g., $H=0$), critical exponents, and the norm $\lVert [\hat{H}] Vert_{\hat{V}}$ used to measure convergence.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The decay rate of interactions ($\Phi$), the lattice dimension ($d$), and the coupling constants ($eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes the existence of a suitable space $V_{	ext{phys}}$ and relies on proving the continuity of the RG map $R$ on this space.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining block-spin RGTs on finite graphs using inverse limits, establishing the map $R: V_{	ext{phys}} 	o V_{	ext{phys}}$, and finally analyzing the flow properties on the quotient space $\hat{V} = V_{	ext{phys}}/R$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

It has been over fifty years since Kenneth Wilson had his Nobel-prize-winning ideas on the renormalization group. In this time frame, despite many attempts, no mathematical results have implemented Wilson's vision to a satisfactory degree. Although a number of predictions stemming from the renormalization group framework have been proven to date, these proofs usually rely on alternative ideas and do not cover the full predictive power of Wilson's renormalization group. The discovery of pathologies within rigorous implementations of renormalization group transformations have further reduced the enthusiasm of the mathematics community for this subject. This paper presents a novel framework to implement Wilson's original idea in a rigorous manner based on inverse limits of finite systems. Doing so, one avoids these pathologies and hence obtains a mathematically sound framework for Wilson's renormalization group. This opens up a completely new path to tackle some of the existing problems in rigorous renormalization group theory like the existence of non-trivial fixed points.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a unified mathematical framework to calculate the quasinormal and quasibound modes for scalar fields in a weakly magnetized black hole spacetime. It achieves this by transforming the governing differential equation into a confluent-Heun form and defining the spectral condition via a global Wronskian. The method provides a robust, horizon-to-infinity spectral condition that matches established theoretical benchmarks.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core physics is general relativity/field theory, the mathematical machinery—reducing a physical boundary value problem to the zeros of a specific transcendental function (the Wronskian)—is highly relevant to open quantum systems and spectral analysis in other physical contexts.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To provide a unified and rigorous method for determining the quasinormal-mode (QNM) and quasibound-state (QBS) spectra for a massless scalar field in a weakly magnetized Ernst--Schwarzschild spacetime.

**Main result.** The spectral condition is unified by constructing an endpoint-selected spectral Wronskian, which successfully reproduces known weak-field benchmarks for both QNMs and QBSs.

**Method.** The analysis reduces the Klein-Gordon equation to a radial confluent-Heun equation, whose spectral condition is determined by setting the global spectral Wronskian to zero.

**Model / system.** The system is a massless scalar field propagating in a weakly magnetized Ernst--Schwarzschild spacetime. The analysis is restricted to the separated, weak-field approximation ($O(B_0^2)$) of the geometry.

**Key observables.** Quasinormal mode frequencies ($\omega$) and quasibound state frequencies, determined as the zeros of the spectral Wronskian.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field strength ($B_0$), mass ($M$), angular number ($\ell$), and the frequency ($\Omega$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results are strictly limited to the separated $O(B^2_0)$ weak-field model, and the method relies on the global spectral Wronskian condition rather than local polynomial termination.

**Figures summary.** Tables compare calculated QNM frequencies against established benchmarks from the literature for varying magnetic fields and masses.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the weak-field reduction to the confluent-Heun equation, derives the global spectral Wronskian from the recurrence relation, and then applies the zero-finding condition to unify the spectral determination for both radiative (QNM) and bound (QBS) states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the quasinormal-mode and quasibound-state spectra of a massless scalar field in weakly magnetized Ernst--Schwarzschild spacetime. In the separated $O(B_0^2)$ regime, the Klein--Gordon equation reduces to a radial confluent-Heun equation. We construct an endpoint-selected spectral Wronskian directly from the canonical confluent-Heun recurrence and its regular-to-irregular connection limit. Quasinormal modes and quasibound states are then obtained as zeros of the same analytic spectral function on the radiative and bound sheets, respectively, providing a unified treatment of the two spectral sectors within the separated weak-field problem. The resulting QNM and QBS frequencies agree with independent weak-field Ernst and massive-Schwarzschild benchmarks available in the literature. We further show, for representative cases, that the frequencies selected by the local Heun-series termination relation examined here do not satisfy the endpoint-selected quasibound-state condition. The construction therefore provides a direct horizon-to-infinity spectral condition for both radiative and bound states within the weak-field Ernst problem.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work explores unconventional linear transverse exciton transport in valley-layer coupled 2D materials. By using an out-of-plane electric field ($E_{\perp}$), the authors demonstrate control over exciton dispersion anisotropy, which generates measurable transverse currents. This provides a versatile platform for engineering excitonic phenomena in next-generation quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to use an external electric field ($E_{\perp}$) to engineer and control specific, symmetry-protected transport channels (like anisotropy-induced currents) in excitonic systems is highly relevant for designing quantum light sources or detectors.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates unconventional linear transverse exciton transport (LTET) in valley-layer coupling (VLC) two-dimensional (2D) materials.

**Main result.** The perpendicular electric field ($E_{\perp}$) acts as a powerful knob to control exciton-dispersion anisotropy, thereby inducing and tuning LTET in both monolayer and twisted bilayer structures.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis using effective exciton Hamiltonians and semi-classical transport theory to calculate conductivity tensor components.

**Model / system.** The system is VLC 2D materials, prototyped by TiSiCo, which exhibit valley coupling via crystal symmetry. The model extends to both monolayer and twisted bilayer configurations, incorporating interlayer Förster coupling.

**Key observables.** Transverse conductivities ($\sigma_{AS}^{	ext{tra}}, \alpha_{AS}^{	ext{tra}}$) and the competition between anisotropy-induced responses and $\mathcal{T}$-even layer Hall/Nernst counterflow.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Perpendicular electric field ($E_{\perp}$), twist angle ($	heta$), and temperature ($T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on the zero-Berry-curvature limit and assumes the exciton binding energy is omitted for simplicity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the crystal structure, exciton dispersion dependence on $E_{\perp}$, and the calculated dependence of transverse conductivities on temperature, $E_{\perp}$, and twist angle for both monolayer and bilayer systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework for LTET in VLC materials, first analyzing the monolayer response governed by anisotropy, and then extending the analysis to the twisted bilayer where multiple competing transport channels (anisotropy-driven vs. layer counterflow) coexist and are tunable.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Valley-layer coupling (VLC) two-dimensional (2D) materials define a distinct class of quantum systems in which valleys are related by crystal, rather than time-reversal ($\mathcal{T}$) symmetry, enabling gate-controlled valley-contrasted layer polarization. Here we extend this concept to excitons. Using TiSiCo as a prototype VLC material, we show that a perpendicular electric field $E_{\perp}$ controls exciton- dispersion anisotropy and thereby generates unconventional linear transverse exciton transport in both monolayer and twisted bilayer structures. In the monolayer, this response is characterized by anisotropy-induced transverse conductivities, arising from the antisymmetric combination of diagonal elements in the conductivity tensor and strongly tunable by $E_{\perp}$. In the twisted bilayer, symmetry additionally permits transverse responses from the symmetric part of the conductivity tensor. Since intralayer excitons in different layers are connected by the Förster coupling, these symmetric and antisymmetric responses coexist and compete with the recently proposed $\mathcal{T}$-even layer Hall and Nernst exciton counterflow. This interplay is highly tunable by twisted angle, temperature, and the direction of the in-plane driving force, providing a route to disentangle distinct transverse exciton transport signals experimentally. Our results establish $E_{\perp}$ as a powerful knob for controlling exciton transport via VLC and identify VLC 2D materials as a promising platform for engineered excitonic phenomena.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses physics-informed machine learning (Neural ODEs) to solve the holographic inverse problem, reconstructing the geometry of a charged AdS black hole from measured fermionic spectral functions. It successfully maps out the physics across three quantum critical regimes, confirming the utility of ML in extracting deep physical structure from boundary data.

**Why it may be interesting.** The application of advanced ML techniques to solve fundamental problems in strongly correlated electron physics (strange metals) via holography provides a powerful, modern tool for theoretical condensed matter research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper tackles the holographic inverse problem: reconstructing the bulk spacetime and gauge field of a charged AdS black hole directly from boundary fermionic spectral functions.

**Main result.** The physics-informed machine learning framework successfully reconstructs the extremal Reissner-Nordström AdS geometry across three distinct quantum critical regimes, demonstrating the network isolates the IR CFT universality.

**Method.** It employs a Neural Ordinary Differential Equations (Neural ODE) framework, constrained by physical boundary conditions, to solve the inverse problem posed by the holographic Dirac equation.

**Model / system.** The context is holographic condensed matter theory (AdS/CMT), modeling a probe Dirac fermion in the background of a charged AdS black hole, relevant for studying quantum critical points.

**Key observables.** Boundary fermionic spectral function, $A(\omega,k) = rac{1}{\pi} 	ext{Im} G^-(\omega,k)$, and the reconstructed bulk spacetime metrics and gauge fields.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Three quantum critical regimes defined by the $U(1)$ probe charge: non-Fermi liquid, marginal Fermi liquid (strange metal), and Fermi-liquid-like states.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes the validity of the AdS/CFT correspondence and uses hard constraints (UV asymptotics, horizon regularity) within the Neural ODE architecture.

**Figures summary.** The notes describe figures illustrating the reconstruction of the extremal RN geometry and comparing recovered profiles to known analytical solutions, highlighting the detection of geometrical degeneracy.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure reviewing the holographic Dirac equations, establishing benchmark spectral functions, detailing the Neural ODE framework, demonstrating the reconstruction process, and finally exploring the resulting geometrical degeneracy.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a physics-informed machine learning framework based on Neural Ordinary Differential Equations that solves the holographic inverse problem: reconstructing the bulk spacetime and gauge field of a charged AdS black hole directly from boundary fermionic spectral functions. Encoding the UV asymptotics, horizon regularity, and zero temperature extremality as hard constraints in the neural network architecture, our framework reliably reconstructs the extremal Reissner-Nordström AdS geometry across three quantum critical regimes set by the $U(1)$ probe charge---non-Fermi liquid, marginal Fermi liquid (strange metal), and Fermi-liquid-like states---and can jointly infer the probe charge itself to sub-percent accuracy. Relaxing the near-AdS boundary constraint uncovers a geometrical degeneracy: bulk profiles that differ throughout the radial direction but share the same near-horizon $AdS_2 \times \mathbb{R}^2$ data reproduce identical spectral functions near the Fermi surface. This isospectral non-uniqueness is precisely the bulk degeneracy expected on general holographic grounds at zero temperature, and its spontaneous emergence across independent training runs shows that the network isolates the IR CFT universality rather than overfitting a single UV completion.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper establishes a rigorous link between Full Eigenstate Thermalization (full ETH) in QFT and the concept of gravitational scrambling. By employing thermal analyticity and free probability theory, the authors derive quantitative bounds that are shown to be saturated by holographic CFTs. This work provides a unified mathematical framework connecting thermalization, non-commutative probability, and quantum gravity effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, the connection established between thermalization dynamics (ETH) and fundamental algebraic structures (free cumulants, modular theory) provides powerful, non-perturbative tools applicable to understanding thermalization in complex quantum many-body systems, including those relevant to AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates Full Eigenstate Thermalization (full ETH) in Quantum Field Theory and establishes a deep connection between full ETH, free cumulants, and the concept of gravitational scrambling.

**Main result.** The authors derive directional large-frequency bounds for full ETH functions, showing that holographic CFTs saturate these bounds at fourth order, thus linking full ETH to gravitational scrambling.

**Method.** The methodology combines thermal analyticity, free probability theory (using free cumulants), and advanced algebraic techniques from quantum field theory, particularly those related to modular flow and the AdS/CFT correspondence.

**Model / system.** The analysis is conducted in continuum Quantum Field Theories (QFTs), focusing on thermal Conformal Field Theories (CFTs) and utilizing holographic models derived from the AdS/CFT correspondence.

**Key observables.** Full ETH smooth functions, two- and three-point functions in thermal CFTs, fourth-order free cumulants, and the holographic eikonal scattering matrix phase shift.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis examines behavior at low orders (two/three-point functions) and the first appearance of dynamics at fourth order, relating to the asymptotic freeness limit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on the complex-time analyticity of regulated thermal free cumulants and assumes the semiclassical near-horizon regime for the holographic identification.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured logically, first reviewing the algebraic framework for gravitational scrambling, then introducing free probability theory, deriving ETH constraints using analyticity, testing these bounds in CFTs, and concluding with the synthesis of the connection between ETH, free cumulants, and scrambling.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Using thermal analyticity, we derive directional large-frequency bounds on the smooth functions entering full eigenstate thermalization in continuum quantum field theory. The strongest directions reproduce the scales previously identified for lattice systems, while the complete analyticity domain reveals a broader asymmetric multifrequency structure. In thermal two-dimensional conformal field theory, exact two- and three-point functions saturate the corresponding bounds. At fourth order, where genuine dynamical information first appears, we show that the holographic CFTs studied here saturate the strongest full-ETH bounds. These results establish a direct connection between full ETH, free cumulants, and gravitational scrambling, and are consistent with the emergence of asymptotic freeness realized by the gravitational scrambling algebra.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates that generic local chaotic quantum evolution naturally generates intermediate quantum states capable of universal entanglement embezzlement. By analyzing the scaling of entanglement measures like $\eta_{emb}$ and $D_{NL}$ in random quantum circuits, the authors establish a physical pathway for creating these complex, non-equilibrium quantum resources. This links the concept of entanglement catalysis to the dynamics of chaotic quantum many-body systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, non-equilibrium physical mechanism—chaotic unitary evolution—for generating highly structured quantum resources (UEEs), which is crucial for understanding quantum resource theory in many-body physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether generic local chaotic quantum evolution can dynamically generate intermediate quantum states possessing the multiscale entanglement-spectrum structure necessary for universal entanglement embezzlement (UEE).

**Main result.** The authors show that such atypical, embezzling structures emerge generically during the evolution, well before the system reaches thermal equilibrium, and this process is linked to a divergence in nonlocal nonstabilizerness.

**Method.** The analysis involves calculating and comparing entanglement measures, specifically entanglement embezzlement ($\eta_{emb}$) and nonlocal magic ($D_{NL}$), across different time scales of unitary evolution.

**Model / system.** The primary model is a one-dimensional brickwork circuit of $N$ qubits subjected to local unitary evolution generated by independently sampled two-qubit Haar-random unitaries.

**Key observables.** Entanglement Embezzlement ($\eta_{emb}$), Nonlocal Magic ($D_{NL}$), and the multiscale structure of the entanglement spectrum $\{\lambda_x\}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size $N$, time $t$, and the comparison between intermediate, Haar-typical, and late-time scrambling regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the competition between scrambling and typicality creates a distinct, measurable intermediate dynamical regime.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the scaling laws for key quantities ($C_E, \eta_{emb}, D_{NL}$) and the weight distribution ($w_j$) in the Haar-random model, showing characteristic scaling with system size $N$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by defining UEE and nonlocal magic, applies these concepts to the Haar-random circuit model, and then analyzes the scaling behavior of the relevant observables across different dynamical regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that, starting from a product state, local unitary quantum evolutions generate intermediate states which exhibit a multiscale entanglement-spectrum structure required for universal entanglement embezzlement. This facilitates entanglement extraction from a catalyst many-body state while leaving it asymptotically unchanged. Remarkably, these atypical structures emerge generically at intermediate stages, well before reaching maximum entropy where thermalization has flattened out the spectral hierarchy. The resulting state is accompanied by nonlocal nonstabilizerness that diverges with the system size, consistent with a recently established equivalence between universal embezzlement and divergent nonlocal magic. Thus, without any fine tuning, a chaotic quantum evolution generates intermediate states which form a universal family of catalytic reservoirs.

</details>

**Authors:** Mihailo Stojnic

**Summary.** This paper tackles the challenging statistical problem of finding upper bounds for the largest average submatrix value in random matrices. The authors introduce a novel Random Duality Theory (RDT) framework to rigorously characterize these bounds. By developing plain and lifted RDT variants, they provide explicit, improved upper bounds that match established results in the limit of small submatrices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core topic is random matrix theory, the mathematical techniques involving duality, bounding, and analyzing phase transitions in high-dimensional random structures are highly relevant to understanding complexity in quantum information and many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper studies statistical variants of the classical largest average submatrix problem (LASP) for random matrices.

**Main result.** The authors developed a Random Duality Theory (RDT) framework to derive explicit, closed-form upper bounds on the largest average submatrix values, showing improvements over previous bounds.

**Method.** The primary method is the development and application of a generic Random Duality Theory (RDT) framework, comparing plain and lifted variants to establish rigorous upper bounds.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on an $m 	imes n$ matrix $A$ whose elements are independent standard normal random variables. The problem is analyzed in the linear regime where submatrix dimensions are proportional to the original matrix dimensions.

**Key observables.** The largest average submatrix value ($\xi$), scaled by $\sqrt{n}$, which is the primary quantity of interest.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dimensionality proportionality parameters ($\alpha, eta_1, eta_2$) and the regime where these parameters approach zero (small submatrices).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The elements of the matrix $A$ are assumed to be independent standard normal random variables, and the analysis focuses on the asymptotic linear regime.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 compares the theoretical predictions (RDT and Lifted RDT) against algorithmic predictions (LAS) for the largest average submatrix value across different dimensions.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the LASP in the statistical regime, develops the generic RDT framework, applies plain RDT to obtain initial bounds, introduces and utilizes a lifted RDT variant for tighter bounds, and finally validates these results by showing agreement with known replica and sublinear results in the small submatrix limit.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study statistical variants of the classical largest average submatrix problem. For small submatrices (where the dimension is less than linearly proportional to the original matrix), the problem is typically well understood and believed to exhibit the statistical-computational gap (SCG). However, analytical treatment of both the information-theoretic and algorithmic aspects of the linear regime remains challenging, and no mathematically rigorous results have yet arrived anywhere close to proving or disproving SCG existence in this setting.   Focusing on the linear regime, we make strong progress in several key directions: 1) We develop a generic Random Duality Theory (RDT) framework to characterize largest average submatrix values. 2) Using the plain RDT variant, we obtain closed-form upper bounds as explicit functions of dimensionality proportionality parameters. 3) We demonstrate that a lifted RDT variant strictly improves upon the plain RDT within a certain linear range of submatrix dimensions. 4) For small submatrices where the dimensional proportionality constants approach zero, we prove that our results match both the replica results from [34] (obtained via one-step replica symmetry breaking) and the sublinear results from [18,45].

</details>

**Summary.** This paper derives rigorous, directional bounds on the correlation functions characterizing full eigenstate thermalization in QFT. By analyzing the analyticity domain in the complex frequency plane, the authors demonstrate that both CFT correlators and gravitational scattering processes saturate these bounds. This provides strong evidence that holographic models realize the full constraints predicted by ETH in continuum quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a rigorous, continuum field-theoretic method to test the limits of thermalization, which is a central topic in non-equilibrium statistical mechanics and quantum chaos, relevant to understanding thermalization in complex quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To derive directional large-frequency bounds on the smooth functions governing full eigenstate thermalization (full ETH) in continuum quantum field theory (QFT) and holographic systems.

**Main result.** The analysis shows that thermal conformal correlators saturate the second- and third-order bounds, and the leading gravitational Regge contribution saturates the strongest fourth-order direction, confirming ETH constraints in holographic CFTs.

**Method.** The authors use thermal analyticity, deforming contours in the complex plane, and deriving bounds on free cumulants ($\kappa_n$) to analyze the large-frequency behavior of correlation functions.

**Model / system.** The study applies to continuum Quantum Field Theory (QFT) and holographic systems (AdS/CFT), using concepts like Out-of-Time-Order Correlators (OTOCs) and free probability theory to characterize thermalization.

**Key observables.** Full ETH smooth functions ($F^{(n)}$), thermal free cumulants ($\kappa_n$), two- and three-point correlation functions, and the fourth free cumulant ($\kappa_4$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Large frequency scale ($W 	o \infty$), energy differences ($\vec{\omega}$), and the inverse temperature ($eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation assumes that the function $C(\vec{\eta})$ is finite in the interior of the analyticity domain and grows at most algebraically near the boundaries.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates imaginary-time deformations of four operator insertions on the thermal circle, showing specific boundary displacements ($\eta_{\max}^3 = eta/4$, $\eta_{\max}^2 = eta/2$, $\eta_{\max}^1 = 3eta/4$). Figure 2 shows the saturation of the full-ETH bound for the three-point function in 2D CFT.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by using thermal analyticity to derive directional bounds on free cumulants. It then demonstrates the saturation of these bounds using concrete calculations from 2D CFT and holographic models, particularly for the fourth free cumulant.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Using thermal analyticity, we derive directional large-frequency bounds on the smooth functions entering full eigenstate thermalization in continuum quantum field theory. The strongest directions reproduce the scales previously identified for lattice systems, while the complete analyticity domain reveals an asymmetric multifrequency structure. Thermal conformal correlators saturate the second- and third-order bounds, and the leading gravitational Regge contribution saturates the strongest fourth-order direction.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a security protocol for Quantum Error Correction against malicious or flawed advice from AI agents. By introducing a trusted calibration step, the authors resolve ambiguities in syndrome measurements that otherwise allow harmful updates. The framework establishes conditional guarantees, ensuring that recovery updates are only accepted if they can be mathematically certified to improve the system's state beyond known error bounds.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, information-theoretic framework for assessing trust in complex, adaptive quantum control loops, which is crucial for deploying quantum computation in noisy, real-world environments.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the security of Quantum Error Correction (QEC) protocols when the advice or syndrome history is potentially misleading or manipulated by an external AI agent.

**Main result.** Security is maintained by implementing a separate, trusted evaluator that requires certified improvement based on calibration uncertainty and drift bounds, effectively separating the 'advice' from the 'authority' of the recovery decision.

**Method.** The authors analyze the joint distribution of passive syndrome histories, using signed calibration measurements to resolve sign ambiguities inherent in the syndrome records, and deriving rigorous bounds on accumulated risk (infidelity).

**Model / system.** The primary model is the odd-distance square toric code, used to encode logical qubits. The analysis involves syndrome measurements, recovery operations, and the incorporation of calibration measurements to test the integrity of the advice.

**Key observables.** Passive syndrome-history distributions, terminal logical measurement outcomes ($q_Y(	heta)$), and the excess infidelity ($D_u(	heta)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The required improvement threshold ($\delta$), the calibration uncertainty, and the drift bound ($L_D v A$) are critical parameters for certification.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The security guarantees rely on the assumption that the drift rate is bounded and that the calibration measurements provide accurate, sign-sensitive information.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates how trusted calibration separates advice from authority by resolving sign ambiguities in passive history distributions. Figure 2 shows the quantitative chain involving syndrome history, calibration, and the decision process using uncertainty and drift bounds.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the ambiguity in passive syndrome records (sign symmetry) and then introduces the role of signed calibration to resolve this. It develops a formal risk assessment framework using drift bounds and finally applies this framework to show that only certified improvements are accepted, while also analyzing the robustness of these bounds.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Can an attacker turn influence over an artificial intelligence (AI) adviser into a harmful quantum error-correction update? We identify an ambiguity in passive syndrome records that obstructs recovery selection, then show how additional calibration measurements support certified recovery updates under uncertainty and drift. In an odd-distance square toric code with error-free preparation, syndrome measurements, and recovery operations, opposite coherent $X$ rotations produce identical passive syndrome-history distributions. Yet a fixed phase correction can help at one sign and harm at the other. A terminal logical measurement on known encoded calibration states supplies the missing sign information. A separate evaluator accepts an update only when calibration uncertainty and a justified drift bound certify improvement over the current recovery, without assuming that the adviser recommends correctly. In simulated advice attacks, calibration-confidence checks reject harmful proposals while retaining beneficial updates under honest advice. We derive sufficient limits on calibration age that require improvement through deployment. In matched simulations, a validated channel-specific bound retains more beneficial updates than the general bound after accounting for evaluation time, while preventing the tested harmful activations under the stated drift assumption. A separate surface-code experiment includes stochastic circuit faults and noise changing during acquisition. Deterministic controllers achieve at least as many beneficial updates with the same observations. Violating the drift assumption permits harmful acceptance in the toric experiment. The results identify information required for recovery selection, establish conditional guarantees against harmful updates, and quantify the recovery improvements forgone through conservative acceptance.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops an alternative, generalized framework for analyzing the Fourier uncertainty principle by focusing on the eigenvalue problem of the shifted Hamiltonian $H_{a,b}$. It employs variational methods and matrix diagonalization in the Hermite-Gauss basis to solve this problem. The work confirms the principle while providing a more broadly applicable mathematical structure for studying uncertainty relations for various integral transforms.

**Why it may be interesting.** The generalization of uncertainty principles beyond the standard Fourier transform setting, by developing a framework applicable to other canonically conjugate operators associated with different integral transforms, is highly relevant for theoretical physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the Fourier uncertainty principle using variational methods that avoid relying on the standard Heisenberg-Weyl structure of the Fourier transform.

**Main result.** The analysis establishes the eigenvalue problem for the shifted Hamiltonian $H_{a,b}$ and confirms the unitary equivalence between $H_{0,0}$ and $H_{a,b}$ via the displacement operator.

**Method.** The authors employ three distinct approaches: a variational minimization of the uncertainty product, solving the resulting eigenvalue problem via recurrence relations in the Hermite-Gauss basis, and an algebraic approach using unitary transformations.

**Model / system.** The analysis centers on the uncertainty product for functions $f$ and their Fourier transforms, governed by the shifted Hamiltonian $H_{a,b} = (X - a)^2 + (P - b)^2$. This Hamiltonian is analyzed in the context of the quantum harmonic oscillator.

**Key observables.** The uncertainty product $J[f] = \langle(X - a)^2angle \langle(P - b)^2angle$, and the eigenvalues $E$ of $H_{a,b}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The shift parameters $a$ and $b$, and the eigenvalue $E$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to functions $f$ in the subspace $\mu_2(\mathbb{R})$ where $\langle X^2 angle$ is finite, and the algebraic approach is noted as being specific to the Fourier transform.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the uncertainty principle using a variational approach, analyzes the resulting eigenvalue problem $H_{a,b}f = Ef$ using matrix representations and recurrence relations, and concludes by establishing the unitary equivalence between $H_{0,0}$ and $H_{a,b}$ using displacement operators.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this paper, we investigate the Fourier uncertainty principle through infinite banded matrices by way of a variational approach to minimizing the uncertainty product. Traditional methods for analyzing the Fourier uncertainty principle rely heavily on the Heisenberg--Weyl structure that underlie the Fourier transform and therefore do not generalize well beyond this setting. We provide three distinct approaches to developing the uncertainty principle based on the spectrum of a shifted quantum harmonic oscillator Hamiltonian, $H_{a,b}$. In the Hermite--Gauss basis, $H_{a,b}$ has an infinite, tridiagonal form which is central to the analysis. One approach exactly solves for the spectrum through analytic methods, another decomposes $H_{a,b}$ into an infinite sum of $2\times 2$ matrices, and the third approach is an algebraic approach to establishing the spectrum via a unitary equivalence with the quantum harmonic oscillator. The approaches taken herein provide alternate avenues that are more broadly applicable to other canonically conjugate operators for other integral transforms which maintain a tridiagonal form.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a rigorous criterion to diagnose the noise sensitivity of terminal outcomes in complex many-body systems. By relating the vanishing of outcome correlations to the scaling of the macroscopic response slope, the authors provide a measurable test for how much information about the final state is retained from the initial microscopic disorder. This is particularly relevant for understanding critical dynamics in disordered systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical framework connecting macroscopic response diagnostics (like the slope) to microscopic information loss (vanishing correlations) provides a powerful, measurable tool for characterizing the predictability of complex, non-equilibrium many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses whether the terminal macroscopic outcome of a many-body system can be inferred from its microscopic initial data without simulating the full trajectory, focusing on noise sensitivity.

**Main result.** A necessary and sufficient diagnostic criterion for noise sensitivity is established: the correlation between original and perturbed outcomes vanishes asymptotically if and only if the slope of the outcome probability with respect to the control field grows slower than the square root of the system volume.

**Method.** The analysis employs Gaussian influence bounds, the Gaussian Russo formula, and compares correlation measures derived from coordinate-perturbed outcomes to establish the diagnostic criterion.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes homogeneous systems with independent Gaussian disorder, specifically testing the 3D driven random-field Ising model (RFIM) and path-dependent best-response games.

**Key observables.** Normalized response slope ($Q_L$), outcome correlation ($S_L(c)$), effective number of influential coordinates ($N_{	ext{eff},L,q}$), and initial-state information content ($D_L$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size ($L$), disorder strength ($R$), and the fixed noise level ($c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the terminal event is monotone in each disorder variable and that a uniform disorder shift is equivalent to a control-field shift with a size-independent conversion factor.

**Figures summary.** Figures show raw outcome-correlation curves and normalized threshold slopes for the RFIM, and show the effective influence fraction and Hermite weight for the Stag-Hunt process, all indicating finite-size trends.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a general criterion for noise sensitivity using Gaussian tools, applies it to the RFIM, generalizes it to path-dependent dynamics (Stag-Hunt), and concludes by analyzing a Bethe-lattice benchmark to distinguish finite-size effects from true thermodynamic limits.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Can the terminal macroscopic outcome of a many-body system be inferred from its microscopic initial data without simulating the full trajectory? Rather than construct such a shortcut, we address a more fundamental question for Gaussian microscopic inputs: can any fixed Wiener-Hermite degree retain a nonvanishing fraction of the variance of the terminal outcome as the system grows? We consider homogeneous systems with independent Gaussian disorder in which all microscopic coordinates are symmetry-equivalent, the terminal event is monotone in each disorder variable, and a uniform disorder shift is exactly equivalent to a control-field shift with a size-independent conversion factor. Using forward and inverse Gaussian influence bounds together with a Gaussian Russo formula, we derive a directly measurable criterion that is both necessary and sufficient for noise sensitivity. Specifically, the correlation between the original and coordinate-perturbed terminal outcomes vanishes asymptotically for every fixed nonzero level of coordinatewise noise if and only if the slope of the outcome probability with respect to the control field at the balanced threshold grows more slowly than the square root of the system volume. Event-driven simulations of the three-dimensional driven random-field Ising model up to linear size 192 find that both the normalized response and the correlations between perturbed samples decrease overall, consistent with the noise-sensitive regime at finite size. For spatial Stag-Hunt dynamics with prescribed seeds, the criterion generalizes through an effective number of influential coordinates. Separately, simulations of a path-dependent best-response game show, over the sizes studied, that the terminal equilibrium can depend on the update schedule while still carrying substantial finite-order predictive information.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes how chiral active particles drive the motion and shape changes of enclosed deformable vesicles. Using simulations and analytical theory, the authors show that chirality dictates the rotational dynamics, predicting an optimal chirality that maximizes rotation. This provides a general framework for understanding active stress transmission in confined, non-equilibrium systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concepts of active stresses, non-equilibrium dynamics, and collective alignment are central to modern statistical mechanics, providing a concrete, solvable model for active matter physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Investigating the transport and shape dynamics of deformable vesicles driven by chiral active Brownian particles (CABPs).

**Main result.** Vesicle rotation is non-monotonic with chirality, and the study establishes a general mechanism by which chirality and collective alignment regulate active stress transmission to deformable boundaries.

**Method.** The study combines numerical simulations (Langevin dynamics) with the development of an analytical theory relating vesicle rotation to the effective torque generated by the active particles.

**Model / system.** The system consists of a 2D, fixed-area vesicle filled with CABPs interacting via excluded volume and polar alignment. The vesicle is modeled as a bead-spring polymer ring, and particle dynamics are governed by Langevin equations.

**Key observables.** Mean-square displacement (MSD), asphericity ($\Delta$), and vesicle rotational velocity ($\Omega$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Péclet number ($Pe$), dimensionless alignment strength ($g$), and chirality parameter ($\omega$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes fixed area conservation for the vesicle and focuses on 2D systems.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic models, simulation snapshots showing diverse vesicle shapes (rod-like, elliptical), and time-series profiles of observables like asphericity and MSD for different chiral regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical model and simulation setup to presenting simulation results characterizing distinct collective motion modes (Run-and-Tumble, Rotor, Spinner). It culminates in developing an analytical theory that predicts the non-monotonic dependence of rotation on chirality and deriving scaling laws.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Active matter systems can generate mechanical stresses that deform their surroundings, providing a route to transport and shape dynamics far from equilibrium. Deformable vesicles containing active particles offer a minimal setting in which such active stresses are directly coupled to boundary mechanics. Here, we use numerical simulations to investigate a two-dimensional, fixed-area vesicle filled with chiral active particles interacting through excluded volume and polar alignment. We find that the coupling between particle chirality and vesicle deformation produces distinct modes of collective motion, including run-and-tumble-like migration, rotor-like dynamics, and persistent spinning. Most notably, we show that vesicle rotation is non-monotonic in chirality: at fixed activity, an optimal chirality maximizes the rotational velocity. We develop an analytical theory that relates the vesicle rotation to the effective torque generated by the chiral active particles. The theory identifies the competition underlying the optimal chirality and predicts a scale-invariant dependence of the rotational velocity on activity and chirality. This scaling collapses simulation results obtained over different activity strengths onto a universal curve. Our results establish a general mechanism by which chirality and collective alignment regulate the transmission of active stresses to deformable boundaries, providing a framework for understanding transport and rotational dynamics in confined active systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analytically characterizes Interactive Quantum Classifiers (IQCs) by deriving a closed-form expression for their reduced quantum channel. By applying Fourier analysis, the authors show that the Hamiltonian parameters directly control the frequency components of the classifier's output. This provides a powerful theoretical framework for designing and understanding open-system-inspired quantum machine learning models.

**Why it may be interesting.** It directly connects the theoretical framework of open quantum systems (Hamiltonian evolution) to the practical structure of quantum machine learning models, providing an analytical tool (Fourier analysis) to understand model expressivity.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To provide an analytical characterization of parametrized Hamiltonians in Interactive Quantum Classifiers (IQCs) and establish Fourier analysis as a useful framework for understanding and designing open-system-inspired quantum learning models.

**Main result.** The analysis yields a closed-form expression showing how Hamiltonian parameters control the constant, sine, and cosine components of the classifier output, establishing a Fourier interpretation of the feature map.

**Method.** The authors derive the reduced quantum channel for a parametrized IQC and employ Fourier analysis to characterize the resulting prediction function.

**Model / system.** The model is the Interactive Quantum Classifier (IQC), a quantum machine learning model inspired by open quantum systems where the interaction is governed by a parametrized Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Classification performance (Accuracy, F1-score), Fourier components (constant, sine, cosine), and the reduced quantum channel.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hamiltonian parameters ($\alpha$), environmental coupling strengths ($W$), and the number of environmental modes ($N_e$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the IQC dynamics can be reduced to a form amenable to Fourier decomposition, and numerical tests compare various parameterized variants.

**Figures summary.** The notes indicate the presence of multiple tables (Tables 6-13) comparing the performance metrics (Accuracy, F1-score, etc.) of various IQC models across several real-world and synthetic datasets.

**Paper structure.** The paper moves from theoretical derivation (closed-form expression, Fourier interpretation) to proposing generalized Hamiltonian encodings, followed by extensive numerical experiments comparing model performance across multiple benchmarks.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Interactive Quantum Classifiers (IQCs) constitute a family of quantum machine learning models inspired by open quantum systems, in which the interaction between a target qubit and an environment is described by a Hamiltonian. Previous works introduced alternative Hamiltonian parameterizations and showed empirically that they can improve classification performance, but the role of these parameters in the resulting classifier remains poorly understood. In this work, we derive a closed-form expression for the reduced quantum channel generated by a parametrized IQC with a single target qubit. The analytical solution explicitly reveals how the Hamiltonian parameters control the constant, sine, and cosine components of the classifier output, establishing a Fourier interpretation of the induced feature map. This analysis motivates a generalized family of Hamiltonian encodings, including matrix-parameterized environmental Hamiltonians whose Fourier components depend on linear combinations of input features, thereby enabling non-separable Fourier structures. Numerical experiments on synthetic and real-world datasets show that the proposed models can improve classification performance on several nonlinear benchmarks. The generalized matrix encoding achieves the strongest aggregate performance in the evaluated benchmark, while a simpler four-parameter extension often attains comparable performance with substantially fewer trainable parameters. We additionally characterize the generated state ensembles using the standard fidelity-based expressibility measure, finding that global expressibility does not directly predict classification performance. Our results provide an analytical characterization of parametrized Hamiltonians in Interactive Quantum Classifiers and establish Fourier analysis as a useful framework for understanding and designing open-system-inspired quantum learning models.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an algorithmic framework to automate the design of heralded linear optical circuits for creating complex multipartite entangled states. By mapping the problem onto a graph-search problem within the Linear Quantum Graph picture, the authors systematically discover efficient circuit blueprints. This methodology significantly advances the feasibility of engineering advanced quantum resources for quantum information tasks.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful computational tool for quantum optics, moving the design of complex entangled states from ad-hoc engineering to a systematic, automated discovery process, which is highly relevant for building quantum processors.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the algorithmic challenge of designing linear optical circuits required to generate specific, complex multipartite entangled quantum states.

**Main result.** They establish a systematic, algorithmic framework using the Linear Quantum Graph (LQG) picture to automatically discover and design efficient circuits for various resource states, including hypergraph magic states and quantum error correcting codes.

**Method.** The core method reformulates circuit design as a three-stage graph-search problem: enumerating Effective Perfect Matching (EPM) graphs, searching this repository for graphs matching the target state's spectral signature, and finally translating the successful graph into a physical circuit.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is linear optical circuits utilizing beam splitters and ancillary measurements (heralded generation). The theoretical model is the Linear Quantum Graph (LQG) picture, which structures the search space using EPM graphs defined by system, ancilla, and photon-subtraction nodes.

**Key observables.** Success probability ($P_{succ}$), the generated quantum state (e.g., GHZ, W, hypergraph magic states), and the structure of the EPM graph.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qubits ($N$), the number of ancilla nodes ($M$), and the specific parameters defining the target state (e.g., $\alpha, eta$ for error-correcting codes).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial framework assumes unweighted graphs (equal amplitudes) and relies on spectral signatures as a necessary, though not sufficient, condition for local unitary (LU) equivalence.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the three-stage algorithmic strategy, show specific circuit realizations for states like the N=3 hypergraph magic state and the N=4 cluster state, and compare different circuit designs for the same target state.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structured algorithmic approach: first, enumerating candidate circuit structures via EPM graphs; second, searching this repository by comparing spectral signatures to the target state; and third, translating the successful graph structure into a physical, optimized linear optical circuit.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Heralded multipartite entanglement is a key resource for various quantum information tasks. However, designing linear optical circuits that generate specific target states is generally challenging due to the complexity of the required optical structures. Here we formulate the design of heralded photonic circuits as an algorithmic graph-search problem. Our framework enables the automated construction and optimization of heralded photonic circuits by substantially reducing the search space using the linear quantum graph (LQG) picture. Our strategy reconstructs circuit structures as graphs in the picture and identifies suitable graphs automatically. As a result, we design efficient schemes for a broad range of useful multipartite resource states, including hypergraph magic states, quantum error correcting codes, general three-qubit states and length-1 caterpillar graph states. Our work establishes an algorithmic framework for the systematic discovery of heralded resource states, laying the foundation for the automated design of increasingly complex multipartite entangled resources.

</details>

**Summary.** This work critically examines the methods used to extract the anomalous Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{AHE}$) from magnetotransport data in quantum materials. By comparing results from $	ext{SrRuO}_3$ and $	ext{GdAlSi}$, the authors demonstrate that the extraction is highly unreliable if the ordinary Hall background subtraction or the longitudinal transport interpretation is ambiguous. The paper serves as a practical caution regarding the quantitative interpretation of excess Hall effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter transport, the rigorous mathematical and physical caution regarding model dependence and ambiguity in extracting fundamental quantities (like Berry curvature related terms) is relevant to any field relying on complex measurements or theoretical modeling.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper assesses the reliability and ambiguity in extracting the anomalous Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{AHE}$) from magnetotransport measurements, particularly when the ordinary Hall background subtraction is non-trivial.

**Main result.** The extraction of $\sigma_{AHE}$ is highly sensitive to the analysis procedure, especially in materials like GdAlSi where the transverse response lacks clear saturation. Different interpretations of the ordinary Hall background can yield vastly different estimates for $\sigma_{AHE}$.

**Method.** The authors compare multiple theoretical and experimental analysis methods, including low-field vs. high-field fitting, and the conversion between resistivity and conductivity tensors, using the Drude model framework.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes magnetotransport measurements in quantum materials, comparing the well-behaved $	ext{SrRuO}_3$ with the more ambiguous $	ext{GdAlSi}$. The analysis relies on extending the single-channel Drude model to account for anomalous and ordinary Hall contributions.

**Key observables.** Anomalous Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{AHE}$), Ordinary Hall conductivity ($\sigma_O$), longitudinal resistance ($ho_L$), and transverse resistance ($ho_T$) as functions of magnetic field ($B$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field ($B$), carrier density ($n$), and carrier mobility ($\mu$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis critically tests assumptions such as the equivalence of OHE subtraction in resistivity vs. conductivity, and the validity of extrapolating low-field linear fits to high fields.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare $\sigma_{AHE}$ extracted using various low-field analysis methods for GdAlSi, and show finite-element simulations of magnetotransport using the single-channel Drude model.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework using the Drude model for magnetotransport. It then uses $	ext{SrRuO}_3$ as a benchmark to validate extraction techniques. Finally, it applies these techniques to $	ext{GdAlSi}$, systematically comparing multiple low-field and high-field analysis procedures to expose the sources of ambiguity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We examine the different methods of extracting the anomalous Hall conductivity using SrRuO$_3$ and GdAlSi. For SrRuO$_3$, where the ordinary Hall background is well defined and the magnetoconductance is small, subtracting the ordinary Hall contribution either before or after conversion from resistivity to conductivity yields nearly identical anomalous Hall conductivities. In GdAlSi, in contrast, the transverse response does not exhibit clear saturation, and both low- and high-field regions can appear approximately linear. We show that different interpretations of these linear regions as the ordinary Hall background, together with the treatment of the measured longitudinal resistance in the resistivity-to-conductivity conversion, can produce completely different estimates of the anomalous Hall conductivity. We also examine whether the nonlinear Hall response can instead be described within an ordinary Hall framework without invoking an anomalous contribution. Our analysis provides practical cautions for extracting excess Hall contributions in quantum materials.

</details>

### [Carrollian axion electrodynamics](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18791v1)

**Authors:** Hemant Rathi, Ashish Shukla  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.18791v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.18791_figures/2609.18791_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: A spherically symmetric interface between regions with θ = 0 and θ = θ0, with a magnetic monopole of charge qm placed at the origin. The monopole induces a uniform distribution of electric charge on the interface, making the observer in the region θ = θ0 infer it to also carry an electric charge.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops the effective field theory describing axion electrodynamics in the low-velocity (Carrollian) limit. It derives two distinct theories, highlighting that the magnetic sector supports a novel monopole-to-dyon conversion effect. The analysis rigorously proves the vanishing of the linear magnetoelectric effect while establishing the complex structure of the resulting phase space.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of effective field theories in extreme limits (like $c 	o 0$) and the resulting topological phenomena (like the Witten effect) provides a framework for understanding low-energy effective theories in condensed matter systems, particularly those involving emergent gauge fields or topological order.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper constructs and analyzes theories of Carrollian electromagnetism coupled to an axion field by performing a low-energy limit expansion ($c 	o 0$) of full axion electrodynamics.

**Main result.** The resulting magnetic theory exhibits a Carrollian analogue of the Witten effect, and the linear magnetoelectric effect is shown to vanish entirely through rigorous constraint analysis.

**Method.** The primary method involves performing a systematic $c 	o 0$ expansion of the Lagrangian density and subsequently employing Dirac-Bergmann analysis to determine the physical phase space structure.

**Model / system.** The system is axion electrodynamics, which is reduced to two types of Carrollian theories (electric and magnetic) in the low-velocity limit. The analysis extends to include couplings to fermions and non-Abelian generalizations.

**Key observables.** Carrollian analogue of the Witten effect (monopole acquiring electric charge), vanishing linear magnetoelectric effect, and non-vanishing Dirac brackets among canonical variables.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The speed of light ($c$), the axion mass ($m$), and the coupling constant ($\alpha$) are central parameters defining the limits and couplings.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies fundamentally on the $c 	o 0$ expansion methodology, and the initial Lagrangian is assumed to be the standard axion electrodynamics form.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from deriving the general $c 	o 0$ expansion for the Lagrangian, separating it into electric and magnetic theories, establishing key physical consequences like the Witten effect, and concluding with detailed analyses of the phase space structure using Dirac brackets.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct theories of Carrollian electromagnetism coupled to an axion field. Obtained by performing a $c \to 0$ expansion of axion electrodynamics, these are of two types: electric and magnetic. We derive the Lagrangians and work out the general classical solutions for both. While the electric theory has the axion effectively decoupled from the electromagnetic fields, the magnetic theory is endowed with a nontrivial axion coupling that leads to interesting phenomenology. In particular, a Carrollian analogue of the Witten effect, wherein a magnetic monopole in the presence of a constant non-vanishing axion acquires an electric charge and becomes a dyon, is present in the magnetic theory. At the same time, the linear magnetoelectric effect vanishes entirely, a conclusion we establish by performing a thorough Dirac-Bergmann analysis of the constraint structure of the magnetic theory and a proper identification of its physical phase space.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel, highly efficient computational method for evaluating complex graph lattice sums that arise in the perturbative analysis of quantum lattice models with long-range interactions. By leveraging graph theory and advanced mathematical tools like generalized zeta functions, the authors drastically reduce computation time from infeasible to minutes. This breakthrough allows for precise theoretical study of exotic quantum materials and their spectral properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to calculate high-order series expansions for long-range interacting systems with unprecedented speed is a major theoretical breakthrough, providing access to regimes previously limited by computational complexity.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is taming the exponential growth of the Hilbert space dimension when simulating gapped quantum lattice models, particularly those featuring long-range (nonlocal) interactions.

**Main result.** The authors developed a novel method to compute required graph lattice sums, reducing computation time from thousands of core-hours to minutes, and achieving full agreement with Monte Carlo simulations for the transverse-field Ising model.

**Method.** The method involves factorizing high-dimensional lattice sums over graph blocks and evaluating them using specialized techniques based on the block's treewidth, including analytic forms via generalized zeta functions and tensor-network elimination.

**Model / system.** The work applies to general long-range interacting quantum lattice models, exemplified by the transverse-field Ising model on various lattices (1D, 2D, 3D). The theory relies on high-order linked-cluster expansions.

**Key observables.** Low-lying spectrum, dispersion relations $\omega(\mathbf{k}, \lambda)$, and the dependence of these on the perturbation parameter $\lambda$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The interaction range (power-law decay), the lattice dimension ($d$), and the treewidth ($\mathrm{tw}$) of the underlying graph structure.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that physically relevant correlation functions retain translational invariance, which is crucial for defining the graph zeta functions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the computed dispersion relation $\omega(\mathbf{k}, \lambda)$ for the 3D LRTFIM, showing high-symmetry points and cuts along high-symmetry paths, confirming the method's ability to resolve the full momentum grid.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of lattice sums, develops the mathematical framework using graph zeta functions, details the computational strategy based on treewidth factorization, and validates the method by reproducing Monte Carlo results for various lattice dimensions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Taming the exponential increase of the Hilbert space dimension with system size in the simulation of gapped quantum lattice models is of the highest relevance for understanding and designing exotic quantum materials, where nonlocal interactions are of particular interest. High-order linked-cluster expansions provide access the solution of the eigenvalue problem for the infinite system, yet rely on the computation of high-dimensional oscillatory lattice sums with a graph structure, only approachable with Monte Carlo methods so far. This work resolves this issue, rendering all required graph lattice sums, referred to as graph zeta functions for kernels involving power-laws, computable. The resulting method reduces the evaluation time for state-of-the art series expansions from tenthousands of core-hours to minutes. After factorizing the lattice sum over blocks, each block is evaluated by the cheapest available strategy depending on its treewidth $\mathrm{tw}$. Basic blocks admit analytic forms in terms of generalized zeta functions. Series-parallel blocks with $\mathrm{tw}\le 2$ can be computed at linear cost in the number of graph nodes and in the size of the momentum grid using a semi-analytical algebra based on Epstein zeta functions and rapidly decaying Fourier series. Finally, for $\mathrm{tw}>2$, the method is combined with tensor-network bucket elimination yielding polynomial scaling of numerical work and memory in momentum grid size with exponents only growing with $\mathrm{tw}$ rather than with the number of vertices. Through use of FFT, the full momentum grid is recovered at the cost of a single momentum evaluation. We provide a detailed analysis of the precision and runtime of our method against analytic and numerical benchmarks. We further reproduce published Monte Carlo data for the transverse-field Ising model on different 1D, 2D, and 3D lattices, obtaining full agreement.

</details>
