**Summary.** This paper provides an exact, analytical calculation of the logical error rates for cultivating magic states using $d=3$ and $d=5$ quantum error correction circuits. By using advanced techniques like Pauli propagation and CRT reconstruction, the authors derive precise power series expansions for the error rates, confirming previous results and establishing the true fault distances of the circuits.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous, non-stochastic analytical results for error rates in fault-tolerant quantum computation, which is foundational knowledge for designing practical quantum hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper computes the exact acceptance and logical error rates (LER) for magic state cultivation circuits at distances $d=3$ and $d=5$. This is crucial for assessing the fault-tolerance of quantum computation.

**Main result.** The authors provide explicit series expansions for the LER, analytically recovering numerical results from previous sampling-based studies. They also determine the actual fault distances for these circuits ($d_{	ext{fault}}=2$ for $d=3$ and $d_{	ext{fault}}=3$ for $d=5$).

**Method.** The calculation employs Pauli propagation and binary tensor contraction to exactly compute probabilities, avoiding sampling errors. Results are presented as power series expansions in $x = p/(1-p)$ and derived using techniques involving the Chinese Remainder Theorem (CRT) for exact integer reconstruction.

**Model / system.** The system models quantum error correction circuits used to cultivate logical magic states, specifically focusing on the double-checking stages for code distances $d=3$ and $d=5$. The process involves Clifford gates and non-Clifford T gates under depolarizing noise.

**Key observables.** Logical Error Rate ($	ext{PL}(p)$), Acceptance Probability ($A(p)$), and the response pair ($F_A, F_B$) to incoming errors.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit-level noise strength ($p$), and the code distances ($d=3, d=5$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculation excludes the escape stage, and the noise model assumes uniform depolarizing noise after every operation with measurement and reset flips.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the circuit structures for $d=3$ and $d=5$ cultivation, showing error propagation. Tables provide the exact rational evaluations and the coefficients for the power series expansions of the LER.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the methodology using Pauli propagation and tensor contraction, derives the LER as a ratio of probabilities, and presents the final results as exact series expansions, validating them against prior numerical work.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We compute exactly the acceptance and logical error rates for the distance $d=3$ and $d=5$ magic state cultivation circuits from Clifft [arXiv:2604.27058] and SOFT [arXiv:2512.23037] using Pauli propagation and binary tensor contraction. Actual $T$ gates are studied, not the $S$-gate proxy used for sampling. The calculation includes every fault order at several circuit-level noise strengths ($p$). We provide a series expansion form to the logical error rates, through order $(p/(1-p))^{10}$. The analytical results recover the numerical values from Clifft and SOFT at both $d=3$ and $d=5$ to within their sampling uncertainty. Then, we show that the $d=3$ and $d=5$ circuits actually have fault distances of $d_{\text{fault}}=2$ and $d_{\text{fault}}=3$ respectively, explaining the similar distance degrading effects from the companion code of [Quantum 10, 2134 (2026)].

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper establishes a fundamental equivalence between two formalisms for quantum causality: Extensible Causal Separability (ECS) and Quantum Circuits with Classical Control of Causal Order (QC-CC). By proving that every ECS process can be realized as a QC-CC, the authors provide a complete operational understanding of this class of quantum processes. This result is crucial for understanding the limits of causal structure in quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous operational interpretation for abstract concepts of causality in quantum information, linking theoretical separability criteria (ECS) directly to realizable computational models (QC-CC).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the long-standing open problem of determining whether every Extensibly Causally Separable (ECS) quantum process admits a realization as a Quantum Circuit with Classical Control of Causal Order (QC-CC).

**Main result.** The authors prove the equivalence between ECS processes and QC-CC processes, establishing that QC-CC structures fully characterize the class of ECS processes.

**Method.** The equivalence is established using a generalized teleportation construction and induction on the number of parties, relating abstract process-matrix definitions to concrete circuit structures.

**Model / system.** The study concerns quantum processes described in the process-matrix framework, involving multiple local laboratories, global past, and global future systems. The core mathematical objects are the process matrices $W$ and their decomposition properties.

**Key observables.** The process matrix $W$, and its decomposition into components compatible with specific causal orderings.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of local laboratories ($N$), and the dimensionality of the input/output spaces ($P, F, A_k$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often considers deterministic processes, though extensions to probabilistic cases are also covered, relying on the existence of specific isometric encodings.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by defining the abstract ECS concept, then defining the operational QC-CC model, and finally proving the equivalence through explicit constructions and inductive steps, covering both deterministic and probabilistic generalizations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Indefinite causal order extends the conventional circuit paradigm by allowing local quantum operations to be connected without a predetermined global order. Within the process-matrix framework, it is characterized by causal nonseparability, whereas extensible causal separability (ECS) requires that, under arbitrary input-ancilla extensions, a process admit a recursive decomposition into components, each compatible with a particular operation acting first. Quantum circuits with classical control of causal order (QC-CC), in which previous outcomes dynamically determine which operation acts next, are known to generate ECS processes, but whether every ECS process admits such a realization has remained a longstanding open problem. We settle it through a generalized teleportation construction that realizes every multipartite ECS process as a QC-CC. We further establish consistency between ECS definitions for trivial and nontrivial global past and future systems. These results provide ECS with an exact operational interpretation and identify QC-CC as the complete circuit structure underlying this class of processes.

</details>

**Summary.** This work explores the role of multi-partite entanglement in detecting the growth of an Einstein-Rosen bridge, refining the ER=EPR conjecture. By using genuine multi-entropy, the authors show that entanglement can continue to grow in black hole analogues even when standard entanglement entropies saturate. This suggests that multi-partite structure is necessary to fully characterize the quantum correlations underlying spacetime connectivity.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on multi-partite entanglement provides a powerful diagnostic tool to probe non-local quantum correlations that are essential for understanding spacetime geometry (ER bridges) in quantum gravity contexts.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates whether quantum entanglement can continue to grow in a system, specifically an Einstein-Rosen bridge, even after the standard entanglement entropies of its subsystems have saturated.

**Main result.** Genuine multi-entropy can grow linearly with time in the holographic description of a two-sided BTZ black hole, extending the regime where entanglement detects ER bridge growth beyond what bipartite entanglement measures can capture.

**Method.** The authors employ genuine multi-entropy ($	ext{GM}(3)$) and compare its time evolution against standard entanglement entropy calculations derived from the Ryu-Takayanagi (HRT) prescription in AdS/CFT.

**Model / system.** The primary systems studied include the two-sided BTZ black hole (dual to a TFD state), the spatially partitioned Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) model, and general spin-chain models.

**Key observables.** Genuine multi-entropy ($	ext{GM}(3)$), entanglement entropy ($S$), and mutual information ($I$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time ($t$), black hole parameters ($r_+, L$), and the number of subsystems (tripartition).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the high-temperature limit ($eta 	o 0$) for the holographic calculations and compares results across different Rényi indices ($n$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dynamical phases of multi-entropy growth in the holographic setup, comparing the multi-entropy network evolution against the HRT surface evolution, and showing time evolution comparisons between $	ext{GM}(3)$ and mutual information in various models.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the problem using multi-partite entanglement measures, applying these measures to holographic models (BTZ black hole) to refine the ER=EPR relation, and then validating these findings by comparing the dynamics across different quantum many-body systems like SYK and spin chains.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Can quantum entanglement continue to grow even after the entanglement entropies of subsystems have saturated? We address this question using genuine multi-entropy, which probes multi-partite entanglement structure beyond bipartite entanglement. In a two-sided BTZ black hole, this quantity grows linearly with time after every subsystem entropy of a fixed tripartition has saturated, extending the interval in which entanglement detects Einstein-Rosen bridge growth. The result refines the ER=EPR relation by identifying multi-partite structure invisible to ordinary entanglement entropies. Some spin-chain models and the spatially partitioned Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) model exhibit distinct transients, while the late-time plateau values in holography, the SYK model, and Haar-random states share a universal behaviour.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes a geometric theory for Hamiltonian engineering in quantum systems controlled by external pulses. It characterizes the set of achievable Hamiltonians using the convex hull of the adjoint orbit, showing how this set extends beyond what simple group averaging predicts. The framework provides necessary and sufficient conditions for tasks like dynamical decoupling in complex quantum architectures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools to determine the limits of quantum control, which is fundamental for designing quantum gates and controlling open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a geometric framework to characterize the set of effective Hamiltonians achievable in finite-dimensional quantum systems using ideal control pulses, going beyond simple group averaging.

**Main result.** The set of achievable Hamiltonians is characterized by the convex hull of the adjoint orbit of the drift Hamiltonian plus the pulse group element, $	ilde{H} = 	ext{conv}(	ext{Ad}_G H_0 + g)$.

**Method.** It employs geometric control theory, utilizing the isotypic decomposition of the adjoint representation and analyzing the geometry of the resulting orbitope.

**Model / system.** The system involves finite-dimensional quantum evolution governed by a bilinear Schrödinger equation, $i \dot{U} = (H_0 + \sum u_j H_j)U$, where control pulses generate the pulse group $G$. Specific applications include qubit networks.

**Key observables.** The effective Hamiltonian $	ilde{H}$, the fixed subspace $V_0$ under the action of $G$, and the structure of the orbitope $	ext{conv}(	ext{Ad}_G H_0)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the control amplitudes $u_j$, the dimension $n$ of the system, and the parameters governing the couplings in qubit networks ($eta, \gamma, \delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the use of 'ideal control pulses' and relies on extensions of control systems theory, such as Filippov's relaxation theorem.

**Figures summary.** The notes do not provide a summary of figures, but they detail mathematical characterizations of the orbitope structure and necessary/sufficient conditions involving norms and sums of squared coefficients.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the general geometric framework using the pulse group and adjoint action, establishing the decoupling condition (Theorem 1.3), and then applying this framework to specific models like qubit networks to derive quantitative conditions for realizability.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a geometric framework for Hamiltonian engineering in finite-dimensional quantum systems using ideal control pulses. Starting from a bilinear Schrödinger equation with unbounded control amplitudes, we construct the closed pulse group and use extensions of control systems and Filippov's relaxation theorem to obtain a family of effective Hamiltonians given by the convex hull of the drift's adjoint orbit plus the Lie algebra of the pulse group. The geometry of this orbitope describes possibilities beyond group averaging. Using the isotypic decomposition of the adjoint representation, we characterize its affine hull and show that the group average lies in its interior. This yields locally accessible families of effective Hamiltonians around the invariant part of the drift. We apply the framework to recover the necessary and sufficient condition for dynamical decoupling from arbitrary interactions with a finite-dimensional bath. For connected abelian pulse groups, we describe the relevant representation decomposition through restricted roots. Finally, for qubit networks with identical pairwise couplings, we give a qualitative characterization and quantitative estimation of effective Hamiltonians that can be generated using our framework.

</details>

**Summary.** This work comprehensively investigates iodine-doped $	ext{Cr}_2	ext{Se}_3$, a quasi-2D magnetic material, by combining structural, magnetic, and transport measurements. The key finding is the identification of a magnetically coupled transport crossover near $150 	ext{ K}$, which is responsible for the giant negative magnetoresistance observed. The study establishes this material as a promising platform for tuning correlated electronic and magnetic properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong coupling between magnetism and charge transport, evidenced by giant negative magnetoresistance, is a hallmark of strongly correlated electron systems, making it relevant for understanding emergent spintronic functionalities.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the complex interplay between charge transport and magnetic ordering in iodine-incorporated $	ext{Cr}_2	ext{Se}_3$. Specifically, it focuses on understanding the origin of a high-temperature transport crossover and giant negative magnetoresistance.

**Main result.** The high-temperature anomaly is identified as a broad, magnetically coupled transport crossover driven by short-range magnetic correlations and channel redistribution, rather than a structural transition. The anion incorporation proves an effective tuning mechanism for spintronic properties.

**Method.** The research employs a combination of advanced experimental techniques, including magnetotransport measurements (MR, Hall), spectroscopy (Raman, ARPES), and theoretical first-principles calculations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is iodine-incorporated $	ext{Cr}_2	ext{Se}_3$, a non-van der Waals quasi-two-dimensional magnetic material with a metal-deficient NiAs-type structure. The investigation probes its magnetic state (AFM ordering below $T_N \approx 52 	ext{ K}$) and its electronic transport properties across various temperatures and magnetic fields.

**Key observables.** Giant negative magnetoresistance ($\approx -78\%$), resistivity minimum near $T^* \approx 150 	ext{ K}$, hole-like conduction at high T, and magnetic susceptibility ($\chi(T)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperatures $T_N \approx 52 	ext{ K}$ and $T^* \approx 150 	ext{ K}$; magnetic fields up to $15 	ext{ T}$; iodine incorporation level (8.33%).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The high-temperature anomaly is interpreted as a magnetically coupled transport crossover, supported by the lack of major structural or electronic reconstruction observed via Raman and ARPES.

**Figures summary.** Figures summarize structural characterization (XRD, TEM, XPS), magnetic behavior ($\chi(T)$, M-H curves), transport properties (MR vs. T, Hall measurements), and electronic structure calculations/ARPES spectra.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically characterizes the material's structure, magnetic properties (using $\chi$, M-H), and transport properties (MR, Hall, $ho(T)$) across varying conditions, culminating in the identification of the crossover mechanism.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report the synthesis and comprehensive investigation of iodine-incorporated $Cr_2Se_3$, a non-van der Waals quasi-two-dimensional magnetic material, using structural, magnetic, transport, spectroscopic, and first-principles methods. Magnetization and electron spin resonance measurements reveal an antiferromagnetically ordered state below $T_N \approx 52$ K. At higher temperatures, a second, broader anomaly emerges near $T^* \approx 150$ K, coinciding with a shallow minimum in the temperature-dependent resistivity that resembles a metal-to-insulator-like crossover. Hall measurements indicate predominantly hole-like conduction at high temperatures ($\geq$ 150 K), while the nonlinear Hall response below T*, together with an anomaly in the third-harmonic electrical signal, suggests the emergence of mobility-dependent multichannel and spatially inhomogeneous transport. A large, non-saturating negative magnetoresistance reaching approximately -78\% at 25 K and 12 T further demonstrates strong coupling between charge transport and the magnetic state. Temperature-dependent Raman spectroscopy reveals no symmetry-changing structural transition near T*, whereas angle resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES) measurements show no major reconstruction of the electronic structure occupied across the crossover. Taken together, these results identify the high-temperature anomaly as a broad magnetically coupled transport crossover arising from the interplay of short-range magnetic correlations, chemical disorder, and redistribution among competing conduction channels. These findings demonstrate that anion incorporation provides an effective route to tuning the coupled electronic and magnetic properties of non-layered Cr$_2$Se$_3$, establishing this system as a promising platform for investigating correlated transport and emergent spintronic functionalities in transition-metal chalcogenides.

</details>

### [Quantum Encodings, Private Messages, and Communication Complexity](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17690v1)

**Authors:** Tom Gur, Miryam Mi-Ying Huang, Er-Cheng Tang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.17690v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.17690_figures/2609.17690_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: Pauli-Port Complementarity Protocol. The protocol is non-interactive. Teleportation arrows denote local teleportation measurements using pre-shared entanglement, not communication between Alice and Bob.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the resource requirements for quantum data encoding and private quantum communication protocols. It establishes a crucial link between quantum encoding size and communication complexity, showing that pre-shared entanglement plays a central role. The findings are particularly insightful for understanding the limitations and efficiencies of quantum information processing for structured versus general quantum channels.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on complexity theory, the results provide fundamental bounds on how much quantum information needs to be shared or encoded to simulate quantum processes, which underpins quantum network security and computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper studies the complexity of quantum decomposable randomized encodings (QDRE) and establishes a deep connection between this model and the communication complexity of quantum private simultaneous message protocols (QPSM).

**Main result.** The required quantum communication complexity and quantum encoding size are critically dependent on the amount of pre-shared entanglement, with structured channels (Clifford-induced) allowing for significantly reduced communication complexity.

**Method.** The authors establish bi-directional connections between the QDRE model and QPSM, utilizing techniques involving teleportation, Pauli operators, and complexity bounding arguments.

**Model / system.** The core models are QDREs, which encode quantum circuits, and QPSM protocols, which secure the computation of an arbitrary quantum channel $\Phi$ among multiple parties.

**Key observables.** Quantum Encoding Size, Quantum Communication Complexity, Pre-shared Entanglement.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relationship between the required resources (entanglement vs. communication) for general vs. structured quantum channels.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis distinguishes between general quantum channels and a structured class called Clifford-induced channels, which permits complexity reduction.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the Pauli-Port Complementarity Protocol, detailing the steps involving ordinary teleportation and port-based teleportation to achieve privacy.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the models (QDRE and QPSM), then presents general upper and lower bounds for arbitrary channels, followed by specialized results for Clifford-induced channels, and finally derives corollaries for the quantum encoding size.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the complexity of quantum decomposable randomized encodings (QDRE). We establish a bi-directional connection between the QDRE model and the communication complexity model of quantum private simultaneous message protocols (QPSM), where classical communication is free, and quantum communication is the primary complexity measure. We show the following upper and lower bounds. (1) For every quantum channel mapping $n$ to $m$ qubits, we give a QPSM protocol with quantum communication $m$, using exponential pre-shared entanglement. In particular, constant-output channels have $O(1)$ quantum communication, and classical-output channels require no quantum communication. (2) With $O(n)$ pre-shared entanglement, every one-qubit-output channel has an $O(n)$-quantum-communication QPSM protocol. Conversely, there exists a channel and a constant $c>0$ for which any protocol with at most $cn$ pre-shared entanglement requires $Ω(n)$ quantum communication. (3) In contrast, we identify a structured class of quantum channels, Clifford-induced channels, for which there exist QPSM protocols with $O(n)$ pre-shared entangled qubits and only $O(1)$ quantum communication complexity. Through our connection, we obtain analogous upper and lower bounds on the quantum encoding size of QDRE. Our results show that the amount of pre-shared entanglement plays a central role in quantum encoding size and quantum communication complexity.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes Rényi and Tsallis information entropies for a quantum harmonic oscillator model featuring a position-dependent mass. By calculating these generalized entropies in both position and momentum space, the authors confirm that these quantities correctly reduce to the standard Shannon entropy in the appropriate limit. The work extends the understanding of quantum uncertainty relations to systems with non-standard mass profiles.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of generalized entropies (Rényi/Tsallis) applied to non-standard Hamiltonians (PDM) provides a generalized framework for understanding quantum uncertainty beyond the standard Heisenberg relation, which is relevant for complex quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the behavior of Rényi and Tsallis information entropies for a quantum system characterized by a position-dependent mass.

**Main result.** The calculated information entropies approach the standard Shannon entropy limit when the entropic parameter alpha approaches 1, and the results validate known behaviors in the asymptotic limit.

**Method.** The authors use analytical and numerical techniques involving Gegenbauer polynomials and modified Bessel functions to evaluate the entropic moments in both position and momentum representations.

**Model / system.** The system is a particle confined in a harmonic oscillator potential, but with a position-dependent mass $m(x) = m_0 / (1 + 	au x^2)^2$. The Hamiltonian is derived from this structure.

**Key observables.** Rényi and Tsallis information entropies ($R(\alpha), T(\alpha)$) in both position and momentum space, and related entropy-based uncertainty relations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The entropic parameter $\alpha$, the deformation parameter $	au$, and the quantum number $n$. The analysis also considers the limit $\alpha 	o 1$ and $	au 	o 0$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Due to the complexity of position entropy integrals, the authors evaluate the asymptotic limit. The momentum entropy calculation is performed without approximation theory.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention figures showing momentum probability densities and plots of Rényi/Tsallis entropies in momentum space versus the quantum number $n$. Tables summarize numerical values for uncertainty relations.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the Hamiltonian and eigenfunctions using Gegenbauer polynomials. It then calculates the information entropies in both representations, noting the difficulty in the position space and using the asymptotic limit. Finally, it evaluates the related entropic uncertainty relations numerically.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this paper, we study Renyi and Tsallis information entropies for a Hamiltonian system with position-dependent mass confined in harmonic oscillator potential. Gegenbauer polynomials are used to obtain the position eigenfunction of such a system, and the modified Bessel function of the second kind is used to determine the equivalent momentum eigenfunction. By means of probability densities of both representations, we analytically and numerically evaluate the Heisenberg-like uncertainty of this system. Because the Renyi and Tsallis information entropies in position representation are described by integral functionals of the Gegenbauer polynomials, these quantities are much more difficult to calculate. To get around this problem, we evaluate these information entropies at the system s asymptotical limit, which corresponds to the behavior of an undeformed harmonic oscillator. Nevertheless, no approximation method is used to obtain the Tsallis and Renyi information entropies in momentum representation. In both representations, we find that these information entropies approach the Shannon entropy when the entropic parameter alpha-> 1 and are closed to the results of similar models of the literature. Finally, we evaluate the related entropic uncertainty relations numerically to validate the latter obersevations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes quantum chaos in a driven impact oscillator, demonstrating that quasiperiodic driving near a grazing collision point induces robust chaotic signatures. By comparing diagnostics like OTOC and fidelity decay across different driving frequencies, the authors show that while initial scrambling is general, the long-term global scrambling is highly sensitive to the irrationality of the driving ratio.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, analytically tractable model (impact oscillator) to study the transition to quantum chaos, using advanced diagnostics like OTOC and fidelity decay, which are central to quantum information theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the emergence of quantum-chaotic dynamics in a quasiperiodically driven impact oscillator, contrasting this with previous findings of strange nonchaotic dynamics under periodic driving.

**Main result.** The research establishes that quasiperiodic driving near the grazing condition generates robust signatures of quantum chaos, with global scrambling being strongly influenced by the irrationality of the driving frequency ratio.

**Method.** The study employs both classical analysis (bifurcation diagrams, Lyapunov exponents) and quantum diagnostics (Shannon entropy, OTOC, Fidelity) to characterize the dynamics.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an impact oscillator, modeled by a time-dependent Hamiltonian incorporating a potential and a quasiperiodic driving force $F(t) = a \sin(\omega_1 t) + b \cos(\omega_2 t)$.

**Key observables.** Shannon entropy time series, 0-1 test values, finite-time Lyapunov exponents, Out-of-time-order correlator (OTOC), and Fidelity decay.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The grazing condition, the ratio of driving frequencies ($\omega_r$), and the perturbation strength ($\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The quantum dynamics are governed by the time-dependent Schrödinger equation, and the classical system assumes perfectly elastic collisions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the potential, and the results summarize diagnostics like the 0-1 test and fidelity decay for different frequency ratios.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the classical transition to chaos, then applies quantum diagnostics (Shannon entropy, OTOC, Fidelity) to show robust quantum signatures of chaos, concluding with a discussion on the role of frequency irrationality.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We demonstrate the emergence of quantum-chaotic dynamics in a quasiperiodically driven impact oscillator near the grazing condition. While previous studies of the quantum impact oscillator under periodic driving reported strange nonchaotic dynamics, we show that quasiperiodic driving produces robust signatures of chaos. The corresponding classical system undergoes a sharp grazing-induced transition from quasiperiodic motion to chaos, as established by bifurcation analysis, Lyapunov exponents, Fourier spectra, and the 0-1 test. In the quantum system, Shannon-entropy time series exhibit broadband spectra, 0-1 test values close to unity, and predominantly positive finite-time Lyapunov exponents for both rational and irrational driving-frequency ratios. Independent quantum diagnostics reinforce this result: the out-of-time-order correlator (OTOC) displays early-time exponential growth with nearly identical rates, while the golden-ratio drive leads to a substantially faster saturation of the OTOC. Fidelity exhibits an exponential decay regime that is approximately independent of perturbation strength. These results establish quasiperiodic driving near grazing as a mechanism for generating quantum-chaotic behavior and reveal that, although the initial onset of scrambling is largely insensitive to frequency-ratio rationality, the subsequent development of global scrambling is strongly influenced by the degree of irrationality of the drive.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces simplification rules for Continuous-Time Quantum Walks (CTQWs) on dynamic graphs, treating these rules as the CTQW equivalent of circuit identities. By developing these rewrite rules, the authors show how complex sequences of time-evolving Hamiltonians can be drastically shortened while preserving the implemented quantum unitary. This offers a new, compact framework for representing and compiling quantum circuits.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, graph-theoretic analogue to circuit identities, which is highly relevant for quantum information theorists developing efficient quantum compilation and transpilation tools.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop simplification rules for Continuous-Time Quantum Walks (CTQWs) on dynamic graphs to significantly shorten the sequence of time-evolving Hamiltonians required to implement a specific unitary transformation.

**Main result.** The authors successfully derived new graph rewrite rules and demonstrated that standard single-qubit gates, as well as the $n$-th root of any single-qubit gate, can be realized efficiently within the CTQW framework.

**Method.** The work involves deriving and applying graph rewrite rules (simplification techniques) to the time evolution operator, which is calculated as the time-ordered exponential of a sequence of time-dependent graph Hamiltonians.

**Model / system.** The system is Continuous-Time Quantum Walks (CTQWs) operating on dynamic graphs. The dynamics are governed by the time evolution operator $e^{-iA_{total}t}$, where $A_{total}$ is constructed from a sequence of time-evolving graph Hamiltonians derived from the graph's adjacency matrix.

**Key observables.** The implemented unitary transformation (e.g., Identity, $P(	heta)$, $R_x(\alpha)$), and the resulting simplified graph sequence.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time evolution times ($t$), graph structure (adjacency matrices $A$), and angular parameters ($	heta, \phi, \lambda, \alpha$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The list of simplification rules presented is not claimed to be exhaustive, and the framework assumes the equivalence between the circuit model and the dynamic graph model.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the realization of specific gates (like Identity and $P(	heta)$) using dynamic graph sequences, and later figures demonstrate the simplification of complex sequences (like $X$ gate evolution) into shorter, equivalent forms.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the context of CTQWs as a universal quantum computation model. It then details the construction of fundamental gates ($iI, -iI, P(	heta)$) using specific graph sequences. The core of the work introduces and proves several graph rewrite rules (Observations IV.1-IV.5) to simplify these sequences, culminating in the realization of general gates and roots.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Continuous-time quantum walks (CTQWs) on dynamic graphs realize quantum gates as sequences of time-evolving graph Hamiltonians, but naive constructions produce long sequences with redundancy. Simplification rules, which are graph rewrite rules that shorten a dynamic graph sequence while preserving the unitary it implements, are the CTQW analogue of circuit identities in the gate model. In this work, we give CTQW realizations of the standard single-qubit gates and introduce new graph rewrite rules. We demonstrate the simplification rules through worked circuit reductions and outline their use as transpilation primitives for converting between the circuit model and the dynamic graph framework.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a deep mathematical analysis of spectral properties for Absolutely PPT quantum states in bipartite systems. It derives sharp upper bounds for purity and determines the minimum entropy for specific dimensions. Crucially, it quantifies the exponential decay rate of the spectral volume, offering precise tools for characterizing quantum state sets.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides sharp, quantitative bounds on fundamental quantum information measures (purity, entropy) for a physically relevant class of states (APPT), which has implications for understanding entanglement and separability in multipartite systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the maximum purity, minimum von Neumann entropy, and relative spectral volume of Absolutely PPT (APPT) quantum states.

**Main result.** It establishes a dimension-dependent upper bound on the purity of APPT states, improves previous bounds, and determines the exact exponential decay rate of the spectral volume for qubit-qudit systems.

**Method.** The research combines optimization techniques over explicitly defined outer spectral polytopes with rigorous mathematical analysis, including interval arithmetic for entropy bounding.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on bipartite quantum states $ho$ on $C^m \otimes C^n$, specifically analyzing the properties of APPT states, which are constrained by linear matrix inequalities derived from the PPT condition.

**Key observables.** Purity ($	ext{Tr}(ho^2)$), Von Neumann Entropy ($S(ho)$), and the relative spectral volume of the state set ($	ext{rvol}(\Lambda_{	ext{APPT}})$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bipartite dimensions ($m, n$), and the asymptotic ratio of volumes, which tends to 3.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on characterizing the APPT property via the outer spectral polytope and assumes that optimization over this polytope yields the extremal values.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing purity bounds and disproving conjectures for specific dimensions ($3\otimes n$). It then tackles the minimum entropy for $2\otimes n$ systems, followed by detailed calculations of the relative spectral volume decay rate, and finally, rigorous bounding of the entropy minimum using interval arithmetic.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study maximum purity and minimum von Neumann entropy of absolutely positive partial transpose (APPT) states, together with the relative volume of their spectral sets. A consequence of Hildebrand's criterion gives an explicit outer spectral polytope, whose vertices we classify. Optimizing purity over this polytope, together with the Song--Chen result for the $2\otimes3$ system, yields, for every $mn\ge6$, an explicit upper bound on the purity of APPT states that is asymptotic to $4/(3mn)$. This improves the previous $2/(mn)$ upper bound for absolutely separable states. The new bound applies to every APPT state, a class containing all absolutely separable states, and is sharp for every $2\otimes n$ system with $n\ge3$. For every $3\otimes n$ system with $n\ge3$, however, the unique outer-polytope maximizer is not APPT, so the bound is strict and disproves the Dũng--Khôi qutrit--qudit conjecture. The polytope also gives an explicit entropy lower bound in arbitrary bipartite dimensions and, together with the Song--Chen extreme-point classification, the exact minimum entropy for every $2\otimes n$ system. Finally, exact formulas for the relative volumes of an inner polytope and the outer spectral polytope give explicit two-sided bounds on the qubit--qudit relative spectral volume $a_n$ whose ratio is less than $4$ and tends to $3$. Consequently, $a_n=Θ\!\left(\sqrt n(4/27)^n\right)$, and the relative volume of the qubit--qudit APPT spectral set (equivalently, the absolutely separable spectral set) has exact exponential decay rate $\ln(27/4)$.

</details>

### [Unbounded Holevo additivity gaps in finite dimensions](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18222v1)

**Authors:** Jinzhao Wang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.18222v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.18222_figures/2609.18222_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: Allowed words at each stage of linearization. Here r labels one of the K words, and xi, yi are the two base generators in tensor factor i. The forward construction enlarges matrix coefficients; the return arrow shows how Step 4 transfers the norm bound from H to P. The shortening stage is skipped when ℓ= 1.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proves that quantum channels can exhibit unbounded two-use Holevo additivity gaps in finite dimensions. By constructing specific channels, the authors show that the classical capacity can diverge even when the single-use Holevo information vanishes. The proof relies on sophisticated tools from free probability and operator theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, constructive proof of superadditivity gaps in finite dimensions, which challenges classical assumptions about channel capacity and has implications for quantum communication complexity.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes the existence of unbounded two-use Holevo additivity gaps in finite-dimensional quantum channels, demonstrating a significant advantage in classical communication rate when using the channel twice versus using it twice independently.

**Main result.** It proves that for a sequence of channels, the single-use Holevo information can approach zero while the two-use Holevo information diverges, implying the channel capacity can diverge.

**Method.** The proof combines advanced techniques from free probability (Haagerup inequality) with strong convergence estimates for tensor products of operators acting on finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on quantum channels $\Phi: \mathcal{M}_d(\mathbb{C}) 	o \mathcal{M}_d(\mathbb{C})$, specifically constructed using structured tensor products of complementary mixed-unitary channels derived from free-probabilistic proofs.

**Key observables.** Two-use Holevo gap ($\chi(\Phi^{\otimes 2}) - 2\chi(\Phi)$), single-use Holevo quantity ($\chi(\Phi)$), and classical capacity ($C(\Phi)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The gap scales linearly with $n$ ($\Delta\chi(T_n) = \Theta_K(n)$), and the construction requires $n$ to be sufficiently large.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on combining Collins--Youn's product-group Haagerup inequality with Bordenave--Collins's quantitative strong-convergence estimates.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds the result by first establishing the gap using free probability tools, then relating this to minimum-output-entropy gaps, and finally proving the divergence of capacity by constructing a specific sequence of channels.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We establish unbounded two-use Holevo additivity gaps in finite dimensions. For each sufficiently large fixed integer $K$ and all sufficiently large $n$, we construct channels $T_n$ with output dimension $K^n$, input dimension $\exp(Θ_K(n^2))$, and \[   χ(T_n^{\otimes2})-2χ(T_n)   \ge n\left[\frac{\log_2K}{K}-2\log_2(1+9/K)\right]-O(1/n). \] The gap is linear in output qubits, with an explicit quadratic input-qubit cost and an explicit threshold on $n$. We also obtain channels whose single-use Holevo quantity tends to zero while their two-use Holevo information, and hence classical capacity, diverges. The channels arise from structured tensor products of the complementary mixed-unitary channels used in Collins's free-probabilistic proof. We establish minimum-output-entropy gaps by combining Collins--Youn's product-group Haagerup inequality with Bordenave--Collins's quantitative strong-convergence estimates

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a comprehensive microscopic theory for Spin-Torque Ferromagnetic Resonance in NM/FM interfaces. It derives the current-induced torque and resulting dc voltage by treating the coupling between localized spins and conduction electrons. The key achievement is establishing a unified framework that links measurable magnetic resonance spectra directly to the electronic spin dynamics of the adjacent nonmagnetic metal.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the use of linear response theory, Green's functions, and the explicit calculation of dynamic susceptibilities ($\chi(\omega)$) mirrors techniques used in open quantum systems and quantum transport theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a microscopic theory to explain Spin-Torque Ferromagnetic Resonance (ST-FMR) in nonmagnetic-metal/ferromagnetic-metal (NM/FM) heterostructures.

**Main result.** The theory successfully connects the measured dc voltage in ST-FMR directly to the electronic spin responses (susceptibility and conductivity) of the adjacent nonmagnetic system.

**Method.** The approach uses Green's function techniques, integrating out conduction electron degrees of freedom to derive effective interfacial exchange couplings and calculating observables via linear response theory.

**Model / system.** The system is an NM/FM heterostructure. The model incorporates the s-d exchange Hamiltonian, treating the FM spins via spin-wave approximations and the NM response using advanced Green's function formalism, including Rashba SOC effects.

**Key observables.** dc voltage ($V_{dc}$), resonance-frequency shift ($\delta\omega_0$), damping modulation ($\delta\alpha_G$), and the Rashba–Edelstein conductivity ($\sigma_{REE}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is sensitive to the effective magnetic fields ($\mathbf{h}_Y(\omega), \mathbf{h}_Z(\omega)$) and the frequency ($\omega$) of the applied microwave field.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory relies on linear-response theory, the random averaging of couplings, and the use of the rotating-wave approximation for spin dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the ST-FMR setup and $V_{dc}$ spectrum, the role of damping-like and field-like torques, band structures showing spin splitting, and the frequency dependence of the resonance shift and damping modulation.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the general Hamiltonian, then deriving the effective interfacial coupling by integrating out FM electrons, and finally applying this framework to calculate specific observables like the dc voltage and spin susceptibility using advanced Green's function techniques.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a microscopic theory of spin-torque ferromagnetic resonance (ST-FMR) in nonmagnetic-metal/ferromagnetic-metal heterostructures. Tracing out the conduction-electron degrees of freedom in the FM, we derive an effective interfacial exchange coupling between the localized spins and the conduction electron spins in the adjacent electron system. Based on this interaction, we calculate the current-induced driving torque, resonance-frequency shift, damping modulation, and resulting dc voltage. As a concrete example, we apply the formulation to a disordered Rashba two-dimensional electron gas and demonstrate that the ST-FMR spectrum reflects the dynamical spin responses of the adjacent electron system. Our formulation applies to a broad class of heterostructures and establishes a unified microscopic framework connecting ST-FMR spectra directly to the electronic spin responses of adjacent systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically examines bipolaron formation in polar two-dimensional monolayers using a variational path-integral approach. It finds that the unique 2D screening effects drastically alter the stability landscape, showing that stable bipolarons are suppressed in the strong coupling limit. This work is crucial for predicting charge transport phenomena in next-generation 2D electronic materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed theoretical framework for understanding charge carrier interactions in modern 2D materials, which is highly relevant for designing novel quantum electronic devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the formation and stability of bipolarons (pairs of charge carriers coupled to phonons) in polar monolayers, contrasting this behavior with conventional quasi-two-dimensional models.

**Main result.** The stability region for bipolarons is strongly modified in monolayers, showing that no stable bipolarons exist in the strong coupling limit, contrary to expectations from simpler models.

**Method.** The analysis employs the Feynman path-integral variational approach to calculate the zero-temperature ground-state energy of the system.

**Model / system.** The system models two charge carriers interacting via Fröhlich electron-phonon coupling and direct Coulomb repulsion within an isolated, polar two-dimensional monolayer environment.

**Key observables.** Bipolaron binding energy ($e_{bp}$), one-polaron energy ($e_{pol}$), and the critical coupling constant required for bipolaron formation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of static to high-frequency polarizability ($\sigma_0$), the effective coupling parameter ($\alpha_{eff}$), and the screening lengths ($r_0, r_\infty$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes an isolated monolayer environment, leading to specific nonlocal dielectric screening effects, and utilizes variational approximations in the path integral formalism.

**Figures summary.** The notes detail the derivation of energy functionals and the minimization of the energy difference between one-polaron and bipolaron states using multiple variational parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the unique physics of 2D monolayers (momentum-dependent vertices, Keldysh-Rytova repulsion) to applying the variational path-integral method to calculate the ground-state energy, culminating in the determination of the stability region.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Motivated by the progress in the physics of two-dimensional materials and the recent two-dimensional generalization of the Fröhlich model, we study the formation of bipolarons in polar monolayers. Because of very special nonlocal dielectric screening in two dimensions, this setting differs qualitatively from the conventional Fröhlich model. In monolayers, (i) long wavelength LO phonons acquire a nontrivial dispersion; (ii) the Fröhlich electron-phonon vertex becomes momentum-dependent and regular at small momenta; and (iii) direct repulsion between charge carriers takes the Keldysh-Rytova form. Using the Feynman path-integral variational approach, we show that the region in the parameter space where stable bipolarons exist is strongly modified compared to the usual quasi-two-dimensional model with dispersionless phonons. Specifically, in the most favorable limit, when the ratio $σ_0$ of the static polarizability to the high-frequency polarizability tends to infinity, the lower critical coupling, sufficient for the formation of bipolarons, can be made arbitrarily small. More surprisingly, we demonstrate that no stable bipolarons can exist in the strong coupling limit: in the isolated monolayer the stability region is always confined to a finite range of coupling constants. In general, the stability region is strongly shifted toward large values of the polarizability ratio $σ_0$, well beyond the parameter regimes in representative crystals, which indicates that polar monolayers do not favor bipolarons.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the ambiguity in determining the free-energy potential and length scale from phase-field dynamics governed by the Allen-Cahn equation. It introduces the STAC method, which uses an external surface-tension measurement to resolve this ambiguity. The technique proves robust by combining weak-form analysis with structural constraints, leading to accurate physical parameter recovery.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical phase transitions, the mathematical framework of solving ill-posed inverse problems using structural constraints (like positivity or specific functional forms) is highly relevant to identifying unknown Hamiltonians or effective potentials in complex quantum many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is the ill-posed inverse problem of recovering the underlying free-energy potential ($F$) and interfacial scale ($\varepsilon$) from phase-field observations governed by the Allen-Cahn equation.

**Main result.** The proposed STAC method successfully recovers a structurally admissible and physically scaled potential by combining weak moment analysis with an independent surface-tension calibration, significantly improving accuracy over unconstrained methods.

**Method.** STAC is a two-step process: first, solving a linear inverse problem using space-time weak moments and enforcing structure via a Bernstein representation; second, algebraically fixing the scale using an independently supplied surface tension ($\sigma$).

**Model / system.** The system is described by phase-field trajectories governed by the Allen-Cahn equation, whose energy functional involves the free energy $F(u)$ and an interfacial scale $\varepsilon$. The dynamics are analyzed via weak formulations.

**Key observables.** Effective force ($G = F'/\varepsilon^2$), surface tension ($\sigma$), and the phase field $u(x,t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The surface tension ($\sigma$) acts as the crucial independent calibration datum that resolves the inherent scale ambiguity in the dynamics.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the existence of a surface tension datum ($\sigma$) and relies on enforcing structural constraints (like positivity via Bernstein polynomials) to stabilize the ill-posed inverse problem.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention comparing error metrics (e.g., mean force error reduction from 8.70% to 5.66%) and showing structural violations in unconstrained vs. constrained methods.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the scale ambiguity of the Allen-Cahn model, introduces the STAC two-step method (weak moments $	o$ Bernstein constraint $	o$ $\sigma$ calibration), proves theoretical uniqueness, and validates the approach using extensive numerical experiments on noisy data.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Phase-field trajectories governed by the Allen--Cahn equation determine the effective force $G=F'/\eps^2$, but not the free-energy potential $F$ and interfacial scale $\eps$ separately. We introduce surface-tension-calibrated Allen--Cahn identification (STAC), a two-step method that resolves this ambiguity using an independently supplied surface tension. First, space--time weak moments produce a linear inverse problem without differentiating the measured field. A Bernstein representation with positive coefficients enforces two stable pure phases and a single central energy maximum. Second, the surface tension fixes the remaining scale algebraically. We prove scale equivalence of the forward model, uniqueness of the calibrated pair within this class, and finite perturbation bounds that remain valid when the potential vanishes at the pure phases. Experiments with resolved synthetic phase fields separate the effects of the structural constraint, ridge regularization, and constitutive approximation error. Across 80 shared noisy records, unconstrained degree-five regression produces six potentials with an incorrect well structure. Positivity combined with ridge regularization eliminates these violations and reduces the mean force error from 8.70\% to 5.66\%. Lower-degree models are nevertheless more accurate in some tests, and an admissible law outside the positive Bernstein family shows how the constraint can introduce approximation bias. STAC thus recovers a structurally admissible, physically scaled potential while making explicit which information comes from the observed dynamics and which comes from the independent surface-tension datum.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically models the interaction between hole-spin qubits and surface acoustic waves (SAWs) using a detailed Hamiltonian approach. It introduces the concept of 'spin-acoustic chirality' to quantify how the direction of the SAW affects the qubit's spin dynamics. The results show that this coupling is highly tunable via external electric and magnetic fields, offering a pathway for coherent quantum control using acoustic phonons.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, physically tunable mechanism for coupling solid-state qubits to acoustic phonons, which is crucial for developing scalable quantum memories and gates using phononics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a unified description linking classical acoustic chirality to quantum anisotropy in hole-spin qubits driven by surface acoustic waves (SAWs). It aims to understand how counterpropagating SAWs differentially couple to the qubit spin.

**Main result.** The coupling strength and directional chirality are tunable by electrostatic gating and magnetic field orientation, allowing for precise control over longitudinal versus transverse qubit driving components.

**Method.** The authors combine a microscopic Luttinger-Kohn Hamiltonian with Bir-Pikus strain theory and 3D finite-element simulations to derive the acoustic g-matrix modulation.

**Model / system.** The system involves gate-defined hole spins in a quantum well coupled to Rayleigh-type surface acoustic waves. The theoretical framework uses the LKBP model to describe strain-mediated coupling to the qubit's g-matrix.

**Key observables.** Acoustic chirality ($\chi$), directional balance ($\Lambda_{TL,\xi}$), and the modulation of the qubit g-matrix components.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Acoustic wave frequency (e.g., 2 GHz), magnetic field orientation ($	heta_B$), and SAW propagation angle ($	heta_{SAW}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on projecting the full six-band LKBP Hamiltonian onto the lowest Zeeman-split doublet and uses leading-order approximations in HH-LH mixing.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the SAW path through the device structure and the coupling of two quantum dots to a confined SAW mode.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical setup and the microscopic Hamiltonian (LKBP model) to deriving the strain-induced g-matrix modulation. It then quantizes this interaction, analyzing the resulting anisotropic Rabi coupling components, and finally discusses control mechanisms via gating and magnetic fields.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Gate-defined hole spins combine strong spin-orbit coupling with exceptional strain sensitivity, making surface acoustic waves a natural route to remote, phase-coherent control. We show that counterpropagating surface acoustic waves can differ in coupling strength and in whether they predominantly drive spin rotations or modulate the qubit frequency. We call the transverse transition-strength imbalance spin-acoustic chirality and show that it is tunable through gate-controlled reshaping of the dot confinement. Combining a multiband Luttinger-Kohn Hamiltonian with the Bir-Pikus description of strain and 3D piezoelectric finite-element simulations, we obtain the acoustic g-matrix modulation governing coherent SAW driving. Quantizing the same strain-mediated interaction yields the corresponding single-phonon coupling, and a generalized anisotropic Rabi interaction with co-rotating, counter-rotating, and longitudinal components, linking classical chirality to quantum anisotropy and directional one-phonon emission. Magnetic field orientation and dot shape tune the acoustic chirality and operator selectivity. We illustrate this framework through bichromatic coherence protection and phonon-mediated heralded Bell-state initialization.

</details>

**Summary.** This work explores the theoretical conditions required for phonons to spontaneously generate frequency combs in the THz regime, a phenomenon rarely observed. By simulating van der Waals solids like WSe2, the authors determined that strong anharmonicity from self-interaction and minimal phonon scattering are crucial for clear comb signatures. This provides a pathway for engineering stable, high-frequency vibrational energy sources.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on anharmonic lattice dynamics and the engineering of coherent vibrational states connects to open quantum systems and quantum optics, particularly in understanding energy quantization in solid-state environments.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the rare spontaneous formation of phonon frequency combs in the high-frequency GHz-THz regime, moving beyond experimentally observed nonlinear driving regimes.

**Main result.** Spontaneous comb formation requires two key conditions: strong anharmonicity from intramolecular self-interaction and limited phonon scattering channels, which were demonstrated in WSe2.

**Method.** The study employs machine-learning-augmented molecular dynamics simulations to calculate spectral energy density and analyze phonon scattering phase space.

**Model / system.** The primary systems are van der Waals solids like CrGeTe3 and WSe2. The analysis models phonon interactions using anharmonic potentials and calculates scattering phase space integrals.

**Key observables.** Phonon frequency combs (series of evenly spaced spectral lines), spectral energy density (SED), and anharmonicity ratios (k3/k2, k4/k2).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Comb spacing ($\sim 0.1$ to $1.7 	ext{ cm}^{-1}$), phonon linewidth ($\Gamma$), and temperature (50 K to 200 K).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial comb features observed in CrGeTe3 are attributed to isotopic mass distribution rather than true comb formation; the analysis relies on identifying conditions that overcome finite phonon linewidths.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate crystal structures, mode vibrations, dispersion relations, and spectral broadening effects, comparing harmonic vs. anharmonic potentials.

**Paper structure.** The paper first addresses the challenge of spontaneous comb formation, analyzing CrGeTe3's isotopic effects, then establishes the necessary physical conditions (anharmonicity and low scattering) using WSe2 as a model, and concludes with detailed calculations confirming comb formation in WSe2's E2g and A2u modes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Phononic frequency combs have been demonstrated experimentally and theoretically in the kHz-MHz regime under nonlinear driving; however, their spontaneous formation in the GHz-THz regime remains rare. We investigate the spontaneous formation of frequency combs in van der Waals solid CrGeTe$_3$, where combs of spacing $\sim$2 cm$^{-1}$ were experimentally proposed to occur in a flat phonon mode but are now reported to originate from isotopic distribution. Our spectral energy density calculations using machine-learning-augmented molecular dynamics simulations show comb-like features with reduced spacings of $\sim$0.1 and 0.6 cm$^{-1}$ in the same mode at 50 and 200 K. However, the spacings of the comb-like features are comparable to the phonon linewidth, making precise identification of the frequency comb challenging. From a comprehensive analysis of different modes of CrGeTe$_3$, we identified two conditions to distinctively observe the combs beyond the experimental resolution: (i) an intramolecular or isolated mode with reasonably large third- or higher-order anharmonicity from phonon self-interaction and (ii) limited phonon scattering channels. Our analysis of other van der Waals solids highlighted that these conditions are met for the nearly flat intramolecular $E_{2g}$ and $A_{2u}$ phonon modes of WSe$_2$. The detailed calculations showed the formation of a spontaneous frequency comb with spacings of 1.7 and 0.67 cm$^{-1}$ from coherent superposition of four and five-phonon eigenstates in the $E_{2g}$ and $A_{2u}$ phonon modes, respectively. Available Raman scattering data of the $E_{2g}$ mode in the literature provide preliminary, if not conclusive, evidence of comb formation. Our findings offer a deeper understanding and open avenues for engineering long-lived phononic frequency combs for various practical applications.

</details>

**Summary.** This experimental work characterizes the thermal transport properties of thin metal films using combined thermoreflectance and electrical measurements. The authors find that the Wiedemann-Franz law, which relates thermal and electrical conductivity, breaks down significantly for highly disordered or resistive films compared to bulk predictions. The findings provide critical guidelines for accurately modeling heat transfer in microelectronic materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state thermal physics, the rigorous comparison of transport coefficients and the identification of systematic deviations from a fundamental law like WF can inform models of energy dissipation in nanoscale electronic devices, which has relevance to open quantum systems modeling.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the need for accurate thermal property determination in metal thin films, which is crucial for microelectronic thermal modeling and interpreting thermoreflectance measurements.

**Main result.** The measured thermal conductivity ($k_m$) deviates significantly from estimates based on the Wiedemann-Franz law ($k_{WF}$), particularly for highly resistive films like sputtered Ta, demonstrating the law's limitations in these regimes.

**Method.** The study combines Square-Pulsed Source (SPS) thermoreflectance measurements with independent van der Pauw electrical measurements to characterize thermal and electrical transport.

**Model / system.** The physical system involves thin metal films (Al, Ti, Ta) deposited via various methods. The analysis compares direct measurements of thermal conductivity ($\kappa_m$) against estimates derived from the Wiedemann-Franz law, which relates thermal and electrical transport.

**Key observables.** Measured thermal conductivity ($k_m$), volumetric heat capacity ($C_m$), apparent Lorenz number ($L_{app}$), and the comparison ratio $k_m / k_{WF,model}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature range (80 to 300 K), deposition method (thermal evaporation, e-beam, sputtering), and the Sommerfeld Lorenz number ($L_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumptions tested are the applicability of the bulk Wiedemann-Franz law and the model used for resistivity ($ho_{model}(T)$).

**Figures summary.** Not specified (Bibliography chunk provided, no figure descriptions).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of thermal characterization, details the combined SPS/van der Pauw measurement technique, compares the measured thermal properties against WF predictions, and concludes by providing practical guidance on the law's applicability.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Accurate thermal properties of metal thin films are essential for microelectronic thermal modeling and for interpreting thermoreflectance measurements when signals are sensitive to film thermal transport. In practice, film thermal conductivity is commonly inferred from electrical resistivity via the Wiedemann-Franz (WF) law with the Sommerfeld-Lorenz number L_0, neglecting phonon contributions and microstructure-dependent scattering. Here, we combine square-pulsed source (SPS) thermoreflectance with van der Pauw measurements to characterize thermal and electrical transport in Al, Ti, and Ta thin films prepared by thermal evaporation, e-beam evaporation, and magnetron sputtering. SPS measurements from 80 to 300 K yield the film thermal conductivity k_m and volumetric heat capacity C_m, with C_m agreeing with bulk values within +/-8%. Compared with WF-based estimates using L_0 and a modeled resistivity, \r{ho}_model (T)=\r{ho}_bulk (T)+\r{ho}_0, k_m deviates by 5-20% for Al and Ti and up to 40% for sputtered Ta at 300 K. While the smaller deviations are comparable to experimental uncertainty, the large mismatch for Ta demonstrates the limitation of applying the bulk L_0 to highly resistive metal films. Apparent Lorenz numbers L_app=k_m \r{ho}_meas/T from 150 to 300 K further reveal that deposition-induced disorder, grain-boundary scattering, and possible phase-related effects can modify the correlation between heat and charge transport in thin metal films. These results clarify the applicability of the WF law to deposited metal thin films and offer practical guidance for thermal modeling and thermoreflectance analysis.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study demonstrates that applying a transverse electric field to zigzag graphene nanoribbons opens a tunable band gap in the THz range. This field breaks symmetry, enabling polarization-selective optical transitions. The resulting phenomena, including a circular photogalvanic response, suggest zGNRs are promising platforms for compact, tunable THz emitters.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong dependence of optical selection rules on external fields and the resulting tunable THz emission mechanism make this highly relevant for quantum optics and open quantum systems research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how applying a transverse electric field to zigzag graphene nanoribbons (zGNRs) can open a tunable band gap in the terahertz (THz) range, enabling novel optical transitions.

**Main result.** The electric field breaks the ribbon's reflection symmetry, leading to a gap that scales linearly with the field strength. This allows for tunable THz emission and the generation of a circular photogalvanic response.

**Method.** The analysis uses a nearest-neighbour $\pi$-orbital tight-binding model solved via the transfer-matrix method and perturbation theory to calculate optical transition matrix elements.

**Model / system.** The system is a zigzag graphene nanoribbon (zGNR) modeled by a tight-binding Hamiltonian. The effect of the transverse electric field is introduced as a symmetry-breaking perturbation.

**Key observables.** Tunable band gap ($\epsilon_g$), polarization anisotropy ($s_1$), and helicity-resolved transition strengths.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Transverse electric field strength ($E$), excitation frequency ($\omega$), and ribbon width ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the nearest-neighbour $\pi$-orbital tight-binding model and uses approximations for large $N$ and specific limits for perturbation theory.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the opening of the gap ($\epsilon_g$ vs. $E$), the polarization anisotropy ($s_1$ vs. $k_y$), and the selective excitation of carriers using circularly polarized light.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the gap opening due to symmetry breaking, to analyzing polarization anisotropy, and finally demonstrating the helicity selectivity leading to a circular photogalvanic response for THz emission.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that a transverse electric field applied to a zigzag graphene nanoribbon opens a band gap whose energy can be tuned through the terahertz (THz) range. The field breaks the reflection symmetry of the ribbon, allowing optical transitions that are forbidden in its absence. The relative strengths of transitions polarized parallel and transverse to the ribbon depend strongly on excitation frequency and become equal at a particular frequency. At this frequency, each helicity selectively excites carriers in only one of the two field-induced edge-state valleys, with the selected valley reversed for the opposite helicity. Since this excitation occurs away from the valley minima, the photoexcited carriers have finite group velocity, giving rise to a circular photogalvanic response. Van Hove singularities enhance radiative recombination at the band edge, providing a route towards compact, tunable THz emitters.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper derives exact solitary wave solutions for a nonlinear Dirac equation featuring combined scalar-scalar and vector-vector interactions. Key findings include the independence of the energy-to-charge ratio from the coupling strength and a detailed analysis of how the wave profile transitions between single and double humps based on system parameters. The work concludes by examining the stability of these solutions in the non-relativistic limit.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of nonlinear wave equations, particularly those derived from Dirac equations, is fundamental in quantum field theory and condensed matter physics, providing insights into stable localized structures (solitons).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to find exact solutions for a two-parameter family of nonlinear Dirac equations incorporating both scalar-scalar (SS) and vector-vector (VV) interactions.

**Main result.** The ratio of energy to charge ($E/Q$) for the solitary wave solutions is found to be independent of the coupling constant $g$, and the existence of bound states is analyzed based on this ratio.

**Method.** The methodology involves seeking stationary solitary wave solutions ($\psi(x,t) = e^{-i\omega t} \psi(x)$) and analyzing the resulting conserved quantities (Energy $E$ and Charge $Q$).

**Model / system.** The model is a Nonlinear Dirac Equation (NLDE) with an interaction Lagrangian $L_I$ containing terms proportional to $(ar{\psi}\psi)^{\kappa+1}$ (SS) and $(ar{\psi}\gamma_\mu\psi ar{\psi}\gamma_\mu\psi)^{\kappa+1}$ (VV).

**Key observables.** Charge ($Q$), Energy ($E$), and the ratio $E/Q$, which determines the existence of bound states ($E/Q < m$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The nonlinearity parameter $\kappa$, the interaction parameter $p$, the frequency $\omega$, and the mass $m$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of stationary solutions of the form $\psi(x, t) = e^{-i\omega t} \psi(x)$, and later, a non-relativistic approximation is used for stability analysis.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the transition between double and single hump solitary wave profiles based on $\kappa p$, and plot the $E/Q$ ratio versus $\omega$ for various parameter choices.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure of deriving the solutions, analyzing their properties (including $E/Q$ and hump shape), and concluding with a non-relativistic reduction to a modified NLSE to discuss stability.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We obtain exact solutions of the nonlinear Dirac equation in $1+1$ dimensions of the form $ψ(x,t) = e^{-iωt} ψ(x)$ for scalar-scalar (SS) plus vector-vector (VV) interaction with interaction Lagrangian given by $$L_{I} = \frac{g^2}{κ+1}[(\barψ ψ)^{κ+1} +\frac{1}{p} (\barψ γ_μψ\barψ γ^μ ψ)^{κ+1}]$$ where $p > 0$ but arbitrary otherwise. We look for solutions with $0 < ω< m$ where $ω, m$ are frequency and mass, respectively. We find solutions for all values of $ω$ in this range. We compute the charge $Q$ and the energy $E$ for each of the solitary wave solutions and explore the region in the ($p, κ$) parameter space in which solitary wave bound states exist (i.e., for which $E/Q < m$). We show that for all the cases while both $E$ and $Q$ depend on the coupling constant $g$, their ratio $E/Q$ is independent of $g$. We further find that in case $pκ\le 1$, the charge density for all the solitary waves have only single hump while for $p κ> 1$ there is a transition from double to single hump and we determine it as a function of $ω/m$. We notice that for all $p$ there is a transition at $κ=2$ in the behavior of $E/Q$ as a function of $ω$ which we speculate is related to the onset of instability of the solutions at $κ=2$. We obtain the nonrelativistic reduction of the two-parameter family to a non-relativistic modified nonlinear Schrödinger equation (NLSE) and discuss the stability of the single hump olitary waves in the domain of validity of the modified NLSE.

</details>
