**Summary.** This paper theoretically investigates altermagnetism in nanomagnet arrays coupled by dipolar interactions, bypassing the need for spin-conserving symmetries. By modeling the system using macrospin theory and analyzing the resulting magnon bands, the authors show that characteristic d-wave spin polarization can be robustly generated. This establishes a tunable platform for anisotropic magnon transport in synthetic magnetic structures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a theoretical framework for engineering exotic magnetic functionalities (altermagnetism) in artificial lattices using only long-range dipolar interactions, which is highly relevant for designing novel quantum magnetic materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine if the defining signatures of altermagnetism persist in nanomagnet arrays coupled only by dipolar interactions, despite the intrinsic breaking of spin conservation.

**Main result.** The study demonstrates that dipolar nanomagnet arrays can exhibit characteristic d-wave spin polarization and spin-split magnon bands, establishing a mesoscopic realization of altermagnetic magnons beyond conventional spin-conserving models.

**Method.** The analysis employs macrospin theory, Holstein-Primakoff transformation, and Bogoliubov transformations to diagonalize the magnon Hamiltonian, supplemented by micromagnetic simulations for validation.

**Model / system.** The system is a dipolar-coupled ferromagnetic nanoisland array, modeled using a total magnetic energy Hamiltonian involving exchange, anisotropy, and dipolar interactions. The analysis focuses on the resulting magnon spectrum and spin expectation values.

**Key observables.** Magnon spin expectation value ($\langle m_{\alpha,z} angle$), spin-split magnon bands, and the characteristic d-wave pattern in the Brillouin zone.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dipolar coupling strength, perpendicular magnetic anisotropy ($K$), and the geometry of the nanoislands (e.g., elliptical vs. circular).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on macrospin theory approximations and specific symmetry elements (e.g., inversion, glide-mirror) to analyze degeneracies and vanishing expectation values.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the magnon dispersion splitting in both circular and elliptical island arrays, and show the resulting d-wave spin polarization pattern across the Brillouin zone.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the Hamiltonian using macrospin theory, derives the magnon spectrum, and then uses symmetry analysis (including lattice momentum sums and Bogoliubov transformations) to prove the existence and nature of the altermagnetic spin polarization.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Altermagnetism is conventionally understood in a spin-conserving framework, where symmetry-enforced momentum-dependent spin splitting emerges in collinear magnets with vanishing net magnetization. Here, we show that its defining signatures persist in nanomagnet arrays coupled exclusively by dipolar interactions, despite the intrinsic breaking of spin conservation. Using a macrospin theory of dipolar-coupled ferromagnetic nanoislands, corroborated by micromagnetic simulations, we demonstrate that arrays engineered with altermagnetic symmetries exhibit spin-split magnon bands whose eigenstates can partially remain strongly spin polarized. The resulting spin expectation value displays the characteristic $d$-wave pattern throughout the Brillouin zone, establishing a mesoscopic realization of altermagnetic magnons beyond the conventional spin-conserving paradigm. As a consequence, spin-wave propagation becomes strongly direction dependent, providing a highly tunable platform for anisotropic magnon transport and synthetic altermagnetic functionality.

</details>

The SHO lattice bonds are shown in blue, the imaginary hopping perturbations are shown in red, and ±m denote the on-site potentials. b Cross sections of the band structure at ky = 0 and ky = π, illustrat- ing the screw-rotation symmetry in energy–momentum space. c Berry-phase flow γ−(ky). d Energy spectrum of the model in a slab geometry. The chiral states localized at the two boundaries are shown in red and blue, respectively.</sub>

**Summary.** This work introduces momentum-space nonsymmorphic chiral symmetry as a novel organizing principle for topological materials. By analyzing systems on twisted manifolds like the Klein bottle, the authors show that this symmetry allows for the existence of nonzero Chern numbers, which are constrained to be even integers. This provides a new theoretical framework for engineering topological phases and predicting robust edge states.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of momentum-space nonsymmorphic chiral symmetry provides a powerful, symmetry-based tool to predict and classify topological phases in condensed matter systems, extending beyond standard crystalline symmetries.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish momentum-space nonsymmorphic chiral symmetry as a new organizing principle for discovering and engineering topological phases beyond conventional crystalline classifications.

**Main result.** The presence of a twisted Klein bottle manifold, arising from chiral glide reflection, allows for nonzero Chern numbers, unlike the untwisted case, and this symmetry constrains the total Chern number to be an even integer.

**Method.** The analysis uses topological classification via twisted K-groups and computes the Chern number using flux integrals over the Brillouin torus.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes bipartite lattices exhibiting momentum-space nonsymmorphic symmetries, specifically chiral glide reflection and chiral twofold screw rotation, which are realized through the interplay of crystalline and sublattice symmetries.

**Key observables.** Nonzero, even Chern numbers ($C_K$), and chiral edge states exhibiting a glide-reflection structure in energy-momentum space.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The symmetry operators anticommute with the Hamiltonian, and the topological invariant depends critically on the twisting $	au \in H^1(M, \mathbb{Z}_2)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The mechanism is limited to producing factor systems valued in $\{\pm 1\}$, restricting the achievable screw rotation to a twofold rotation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the momentum space reduction to a Klein bottle/dicosm and the edge gluing rules. Figure 2 shows the band structure for an SHO lattice model, detailing Berry-phase flow and edge states.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the concept of momentum-space nonsymmorphic chiral symmetry, derives the symmetry operators for specific lattices, calculates the topological invariant using twisted K-theory, and applies this framework to specific models (2D and 3D) to demonstrate nontrivial topology.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recently, momentum-space nonsymmorphic symmetries have attracted considerable attention. However, their realizations typically rely on fine-tuning hopping phases to satisfy the required projective symmetry algebras. Here, we show that such symmetry algebras arise naturally on bipartite lattices through the interplay of crystalline and sublattice symmetries. Unlike previously studied cases, these symmetry operators can anticommute with the Hamiltonian and are therefore referred to as momentum-space nonsymmorphic chiral symmetries. Although the free actions of these symmetries reduce the Brillouin torus to more elementary manifolds, the resulting manifolds are twisted in the Atiyah--Segal formalism. In particular, chiral glide reflection (screw rotation) gives rise to a twisted Klein bottle (dicosm) in two (three) dimensions, and this twisting dramatically alters the topological classification. Unlike the untwisted Klein bottle, whose nonorientability forces the Chern number to vanish, the twisted Klein bottle can host nonzero Chern numbers, corresponding to even Chern numbers on the torus. At open boundaries, the corresponding topological invariant manifests itself through chiral edge states exhibiting a glide-reflection structure in energy--momentum space. Our results establish momentum-space nonsymmorphic chiral symmetry as a new organizing principle for discovering and engineering topological phases beyond conventional crystalline classifications.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents a high-performance magnetic memory utilizing orbital-torque (OT) effects in a Ti/W bilayer structure on a large-scale 300-mm wafer. By leveraging the orbital angular momentum, the authors achieved ultra-low write energy and high TMR, offering a practical pathway for next-generation, energy-efficient memory for AI and high-performance computing.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on spintronics/materials science, the underlying physics involves spin-orbit coupling and emergent magnetic torques, which are crucial areas of study in condensed matter theory related to topological materials and quantum transport.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental limitations of existing memory technologies, specifically the trade-off between storage capacity, access latency, and power consumption in SOT-MRAM.

**Main result.** They demonstrate that utilizing orbital torque (OT) in a Ti/W bilayer structure enables MRAM devices with giant TMR (up to 182%), ultra-low write energy (< 0.1 pJ/bit), and high endurance.

**Method.** The research combines extensive experimental characterization (TMR measurement, ST-FMR, endurance testing) with first-principles theoretical calculations (calculating orbital and spin Hall conductivities).

**Model / system.** The physical system is an orbital-torque magnetic random-access memory (OT-MRAM) device built on a scalable 300-mm wafer platform, utilizing a pMTJ stack with a Ti/W bilayer as the orbital-current channel.

**Key observables.** TMR ratio (up to 182%), write energy (< 0.1 pJ/bit), endurance ($10^{12}$ cycles), and orbital-to-spin conversion efficiency ($\xi_{OT}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** 300-mm wafer platform, Ti/W bilayer thickness optimization (e.g., $t_W = 1.5$ nm), and low critical switching current densities ($J_c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that exploiting the orbital degree of freedom via the Ti/W interface can overcome the efficiency and scaling issues inherent in traditional SOT-MRAM.

**Figures summary.** Figures show wafer-scale TMR mapping, R-V loops demonstrating switching, and plots detailing the dependence of $\xi_{OT}$ and write energy on material parameters and pulse width.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure of identifying the memory bottleneck, proposing the orbital-torque mechanism using Ti/W, presenting comprehensive experimental validation across read/write/endurance metrics, and supporting these findings with first-principles calculations of transport properties.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Contemporary memory technologies are increasingly constrained by the fundamental trilemma of storage capacity, access latency, and power consumption. Among the emerging technologies, spin-orbit torque magnetic random-access memory (SOT-MRAM) shows promise to circumvent these challenges, owing to its fast switching dynamics and high endurance. However, the application of SOT-MRAM is hindered by the relatively low write and read efficiencies, resulting in a large bitcell area and an insufficient sensing margin. Meanwhile, the involvement of an ultrathin spin-source channel, typically within a few nanometers, imposes technological challenges for mass production. Here, we resolve these issues on a 300-mm wafer platform by exploiting the emerging orbital degree of freedom and the resultant orbital torque (OT) from the relatively thick Ti/W bilayer. In particular, OT memory nanodevices exhibit a giant tunnel magnetoresistance (TMR) of 182%, nanosecond-scale response, 1012 endurance, together with an enhanced switching efficiency (E_b/I_c), which consequently enables an ultra-low write energy of less than 0.1 pJ/bit. Our findings demonstrate that orbital angular momentum can be implemented for building energy-efficient MRAM devices, offering a practical pathway towards low-latency memory that is demanded for high-performance computing and AI applications.

</details>

### [Landscape geometry of Majorana zero modes in inhomogeneous superconductors](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18138v1)

**Authors:** Guo-Jian Qiao, Zhi-Lei Zhang, Kang Xu, C. P. Sun  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.18138v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.18138_figures/2609.18138_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. A zero-energy equation with spatially inhomogeneous pa- rameters µ(x), subject to boundary conditions, is recast as a spatial dynamical system. The physical boundary condition selects allowed initial directions in phase space, while a Majorana zero mode (Mzm) corresponds to a stable trajectory approaching the origin as x →∞.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2609.18138_figures/2609.18138_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 2. Landscape geometry across the topological phase transition. Left: For µ &gt; 0, the landscape is positive definite and has a bowl- like geometry around the origin, supporting Majorana zero modes. For µ &lt; 0, the landscape is indefinite, and the origin has a saddle geometry with stable and unstable directions, where Majorana zero modes are absent. The topological phase transition, characterized by the presence or absence of Majorana zero modes, is thus manifested as a qualitative change in the landscape geometry.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 3</summary>

<img src="2609.18138_figures/2609.18138_fig3.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 3. Schematic illustration of the stable subspace and boundary conditions for Majorana zero modes. The landscape inertia deter- mines the dimension of the stable subspace Es (light-blue region), while the boundary subspace B contains the physically allowed ini- tial conditions (light-yellow dashed line). Majorana zero modes cor- respond to nontrivial intersections Es ∩B.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 4</summary>

<img src="2609.18138_figures/2609.18138_fig4.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 4. Parameter regime supporting Majorana zero modes under the landscape criterion for a Gaussian chemical-potential modula- tion. The shaded region in the (V/∆, lso/σ) plane satisfies the suf- ficient condition in Eq. (14). Its boundary is therefore a sufficient bound on the strength and spatial variation of the inhomogeneity, rather than a necessary topological phase boundary. Parameters are ∆= 0.2 meV, lso = 100 nm, µ0 = 0.2 meV, and B = 0.8 meV.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel real-space landscape approach to count Majorana zero modes in inhomogeneous superconductors where standard topological methods fail. By transforming the zero-energy equation into a spatial dynamical system, the authors determine the number of modes based on the geometric intersection of the system's stable subspace with the physical boundary. This provides quantitative, design-friendly criteria for realizing topological superconductivity in engineered nanowires.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of a real-space, geometric criterion for topological features bypasses the need for global symmetry assumptions, offering a powerful, local diagnostic tool applicable to realistic, engineered superconducting devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of determining the existence and number of Majorana zero modes (MZMs) in superconducting devices where spatial inhomogeneity breaks the translational symmetry required for standard topological invariants.

**Main result.** A real-space landscape approach is developed, showing that the number of MZMs is determined by the intersection of the stable subspace of the associated dynamical system with the physical boundary subspace.

**Method.** The zero-energy boundary-value problem is recast as a first-order spatial dynamical system, and the existence of zero modes is analyzed using a quadratic landscape function whose geometry dictates the dimensions of the stable and unstable subspaces.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on Majorana zero modes in inhomogeneous superconductors, modeled using the Bogoliubov-de Gennes (BdG) Hamiltonian, particularly applied to 1D spinless p-wave superconductors and nanowire-superconductor systems.

**Key observables.** The number of Majorana zero modes ($N$), the landscape geometry (positive definite, indefinite), and the stable/unstable subspaces ($E_s, E_u$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inhomogeneity parameters like the spatial variation of the chemical potential $\mu(x)$ and the superconducting gap $\Delta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a robust stable–unstable splitting (exponential dichotomy) and that the boundary-condition subspace intersects the stable subspace transversely.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the conversion of the zero-energy equation into a dynamical system, show the landscape geometry across topological transitions, and schematically depict the intersection of the stable subspace with the boundary subspace.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the failure of Bloch-band invariants in inhomogeneous systems. It then reformulates the problem into a first-order dynamical system and introduces the landscape function. Key theorems relate the landscape inertia to the subspace dimensions, culminating in a criterion for counting zero modes based on subspace intersections.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spatial inhomogeneity breaks translational symmetry and prevents conventional Bloch-band topological invariants from directly answering a practical question: do Majorana zero modes survive in a given inhomogeneous superconducting device? In this Letter, we develop a real-space landscape approach to address this question. Rather than solving the zero-energy equation as a boundary-value problem, we treat the spatial coordinate as an evolution parameter and recast the equation as a first-order dynamical system. A Majorana zero mode is then identified with a stable trajectory that satisfies the physical boundary condition and approaches the origin at large distance. We show that the landscape geometry fixes the dimensions of the stable and unstable subspaces, while the intersection of the stable subspace with the physical boundary subspace determines the number of Majorana zero modes. In the homogeneous limit, the topological phase transition is manifested as a geometric transition of the landscape. Applied to one-dimensional spinless $p$-wave superconductors and nanowire--superconductor systems, the approach yields sufficient bounds on both the amplitude and spatial gradient of the inhomogeneity, providing quantitative criteria for the design of Majorana devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides a comprehensive spectroscopic characterization of $\mathrm{Mo}_{0.58}\mathrm{W}_{0.42}\mathrm{Se}_2$ alloys across multiple layers. By combining Raman, PL, and RC spectroscopy with theoretical modeling, the authors quantified how mechanical exfoliation tunes both the vibrational coupling and the electronic band structure. The findings establish a robust, non-destructive method for analyzing 2D van der Waals materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state characterization, the quantitative determination of interlayer coupling constants and the modeling of quantum confinement effects in 2D materials are fundamental concepts relevant to understanding engineered quantum systems and heterostructures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study systematically investigates how the optical and vibrational properties of $\mathrm{Mo}_{0.58}\mathrm{W}_{0.42}\mathrm{Se}_2$ alloys change as a function of their physical thickness.

**Main result.** The research established comprehensive spectroscopic fingerprints using Raman, PL, and RC spectroscopy, showing thickness-dependent changes in both vibrational modes (interlayer coupling) and electronic band structure (excitonic resonances).

**Method.** The authors combined multiple experimental techniques—Raman scattering (RS), photoluminescence (PL), and reflectance contrast (RC) spectroscopy—with first-principles phonon calculations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is semiconducting $\mathrm{Mo}_{0.58}\mathrm{W}_{0.42}\mathrm{Se}_2$ alloys, studied as mechanically exfoliated flakes ranging from monolayer (1L) to nine layers. The analysis utilizes models like the linear-chain model for interlayer coupling and quantum-confinement models for electronic structure.

**Key observables.** Interlayer shear mode frequency, bandgap transition (direct to indirect), excitonic resonance shifts (A, B, C, D), and the interlayer force constant ($K_s$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Layer thickness (1L to 9L), interlayer force constant ($K_s \approx 3.0 	imes 10^{19} 	ext{ N m}^{-3}$), and out-of-plane reduced effective mass ($\mu_\perp = 0.75 m_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The linear-chain model is assumed to accurately describe the thickness dependence of the interlayer shear modes, and the analysis relies on comparing experimental data with DFT-calculated phonon dispersions.

**Figures summary.** Figures show room-temperature Raman spectra across various thicknesses (1L to 9L), illustrating the thickness evolution of phonon modes. Other figures detail the structural characterization via AFM height profiles and PL intensity variations.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first characterizing the material structure (AFM/EDX) to confirm thickness and composition, followed by detailed spectroscopic analysis. Vibrational properties are analyzed using RS and phonon calculations, while electronic properties are probed using PL and RC spectroscopy to map out thickness-dependent changes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Semiconducting Mo$_x$W$_{1-x}$Se$_2$ alloys provide a versatile platform for tailoring the optical properties of two-dimensional materials through both composition and layer thickness. Here, we systematically investigate mechanically exfoliated Mo$_{0.58}$W$_{0.42}$Se$_2$ flakes ranging from monolayer (1L) to nine layers by combining Raman scattering (RS), photoluminescence (PL), reflectance contrast (RC) spectroscopy, and first-principles phonon calculations. Thirteen RS peaks are identified, including the low-frequency interlayer shear mode, whose thickness dependence is well described by a linear-chain model, yielding an interlayer force constant of $K_s=(2.996\pm0.015)\times10^{19}$ N m$^{-3}$. PL measurements reveal a crossover from the direct-bandgap 1L to indirect-bandgap multilayers. The thickness evolution of the indirect optical transition is quantitatively reproduced using a quantum-confinement model, yielding an out-of-plane reduced effective mass of $μ_\perp=0.75 m_0$. RC spectroscopy reveals four excitonic resonances. While the A and B excitons associated with the $K^\pm$ valleys remain nearly independent of layer thickness, the higher-energy C and D resonances originating from the band-nesting regions exhibit pronounced redshifts, reflecting substantial thickness-induced modifications of the electronic band structure. These results establish comprehensive spectroscopic fingerprints of flake thickness, interlayer coupling, and electronic structure in Mo$_x$W$_{1-x}$Se$_2$ alloys and provide a reliable, non-destructive framework for their optical characterization.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study uses DFT to map out the physical properties of the electride superconductor Nb5Ir3N across pressures up to 20 GPa. It confirms the material's stability and details how its mechanical, electronic, and optical characteristics change under compression. The work provides a comprehensive computational picture of this novel superconducting material.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on superconductivity, the comprehensive DFT analysis of pressure-dependent electronic and optical properties provides rich data on material response under extreme conditions, relevant for understanding quantum phenomena in condensed matter.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the multifunctional physical properties of the nitrogen-based electride superconductor Nb5Ir3N under varying external pressures.

**Main result.** The research confirms the structural, thermodynamic, mechanical, and dynamical stability of Nb5Ir3N across the entire pressure range, while detailing pressure-dependent electronic and optical responses.

**Method.** The investigation relies entirely on Density Functional Theory (DFT) based first-principles calculations, simulating properties from 0 to 20 GPa.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the ternary nitride electride superconductor, Nb5Ir3N, which is modeled using DFT calculations to explore its behavior under high pressure.

**Key observables.** Structural parameters, elastic constants, phonon spectra, electronic band structure, density of states, optical response ($\epsilon(\omega)$), and superconducting features.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pressure range (0 to 20 GPa), Spin-Orbit Coupling (SOC) inclusion, and the hexagonal crystal structure.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on DFT approximations, and the discussion of superconducting features is noted as being qualitative.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the analysis covers structural, mechanical, electronic, and optical spectra as a function of pressure.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the material's stability (structural/thermodynamic), mechanical response (ductility/anisotropy), electronic structure (band/DOS), and optical properties (reflectivity/absorption) as pressure is varied.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Discovery and study of superconducting electrides have opened new avenues in condensed matter physics, driven by their intriguing multifunctional features spanning ambient and high-pressure regimes. This work investigates the ternary nitride superconductor Nb5Ir3N under pressure ranging from 0 to 20 GPa via density functional theory based simulations. Estimated structural parameters agree well with the available data, confirming their reliability and supporting the validity of this analysis. Negative formation enthalpy, together with evaluated elastic constants and phonon spectra, confirm the structural, thermodynamic, mechanical, and dynamical stability of Nb5Ir3N over the entire pressure range. Pressure dependent elastic constants and polycrystalline elastic moduli are investigated. The compound is categorized as ductile in light of estimated mechanical indices. Elastic anisotropy factors indicate that Nb5Ir3N remains anisotropic, with the degree of anisotropy gradually decreasing as pressure increases. Electronic band structure and density of states are calculated with and without spin orbit coupling to investigate its influence on the electronic structure. Calculated optical response reveals substantial intraband contributions in the low energy region, intense ultraviolet absorption, and strong reflectivity. The spectra exhibit optical anisotropy and a broadening at higher pressures. Pressure induced superconducting features are also discussed qualitatively.

</details>
